## About this Book

This is a digital copy of a book that has been preserved on library shelves for generations before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project intended to make the world's books available online.

The book has outlived its copyright and can now be made available to the public. A public domain book is a book that was never subject to copyright or for which the copyright protection period has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary from country to country. Public domain books are our gateway to the past and represent a historical, cultural, and scientific wealth that is often difficult to discover.

Marks of use, annotations, and other marginalia contained in the original volume are also found in this file—a reminder of the long journey the book has taken from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage Guidelines

Google is proud to work in partnership with libraries to digitize public domain material and make it accessible to a broad audience. Public domain books belong to the public, and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive. To continue providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties. This includes technical restrictions on automated queries.

We ask that you adhere to the following guidelines:

+ *Use of the files for non-commercial purposes* We designed Google Book Search for end users and want you to use these files only for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ *No automated queries* Do not send automated queries of any kind to the Google system. If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition <note>OCR</note>, or other areas where access to text in large quantities is useful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain material for these purposes and may be able to help you.

+ *Retention of Google branding elements* The Google "watermark" that you find in every file is important for providing information about this project and helps users find further material via Google Book Search. Please do not remove the watermark.

+ *Stay within the law* Depending on your intended use, you must be aware of your responsibility to ensure that your use is legal. Do not assume that a book we believe to be in the public domain for users in the USA is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still subject to copyright varies from country to country. We cannot provide advice on whether a specific use of a specific book is legally permitted. Do not assume that the appearance of a book in Google Book Search means it can be used in any form and anywhere in the world. A copyright violation can have serious consequences.

## About Google Book Search

Google's goal is to organize the world's information and make it universally useful and accessible. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world's books and supports authors and publishers in reaching new audiences. You can search the entire text of the book on the internet at <insert>http://books.google.com</insert>.

# Front Cover of the Manuscript

->**[Exterior Binding]**<-

The volume is bound in a combination of aged brown leather and deep red decorative panels. The front cover features a rich, embossed floral pattern <note>This style of relief decoration, known as "blind tooling," was common in high-quality bookbinding to provide both texture and aesthetic appeal</note> consisting of intricate leaf-like scrolls and blooming flower motifs.

The spine and the corners of the boards are reinforced with dark, weathered leather, showing signs of historical use. A small library classification label is affixed to the lower section of the spine <note>original: "white library label with black markings"</note>, indicating its later provenance in a formal collection or archive.

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>Following the formal dedication to his father in Constantinople (1849), the translator, Georg Rosen, begins his preface. This section shifts from a personal tribute to a philosophical introduction, situating Jalal al-Din Rumi’s work within the broader context of medieval mysticism and comparing it to the scientific pursuits of alchemy.</meta>

# Preface to the New Edition

## along with some remarks on the worldview of Jalal al-Din Rumi, as it emerges from his works.

Mysticism is the alchemy of religion. Just as alchemy sought the formula to produce gold, the humanity of the Middle Ages simultaneously sought a simple formula—a reliable method for achieving the salvation of the soul. In both cases, the goal was to peer behind the veil of mystery that hid the longed-for objective. The alchemists produced no gold, yet their searching was not in vain. It led to the precise observation of natural forces and thus to the most significant discoveries and inventions, and finally to the founding of that science <note>The author refers here to Chemistry.</note> which is perhaps most qualified of all to offer a deep insight into the essence of things outside ourselves—yes, perhaps even into the question of the origin and nature of life itself. 

Mysticism is different: its goals were not tangible. Whether they were reached or not was decided not by objective findings, but solely by the subjective state of the seeker's soul. He who falls into religious ecstasy has attained union with God. His faith has helped him. The journey was his destination. What is subjectively experienced is truth for the subject. One owes no proof of it to anyone. Only

<column-break/>

<term>Preface</term>, <term>Mysticism</term>, <term>Alchemy</term>, <term>Jalal al-Din Rumi</term>, <term>Salvation of the Soul</term>, <term>Natural Forces</term>, <term>Ecstasy</term>, <term>Masnavi</term> <note>original: "Meßnevi"; the title of Rumi's primary poetic work, meaning "rhyming couplets."</note>

<summary>In this preface, the translator compares mysticism to alchemy, arguing that while alchemy led to the physical sciences, mysticism provided a subjective path to spiritual union and the salvation of the soul.</summary>
<keywords>Jalal al-Din Rumi, mysticism, alchemy, religious ecstasy, philosophy of religion, Masnavi, Middle Ages, Georg Rosen</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>Continues the preface from the previous page. The text moves from discussing the subjective nature of mystical ecstasy to explaining how these private experiences became structured religious paths and, ultimately, the defining characteristic of Persian literature.</meta>

if one wishes to let others participate in what they have personally experienced, then one must have a method, a path, upon which others can reach the same goal. The need of individual enlightened souls to make the perceived blessings of salvation accessible to their fellow human beings led to the emergence of sects in Christianity, the establishment of countless "directions" in Muhammadanism <note>original: "Muhammedanismus"; a historical Western term for Islam.</note>, and finally to the founding of the various dervish orders. And these, in turn, branched out and ramified further, depending on the views of the individual <term>Pirs</term> <note>original: "Pire"; spiritual elders or masters.</note> or spiritual leaders who knew how to give weight to their method (<term>tariqat</term> <note>original: "tariqät"; the spiritual path or system of a Sufi order.</note>). In Persia, however, all these directions—which are summarized under the name of Sufism—nonetheless have something in common. They adopted the form of poetry and, from the tenth century onward, increasingly filled the entire body of poetic literature; so much so that one can say without exaggeration that every poet was henceforth a mystic, and almost every poetic work appears or can be interpreted as more or less mystically colored.

In this literature, Sufism has bestowed upon the world a precious and inexhaustible treasure of first-rate artworks. On the soil of no other country has mysticism produced such beautiful and rich blossoms as on that of Persia. From this source, since the time of Goethe, German literature has drawn extensively and enriched itself with new ideas and art forms, however deficient and often misleading the translations of Persian poetic works were at first, and however imperfect our initial knowledge of Oriental philosophy might have been. Rarely has a poet possessed—as was the case with Rückert <note>Friedrich Rückert (1788–1866), a German poet and translator famous for his linguistic genius and translations of Eastern literature.</note>—a thorough, scientific-Oriental knowledge of both language and subject matter. In many cases, poetic talent has [leaped] over the depth of the world of thought...

<term>Sufism</term>, <term>Mysticism</term>, <term>Persia</term>, <term>Goethe</term>, <term>Rückert</term>, <term>Dervish Order</term>, <term>Pirs</term>, <term>tariqat</term>, <term>Muhammadanism</term>, <term>Poetry</term>

<summary>This page describes the formalization of individual mystical experiences into the structured "paths" of Sufi dervish orders and notes how this spiritual movement became the dominant force in Persian poetry. It also highlights the historical impact of Persian mysticism on German literature, particularly on figures like Goethe and Rückert.</summary>
<keywords>Sufism, Persian poetry, Dervish orders, Friedrich Rückert, Johann Wolfgang von Goethe, tariqat, mysticism, religious philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction. The previous page ended midsentence, likely referring to the "deficient and often misleading translations" of Persian literature into German. This page begins by criticizing those who focus only on the surface level of the text rather than its spiritual core, before introducing Georg Rosen’s specific contribution.</meta>

...brushed aside and, through the clever use of Oriental forms and expressions, given us only the shell without unlocking the kernel. On the other hand, even among true scholars, only a few have been granted—and few have felt the need—to reproduce the beauty of the literatures accessible to them in a generally understandable and artistic form.

In the present work, **Georg Rosen** set himself this task. He wished to make the greatest and most significant product of Persian mysticism accessible and understandable to the educated German reading public, while reproducing the poetic form as closely as possible. This is a work that is held in almost equal esteem to the Quran and, even today, seven hundred years later, more or less dominates the intellectual world of Islam from the Adriatic Sea to the Bay of Bengal, and from Turkestan to Yemen. That **Georg Rosen's Masnavi translation** <note>original: "Mesnevi-Übersezung"; the *Masnavi-i Ma'navi* is Rumi's magnum opus of spiritual couplets.</note> fulfilled this task only to a limited extent was likely due less to the work itself than to external circumstances. The presumably small edition of 1849 was soon out of print, and for a new edition, the publisher likely lacked initiative and the author lacked encouragement, especially since newer and larger tasks occupied him completely. Thus, the book became a rarity in scholarly libraries and has almost fallen into oblivion. Indeed, even some researchers specializing in the mysticism of **Jalal al-Din** <note>original: "Dschelāl ed dīn"; referring to Jalal al-Din Muhammad Rumi (1207–1273).</note> seem not to know of it. 1

---

1. In the latest (1910), and otherwise excellent work published on the **Masnavi**: *The Masnavi by Jalalu 'D Din Rumi, book II*, the author **C. E. Wilson** says: "In 1881 Sir James Redhouse translated the First Book of this poem, but with the exception of that poem and of Mr. Whinfield’s abstract, nothing of importance in any European language has been attempted up to the present to further the knowledge of a work so valuable to..." <note>The author points out that Wilson was unaware of Rosen's earlier German translation when he claimed no other important European translations existed besides Redhouse and Whinfield.</note>

<summary>The author explains the difficulty of translating Persian mystical poetry and introduces Georg Rosen’s 1849 translation of Rumi’s Masnavi, which had unfortunately fallen into obscurity despite its significance.</summary>

<keywords>Georg Rosen, Masnavi, Rumi, Persian mysticism, Sufism, Islam, translation history, C. E. Wilson, 19th-century scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface written by Friedrich Rosen, the son of the original translator Georg Rosen. The previous page ended with the observation that even scholars of Rumi seemed not to know of Georg Rosen's earlier work. This page explains the motivation behind the new edition and the decision to keep the original text largely unchanged.</meta>

...f it.

It must, however, be deeply regretted that a work of such solid scientific thoroughness and a German translation of such perfect poetic form, belonging to such a significant literary monument, should be allowed to perish.

The publishing house of **Georg Müller** <note>Georg Müller (1877–1917) was a prominent publisher in Munich known for high-quality editions of world literature.</note> in Munich and Leipzig has now set itself the noble task of making the spirit of the Orient accessible through quality translations, not only to specialized scholars but to the entirety of educated Germany. They have invited me to lend a hand in a new edition of my father's **Masnavi translation**. <note>original: "Mesnevi-Übersetzung"; referring to the *Masnavi-i Ma'navi*, the masterpiece of spiritual couplets by the Persian poet Rumi.</note> When I took on this task, I initially believed that I would have to subject the work to a thorough revision. However, upon closer examination of the literature published since then, I find that essentially no new perspectives for explaining the text have come to light. The two sources from which **Georg Rosen** <note>Georg Rosen (1820–1891) was a German diplomat and orientalist who served in the Ottoman Empire.</note> had drawn—the Eastern commentators and the *Sufism* <note>original: "Sufismus"; the mystical branch of Islam focused on the inner, spiritual dimension of the faith.</note> still living in the East—have more or less been the sources for his successors as well. The only difference is that in the **Constantinople** <note>Modern-day Istanbul.</note> of the 1840s, this latter source flowed even more directly for him. Furthermore, no historically verified information of significance regarding the poet's life has since come to light. Thus, I have ultimately left the work in its original form, save for a few minor details. I do not doubt that it will find a friendly reception as a scientifically reliable explanation and, at the same time, an artistic rendering of the thoughts of the "greatest mystic of the Orient <note>original: "Morgenlandes"; literally 'the land of the morning', a common 19th-century term for the East or Asia.</note> and simultaneously the greatest pantheistic <note>Pantheism is the philosophical view that the universe and God are identical, or that God is present in all things.</note> poet of all time." 2

---

students not only of Sufism, but also of philosophy generally, including the modern development, theosophy. 2. Ethé, *New Persian Literature*, in Geyer and Kuhn’s *Compendium of Iranian Philology*. <note>original: "Ethé, Neupersische Literatur, in Geyer und Kuhns Grundriß der iranischen Philologie"; refers to the work of Hermann Ethé, a foundational scholar of Persian studies.</note>

<summary>Friedrich Rosen explains that he is republishing his father Georg Rosen's 1849 translation of Rumi's Masnavi through the Georg Müller publishing house. He argues that the original work remains the most authoritative and poetic version available, as the scholarly understanding of Sufism has not changed significantly since his father's time in 19th-century Constantinople.</summary>
<keywords>Georg Rosen, Friedrich Rosen, Georg Müller, Masnavi, Rumi, Sufism, Constantinople, Persian Literature, Orientalism, Pantheism, Hermann Ethé</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues Friedrich Rosen’s introduction to the new edition of his father’s work. He shifts from discussing the history of the translation to the broader academic context of Sufi studies and his own personal goals for this edition.</meta>

If any new scholarly insights regarding the mysticism of **Jalal al-Din** <note>Jalal al-Din Muhammad Rumi (1207–1273), the preeminent Persian poet and founder of the Mevlevi Order of Sufis.</note> are to be offered, they could likely only arise from the perspective of a general knowledge of the philosophy and religious history of the Orient. This is a field that has been diligently cultivated since the first appearance of **Georg Rosen’s** translation of the **Masnavi** <note>original: "Mesnevi-Übersetzung"; the *Masnavi-i Ma'navi* is Rumi's most famous work, a massive collection of ethical and spiritual anecdotes.</note>, and it has borne beautiful fruit. In the realm of philosophy, **Dieterici** <note>Friedrich Dieterici (1821–1903), a German scholar who specialized in the transmission of Greek philosophy to the Islamic world.</note> has especially deepened our understanding of the Eastern world of thought. But how few philosophers are granted the opportunity to read and utilize the difficult Oriental texts! — And it is similar in the related field of the history of religions. Among the representatives of this new science, the theological naturally predominates over the Orientalist. Even though, most gratifyingly, one of the greatest modern authorities in this field is able to reproduce works drawn from the original Persian text in an artistic form, <note>referring to footnote 3</note> many great treasures still remain buried due to linguistic and technical difficulties. Meanwhile, general interest has turned to the study of mysticism to such a high degree that it can no longer dispense with a closer knowledge of the poetry and philosophy of **Jalal al-Din**.

When I now undertake to add here a short sketch of the philosophical ideas of the **Mawlana** <note>original: "Mewlānā"; an Arabic title meaning "Our Master," used as a respectful name for Rumi.</note>, I must immediately remark that this does not claim to give an exhaustive picture of his system. For that, I primarily lack the necessary leisure. Rather, it is a distillation of what I gathered in earlier years of travel in the Orient through immersion in the works of **Jalal al-Din** and other similar poets, and no less through many years of association with many who [follow] the great mystic—

***

3. Edv. Lehmann (Professor of the History of Religions at Berlin University), *Textbook on the History of Religions*, Leipzig 1912. <note>original title: "Textbuch zur Religionsgeschichte". Edvard Lehmann (1862–1930) was a Danish scholar central to the establishment of the history of religions as a discipline.</note>

<summary>Friedrich Rosen argues that understanding Rumi requires a bridge between Orientalist language skills and the history of philosophy. He introduces his own summary of Rumi's thought, based on his personal travels and studies in the East.</summary>

<keywords>Jalal al-Din Rumi, Masnavi, Georg Rosen, Friedrich Dieterici, Edvard Lehmann, Sufism, Oriental Philosophy, History of Religions</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues Friedrich Rosen's preface, where he acknowledges the personal influences and Persian social circles that informed his understanding of Sufism. He transitions from academic context to personal gratitude and an explanation of the book's frontispiece.</meta>

kindred souls. In particular, I am indebted to the dervish order of the **Safi Ali Shahi** in Tehran, its founder **Hajji Mirza Hassan**, and especially its current head, **Zahir od-Dowleh** <note>Ali Khan Qajar, known as Zahir od-Dowleh (1864–1924), was a high-ranking official and a prominent Sufi leader who founded the Anjuman-i Ukhuwwat (Society of Brotherhood).</note>—bearing the dervish title **Safi Ali Shah**—likewise to **Prince Imad od-Dowleh** and the circle gathered around him, furthermore to His Excellency **Hajji Mirza Mahmud Khan Qajar** (currently the Persian ambassador in Constantinople), and finally to His Excellency **Mirza Ali Muhammad Khan Mu'addil es-Saltaneh** from Shiraz, for the most diverse forms of encouragement and instruction. In the circles of the educated at that time in Persia, Sufism prevailed, and those in the highest positions proudly claimed the status of a dervish for themselves.

> “Sufism does not lie in a woolen coat; dress 
> as you wish, for there are also dervishes in silk.” <gloss>4</gloss>

Thanks to the manifold inspirations and insights I gained during my many years of residence in Persia among these men, the ideas of Oriental philosophy, which are often abstruse in themselves, could become a living reality for me. Thus, I may hope that the reader will gain a certain insight into the unique world of ideas of our mystic from this sketch—an insight they might not have been able to draw from mere book wisdom alone. Our frontispiece depicts a scene in which the **Murshid** <term>spiritual guide</term> of our **Mevlana** <note>Mevlana, meaning "Our Master," is the common title for Rumi.</note> throws his precious manuscripts into the water. <note>This refers to the legendary meeting between Rumi and his mentor Shams-i Tabrizi, who threw Rumi’s books into a fountain to demonstrate that true divine knowledge comes from the heart, not from written texts.</note> With this, he intended to show him that it is not books, but the following of the right path that should guide the disciple.

4. This verse by **Jalal al-Din** alludes to the fact that the Sufis derived their name from the Arabic word *suf*, meaning wool, because the woolen garment—the "hair shirt"—is the actual dress of the dervishes.

<summary>Friedrich Rosen acknowledges the Persian Sufi mentors and noblemen who helped him understand Rumi's philosophy as a living tradition rather than just an academic subject. He also explains the symbolic frontispiece depicting the rejection of "book wisdom" in favor of spiritual experience.</summary>
<keywords>Safi Ali Shahi, Tehran, Zahir od-Dowleh, Sufism, dervish, Rumi, Murshid, Shams-i Tabrizi, Persian nobility, wool garment</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues Friedrich Rosen's introduction. Having established his personal connection to Persian Sufi circles on the previous page, he now transitions into a formal summary of Jalal al-Din Rumi's philosophical foundations, specifically how Rumi's interpretation of the Quran differs from orthodox scholarship.</meta>

> “The scholar follows the black trail of the reed pen;
> The Sufi follows only the Master’s footprints.”
> <note>original: „Des Schreibrohrs schwarzer Spur folgt der Gelehrte, / Der Sufi folgt allein des Meisters Fährte.“</note>

I will attempt to lead the reader “on the Master’s footprints” toward an understanding of him, and I will, as far as possible, provide an image of the worldview that serves as the foundation of his philosophy in his own words. In this short sketch, I will dispense with literary references. For those, however, who wish to occupy themselves more deeply with the subject, I provide a list at the end of the more recent—that is, published after 1850—noteworthy works that are particularly relevant to Jalal al-Din and his philosophy.

### The Mystical Worldview of Jalal al-Din Rumi.

There are three things upon which the mystical teachings of Jalal al-Din are built: the Quran, Greek philosophy, and finally, the wisdom of India. The Quran stands in the first place and to an unrestricted degree. However, it is no longer the holy book exactly as the Arabs of the seventh century heard and received it from the mouth of the Prophet. It is the Quran as it presents itself to a much more refined, non-Arab society under foreign influences through mystical or otherwise allegorical interpretation. The material and the obvious <term>zāhir; the outward or exoteric meaning</term> is perceived as a symbol of something higher and hidden, and this hidden element <term>bātin; the inward or esoteric meaning</term> is the true sense of the divine words. It is not as if the events recounted in the Quran are not viewed or intended to be regarded as historically true: they are indeed true, but these stories, which are often insignificant in themselves, are told only so that the <term>'Arif</term> <note>The 'Arif is the "Gnostic" or "Knower"—one who possesses intuitive, spiritual knowledge.</note>, the initiate, in contrast to the <term>'Alim</term> <note>The 'Alim is the traditional "Scholar"—one who focuses on formal religious law and dogma.</note>, the scribe and dogmatist, may find a deeper...

<summary>Rosen introduces the three pillars of Rumi’s thought—the Quran, Greek philosophy, and Indian wisdom—and explains the Sufi distinction between the outward (zahir) and inward (batin) meanings of scripture.</summary>
<keywords>Jalal al-Din Rumi, Sufism, Quranic interpretation, zahir and batin, 'Arif vs 'Alim, Islamic mysticism, Friedrich Rosen</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the two-fold nature of the Quran: the "zāhir" (outward/literal) and "bātin" (inward/hidden). It explains how mystical interpretation allows the "educated" believer to find intellectual and spiritual depth in texts that might otherwise seem simplistic or contradictory.</meta>

...should extract meaning from it. If this deeper sense did not lie beneath the often coarsely material words and the frequently childlike, naive stories, then the educated person among the believers of his holy book would almost have to be ashamed. Thus, Philo of Alexandria <note>A Hellenistic Jewish philosopher (c. 20 BCE – 50 CE) who used allegorical methods to harmonize the Torah with Greek philosophy.</note> had already sought to reconcile the Old Testament with Greek science through allegorical interpretations; from him, this same tendency flows in three streams through the Kabbalah <note>original: "Kabala"</note> of Judaism, through the Scholasticism <term>Scholasticism; a method of critical thought used by medieval Christian theologians to harmonize faith with reason</term> of Christianity, and through the Mysticism of Islam. In this way, Sufism <term>Sufism; the inward, mystical dimension of Islam</term> rescues the Quran.

It is similar with the *Masnavi* <note>original: *Mesnevi*</note>. This work also faced—indeed, even during the poet's lifetime—sharp criticism. It was claimed that the work lacked a proper method, a secure path by which the disciple is led step by step, yet faster than by any previously known way, toward the salvation of the soul. Jalal al-Din <note>original: *Dschelāl ed dīn*</note> himself experienced this criticism of his life’s work and responded to it in the fourth book of the *Masnavi*, pointing to the Quran and its deeper interpretations. This passage is so characteristic of both the conception of the Quran and the *Masnavi* that I wish to reproduce it here in an unabridged translation.

> Before the story reaches its conclusion,
> The stinking river of envy pours out its confusion!
> Though no harm is done to me by this attack,
> It easily leads the pious from the proper track.
> How beautifully Sanai <gloss>5. A predecessor of Jalal al-Din.</gloss> taught us this lesson,
> When explaining the Quran’s deeper expression:
> "It does not surprise me that those who walk in the dark,
> See in the Quran only the word's literal mark.
> The blind man, too, feels from the sun's bright face
> Only the warmth, but cannot see its radiant grace."

5. A predecessor of Jalal al-Din.

It includes a poetic defense by Rumi against critics who found his work lacked a clear systematic method.</summary>

> To test this, I would know a remedy,
> Just try to write one chapter of the Quran!” —
> Indeed, should angels and spirits themselves try to write it,
> They would get stuck at the very first verse.
> The Quran’s meaning does not lie clearly on the surface,
> Within the manifest lies the hidden <note>This refers to the concept of *zahir* (the apparent, literal meaning) and *batin* (the hidden, esoteric meaning) in Islamic theology.</note>,
> In this again a third; whoever wishes
> To grasp that, his understanding will stand still,
> And then a fourth, which only the Prophet
> And God, the Incomparable, understands.
> And so it goes, one inside the other,
> That every word yields a sevenfold meaning.
> The fool sees only the exterior of the Quran,
> Just as to the Devil, Adam was only a lump of earth <note>In Quranic tradition, Iblis (Satan) refused to bow to Adam because he only saw Adam's physical form made of clay, failing to recognize the divine spirit breathed into him.</note>.
> The human body and the Quran's outward sense
> Are visible—but invisible is the spirit within.

What was it that made this interpretation of the Quran, heightened to the number seven, possible? It was "science." The science of the Middle Ages was—in the Orient even more so than in the West—Greek science. What Pythagoras, Plato, and Aristotle, Plotinus and Philo <note>A list of foundational Greek and Hellenistic philosophers whose works on mathematics, metaphysics, and mysticism deeply influenced Islamic thought.</note> had once taught, now experienced a rich second flowering in Arabic translations on foreign soil. Classical Hellenism was valued in the land of the "barbarians"; meanwhile, in Greece itself—that is, in Byzantium—it was smothered by rampant dogmatism and ritualism, and was thereby also lost to the West for a time.

It is perhaps one of the most difficult and at the same time most important questions of all cultural history, whether Plato, through his doctrine of the existence of a world of ideas and the—

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how Greek philosophical concepts, particularly Neoplatonism, influenced Islamic thought. Having established that the Quran was interpreted through multiple layers of meaning, the author now explores the philosophical consequences of viewing the material world as an illusion compared to the "world of ideas."</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>

...simultaneous "non-being" of the world of phenomena <note>The visible, material world as perceived by the senses.</note> visible to us has furthered or hindered humanity on its path to knowledge and in its entire spiritual development. As far as the Orient is concerned, I am inclined to assume the latter. What to the Western mind is little more than a clever intellectual game, the Orient easily accepts as unassailable truth. The entire world of phenomena is a fleeting illusion, while that which truly exists has rested since all eternity in the transcendent world of ideas—the perfect archetypes—into which we, along with the world of phenomena, will one day return.

> “You asked me about the world of appearances,
> Do you wish to know what the wise man thinks of it?
> A misty image that rises from the world-ocean
> And falls back into the world-ocean again.”⁹

Such a teaching fit perfectly with the Oriental tendency of turning away from the world, which more or less all the sages of the East had taught. How much more reason is there to turn away from this world if it is merely a kind of mirage, if it truly does not even exist! How much more strongly is the soul drawn to its true home, the world of ideas, which rest in the primordial source of all that exists! —

Life in this world of thought had led early on in Baghdad to the raising of the question of whether the Quran was created or—like the “immutable essences” <note>original: *’ayāni sābita*; these are the fixed archetypes or potential forms of things in God's knowledge, often compared to Platonic Ideas.</note>, the (Platonic) ideas—it was pre-existent. Since the word of God cannot have changed, it must have existed from the beginning; thus, the conclusion was reached that the Quran had already existed in “beginningless eternity” <note>original: *āzāl*</note>, just like the “Tablet” <note>original: *louh*; refers to the *al-Lawḥ al-Maḥfūẓ* or the "Preserved Tablet" upon which God wrote the destiny of all creation.</note> on which the fates of all...

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Islamic metaphysics, transitioning from the Platonic "world of ideas" to the specific doctrine of the pre-existence of the Prophet Muhammad. It draws parallels between the Christian concept of the Logos and the Sufi concept of the "Muhammadan Reality."</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>13</page-num>

...beings were recorded. One only needs to follow the logic further to also assume that the **Prophet Muhammad** was **pre-existent** <note>The belief that the Prophet's spiritual essence existed before the physical creation of the world.</note>, though this doctrine was reached via a different path. As is well known, the scholars of Alexandria—Philo among them—had further developed the ancient Heraclitean doctrine of the **Logos** <note>original: *Logos*; a Greek term meaning "word," "reason," or "plan." Philo of Alexandria was a Jewish philosopher who blended Greek philosophy with religious scripture.</note> as the personified divine reason, which was said to be the first thing the Creator had brought into being. It is also well known how, in the Gospel of John, the Logos (rendered as "the Word" in Luther's translation), which was in the beginning, becomes flesh and is embodied in the person of Jesus Christ. We find this same concept again among the mystics of Islam in relation to the Prophet. Muhammad is the **'Aql** <note>original: *’Aql*; the Arabic term for "Intellect" or "Reason." In Islamic philosophy, the First Intellect is the first emanation from God.</note>, the pure reason that was with God from the very beginning, regardless of whether he was created by Him or was a part of His essence. Thus, according to a supposedly verified **Hadith** <note>original: *Hadis*; a traditional report of the sayings or actions of the Prophet Muhammad.</note>, Muhammad could say of himself: "I was already a prophet while Adam was still between the water and the clay" <note>original: *“Kuntu nebīyan wa Ādam bein el mā wa’t tīn”*</note>—that is, before the clay from which God created him had even been mixed.

But Muhammad is even more than that to the **Sufi** <note>A practitioner of Islamic mysticism.</note>. He is the occasion, the **moving cause** <note>original: *causa movens* (Latin); the motivating force that triggers an event.</note> of the entire creation. This doctrine was of even greater importance because it was not inherently clear why God decided to awaken the world—which had been slumbering in perfection—into existence, thereby exposing it to imperfection. The explanation was found again in a "Hadith," in which the Prophet has God say to him: "I was a hidden treasure and I wanted to be known, therefore I created the world." <note>A famous "Sacred Hadith" (Hadith Qudsi) expressing that the purpose of creation is for the Divine to be recognized through its manifestations.</note> And in another "Hadith," God says to His Prophet: "If it were not for you, I would not have created the spheres" <note>original: *“lou lāk lamā akhlaqtu l aflāk”*; "the spheres" refers to the celestial bodies or the entire universe.</note>. Thus is the **Muhammad-Logos**...

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Muhammadan Reality," concluding the argument that the Prophet is the spiritual cornerstone of creation before transitioning into how this concept of divine authority and pre-existence extends to the Imams of Shiism and the spiritual masters of Sufism.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>

...the reason and the explanation simultaneously for the creation of the world, just as he is also the capstone of prophethood on earth.

If one continues thinking along these lines, one arrives at even more astonishing results: The entire worldview of the Semitic religions is founded upon a belief in authority. Without this—and its prerequisite, revelation—it cannot survive. Therefore, it presupposes a prophet for every era who delivered God’s revelations and commands to humanity and served as their leader. Since no prophet could appear after ***Muhammad***, the twelve Imams took their place among the **Shiites** <note>The branch of Islam that believes leadership should have remained within the family of the Prophet Muhammad.</note>, the last of whom, the **Mahdi**, still lives today in concealment. He is the **Lord of the Age** <note>original: *sāhib zamān*; a title for the Mahdi, signifying his ongoing spiritual authority over the world despite his physical absence.</note>. This idea is less pronounced in the Caliphate of the **Sunnis**. In contrast, in **Sufism** <note>The mystical dimension of Islam.</note>—which leans much more toward **Shiism** than **Sunnism**—the **Pirs** <note>original: *Pire*; a Persian term for "elders" or spiritual guides who lead disciples on the mystical path.</note> or spiritual leaders play a role similar to the Imams. **Ali** is not only the first Imam of the Shiites (and the **Ismailis**), but also the starting point of Sufism. Given the boundless veneration that the Pirs enjoyed from their followers and successors, it was inevitable that they, too, would be attributed **pre-existence** <note>The belief that these holy figures existed as spiritual lights before the physical creation of the world.</note>, just like the Prophet. They have thus existed from the very beginning—if there is a beginning at all—and have therefore shared in the attributes of the Supreme Being. When they were then sent to earth, each in his own time, they still partook of divine wisdom. What they taught were merely memory-images, **recollections** <note>original: *Anamnesen*; referencing the Platonic theory that "learning" is actually the soul recovering knowledge it possessed in a celestial state before birth.</note>, from their earlier and true state. Thus, in the most monotheistic of all religions, not only is its founder, the Prophet ***Muhammad***, deified, but there is also...

...besides him, a whole Olympus of demigods <note>A metaphorical comparison by the author to Greek mythology, referring to the high spiritual rank of Sufi saints.</note> was created, who, albeit in a very abstract way, participate in the divine attributes. In the second book of the ***Masnavi*** <note>original: *Mesnevi*; the masterpiece of Persian mystical poetry by Rumi.</note>, **Jelal ad-Din** <note>original: *Dschelāl ed dīn*; referring to Rumi (1207–1273), the famous Persian poet and Sufi mystic.</note> presents this doctrine of the existence of the **Pirs** <note>original: *Pire*; spiritual guides or elders.</note> before the creation of the world in a peculiar way:

### The Pre-existence of the Pirs.

> Where they see a door, only a wall appears to you,
> What is a stone to you, is a diamond to them.
> More than the mirror’s glow reflects back to you,
> Is revealed to the **Pir** in a simple brick.
> **Pirs** are those who, before the word of creation <note>Referring to the divine command "Be!" (*Kun* in Arabic) which brought the world into existence.</note>
> Had yet fallen, already lived in the treasury of Omnipotence.
> There they lived before space and time,
> Before a sea even existed, pearls <insert>10</insert> were already strung.
> While the plan for the universe was still being weighed,
> They already stood up to their necks in the process of becoming;
> And when the angels then, when asked for counsel
> (Before He spoke the great word of creation),
> Advised against the making of creatures,
> The **Pirs** were already shaking their heads.
> They had already found the form of all beings
> Before God had bound Himself to any norm.
> Before the vault of heaven was constructed,
> They had long since gazed upon **Saturn** <insert>10a</insert>.
> The taste of bread was already known to them,
> When no field of grain yet stood upon the earth.
> When there was no army and no war,
> The **Pirs** had long known victory.
> Before there was a brain, before a heart pulsed,
> The **Pirs** had already philosophized.

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of Sufi cosmology, specifically the pre-existence of the Pirs (spiritual masters) as described in Rumi’s Masnavi. It follows the previous page’s discussion on how these holy figures existed in the divine mind before the physical creation of the world.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>

> “Before the miner’s pick ever reached the ore,
> They had already separated the metal from the dross.
> They were drunk from the juice of the grape
> Even before the Creator’s power had brought forth the vine.
> In winter’s depth, they sensed the summer’s joy;
> In the middle of December, they saw the month of August.
> They saw the shadow within the sun’s radiance,
> And in absolute Nothingness, they saw Being.”

And then the poet further explains to us what the philosophizing of the <term>Pirs</term> <note>spiritual guides or elders</note> consisted of in that state of primal slumber. The content of their thought was none other than those <term>fixed essences</term> <note>original: *‘ayāni sābita*; in Sufi metaphysics, these are the eternal archetypes or latent realities of all things as they exist in God’s knowledge before being manifested.</note>—the (Platonic) Ideas or archetypal concepts—which already existed back then. At a time when space and time, past and future, did not yet exist, these ideas dwelt easily together:

> “Their thoughts were the ‘Ideas’—
> That is, the world that we see embodied.
> Still liberated, their thoughts hovered there,
> Free from the barriers of past and future.
> Where these two obstacles vanish,
> The solution to the riddle is easily found.”

In this world of Ideas, the Pirs had achieved absolute unity with God and, consequently, unity with one another—a state that serves as the aim and goal of all <term>mysticism</term> <note>The tradition of seeking direct, personal experience of the Divine.</note>.

> “And if we see two Pirs united, we can
> Call them one, or even a hundred thousand.
> Number is like water that ripples
> When the breath of the west wind <note>original: *Zephir*</note> whispers over it.
> Their multiplicity is like the waves
> When the winds sweep across the single sea.”

It emphasizes the mystical unity where multiplicity (the many) is seen as merely surface ripples on the single ocean of Divine Being.</summary>

> Only the sun’s light is divided among souls,
> When it breaks through the windows of individuals. 11)
> If you look at the sun, it is only one,

> Animal instinct 12 alone is divided,

> The human soul is the heir to the World Spirit,
> The animal soul is but a shard of matter.

If one presupposes the *Ideas* along with the *Tablet of Destiny* <note>original: *Schicksalstafel*; refers to the *al-Lawh al-Mahfuz* or "Preserved Tablet," which in Islamic theology contains the eternal records of all that has been and will be.</note> as the long-established plan of the world and all that happens, the Creator needed only to speak the weight-filled little word "*Kun!*" <note>original: *Kun*; Arabic for "Be!"</note>, "*Be!*" and the world stood there, complete. This, however, by no means implies a denial of the account of creation in the *Koran*. The creation of Adam from earth and water presupposes the prior existence of these two elements. But even here, alongside Ancient Semitic tradition, Greek science plays a role, which taught the development of higher beings from the inorganic world through plants and animals. We encounter this theory as well in the *Masnavi*:

> Humanity began in the mineral kingdom
> And then transitioned to the plant kingdom.
> There it lived for unmeasured eons
> And has forgotten its mineral state.

11. The image the poet has in mind here is that of sunbeams divided by the many small individual bullseye windowpanes <note>small, thick panes of glass common in older architecture</note> in the dome of an Oriental bath, which could create the impression of a multiplicity of light sources. 12. In addition to pure reason, it was assumed there was an "animal intellect" directed toward survival and the external needs of life. Only pure reason is a part of <term>Divine Reason</term> <note>original: *Logos*; the Greek philosophical concept of the "Word" or "Reason" that acts as the bridge between God and the created world.</note>, which was in the beginning. It is one and indivisible in God and in all beings endowed with reason.

<summary>This page explores the Sufi perspective on the unity of the human soul versus the multiplicity of the physical world, using the metaphor of sunlight through windows. It also discusses the spiritual evolution of the soul from mineral and plant states into the human form, influenced by both Islamic and Greek philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Sufism, Masnavi, Rumi, Logos, Evolution of the Soul, Tablet of Destiny, Creation, Unity of Being</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul’s journey through the stages of existence as described in Rumi’s Masnavi. Having previously covered the mineral and plant stages, the text now transitions into the animal and human stages and explores the philosophical origins of these ideas.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>

> When it finally found its way into the animal kingdom,
> The plant kingdom also vanished from its memory,
> Save for the longing that draws it toward the green,
> Especially when the lovely spring <note>original: *Lenz*; a poetic German term for springtime</note> blooms.
> It resembles the yearning that unconsciously
> Draws children toward their mother’s breast. —
> Yet, from animal nature, in due time,
> The Creator raised it up to humanity.
> Thus, it rose slowly from species to species,
> Until it became endowed with reason and wise;
> Of its spiritual state in its former lives,
> It can give no account of itself.

We might believe we are hearing Darwin or Haeckel <note>Ernst Haeckel (1834–1919) was a German biologist who championed Darwinism and proposed that "ontogeny recapitulates phylogeny."</note> here, but it is in reality Aristotle who is the precursor of the theory of evolution. Mysticism gladly links itself to this doctrine in order to point toward an even further transformation awaiting humanity: the return to the state of annihilation <note>original: *Fenā*; the Sufi concept of the "passing away" or dissolution of the individual ego into the Divine</note>, of non-existence—that is, the merging into the World Soul. This is the point at which Persian Sufism comes closest to the Indian world of thought. If we equate the annihilation <note>original: *Fenā*</note> sought by the Sufis with the *Nirvana* <term>*Nirvana*: a Sanskrit term in Indian religions referring to the liberation from the cycle of rebirth and the extinction of individual desire</term> of the Indians, a correspondence between both worlds of ideas is often revealed that reaches even to the literal wording, 13 though this is not to be regarded as proof of a direct connection. In itself, a parallel development could also be the cause of the correspondence. However, we are likely not mistaken if we assume a significant influence from India on the origin and shaping of Persian mysticism, even if we are not always able to prove this connection in detail. But I would also like to point out one difference: Annihilation <note>original: *Das Fenā*</note>

13. Regarding the influence of Indian teachings on the development of Sufism, Goldziher <note>Ignác Goldziher (1850–1921), a Hungarian scholar regarded as one of the founders of modern Islamic studies</note> has provided exhaustive information in his *Lectures on Islam*.

<summary>This page concludes the poetic description of the soul's ascent from the animal realm to human reason, while situating Rumi's evolutionary ideas between Aristotelian science and Indian concepts of Nirvana.</summary>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between Persian Sufism and Indian philosophy, specifically focusing on the concept of spiritual annihilation and the poetry of Rumi. It follows the previous page's discussion on the soul's evolution and its ultimate goal of merging with the World Soul.</meta>

<page-num>19</page-num>

(The annihilation) <note>original: *Fenā*; the Sufi concept of the dissolution of the ego</note> of the Sufis is not quite the same as the *Nirvana* <term>*Nirvana*: the Indian concept of liberation from the cycle of rebirth and the extinction of individual desire</term> of the Indians. It appears to me not quite so negative, not so absolute as the latter, and refers more to the state before death than after. Therefore, Sufi doctrine also speaks of a *baqā bil fanā* <term>*Baqā bil Fanā*: "subsistence through annihilation," a state where the mystic survives in God after their individual ego has passed away</term>, an “eternal life in annihilation.” Nevertheless, both worldviews share a complete turning away from life and a predominantly negative character. And it is precisely this negativity—in ancient Indian hymns as in Sufi songs—that often lends them their unrivaled sublimity. The image of the Godhead, purified of all attributes, stands high above the humanized conceptions such as those held by the Greeks and the Germanic peoples regarding their gods. And it is the same with the intoxicated state of ecstasy into which the Sufi enters when he feels himself at one with the Godhead. The *Diwan-i Shams-i Tabrizi* <note>The "Great Divan," a collection of lyric poems by Rumi</note>, the collection of odes which *Jalal ad-Din* dedicated to his spiritual guide (*Shams-i Tabrizi*, the “Sun of *Tabriz*”), is full of such dithyrambs <note>passionate, ecstatic hymns or poems</note> that still resonate throughout the entire Orient today. The following ode, for example, which I provide in an almost literal translation, I heard for the first time from an Afghan in *Simla*, then again from Punjabi Indians in the garden of the Taj near *Agra*, and countless times sung in Persia. It is perhaps the best known of his songs and may serve as a sample of his lyrical style.

->Ghazal from the *Diwan-i Shams-i Tabrizi* by<-
->*Jalal ad-Din Rumi*.<-

> Ye Muslims! <note>original: *Muselmänner*; an archaic European term for Muslims</note> alas, I know myself no more,
> what shall I do?
> I am no Jew and no Christian, no Parsi <note>a follower of Zoroastrianism</note> and no
> Muslim. —

...of Jalal al-Din <note>original: "Dschelāl ed dīns"; Jalal al-Din Muhammad Rumi (1207–1273), the famous Persian poet and Sufi master</note> and other mystics. They all wish to know nothing of the entire world or of themselves. Only once everything has first disappeared for them does <term>tawhid</term> <note>original: "touhīd"; the Islamic doctrine of the "Oneness of God," which in Sufism implies the realization that nothing exists but God</note> remain—the consciousness of unity with the Deity—and then their goal is reached.

It is no wonder that protests also arose against such a philosophy of non-being and non-knowing. Our poet himself was certainly often made aware of this, and he responded to it here as well. In the third book of the <term>Masnavi</term> <note>original: "Mesnevi"; Rumi's six-volume masterpiece of spiritual couplets</note>, he tells how a drunken Turk has a Persian singer perform for him and takes offense at the singer's constant negations. Not without humor, he parodies here his own lyric style, of the kind we encountered in the <term>ghazal</term> <note>original: "Ghazel"; a short lyric poem, often about love or divine longing</note> just cited. Here, the "Turk" represents the common intellect, unilluminated by the fire of mysticism, which is then answered with the arguments of esoteric doctrine:

### The Drunken Turk and the Singer.
#### From the "Masnavi," Book III.

> Before that drunken Turk, the singer then
> Sat down and began to sing his strain.
> And through the veil of the poetic art, his mouth
> Made known the deepest of mysteries:
> "I do not know—are you the full moon?
> Are you an idol <note>In Persian poetry, a "Götterbild" (idol/beauty) often represents the divine Beloved</note> enthroned before me?
> I know not how you fill me so completely,
> Nor do I know at all what you desire of me.—
> I know not what draws me—pleasure or pain?
> One moment you crush me to death, the next to your heart.
> I do not know—how shall I approach you?
> Should I love you? Should I pray to you?

It became too much for our Turk <note>In Sufi literature, the "Turk" often symbolizes a person of action or the literal-minded intellect, contrasted here with the Persian singer's mystical abstraction.</note> after all;

It brings you a scent of the positive.

Just as one only knows the truth in death.

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Sufi "via negativa" (the way of negation) as expressed in Rumi's poetry. <page-num>23</page-num>

> Life draws a veil before your eye.
> Until the light of the stars has fully faded for you,
> You certainly will not behold the bright sun.
> Therefore, turn the mace rather against yourself! <note>A reference to the previous page, where a "Turk" (the literal mind) threatened the singer with a mace. Here, the mace is turned inward to destroy the ego.</note>
> Strike hard, friend, and smash your "I"! <note>original: "Ich". In this context, it refers to the "nafs" or the lower, selfish ego.</note>
> For, if you wish to gain Knowledge <term>Erkenntnis: specifically spiritual gnosis or direct perception of Truth</term> here already,
> Follow the Prophet’s advice: "Die before you die." <note>original: "Stirb vor dem Sterben." This is a famous prophetic tradition (Hadith) in Sufism, referring to the spiritual death of the ego before the physical death of the body.</note>

Thus, only the negation of everything perceptible is the path to the knowledge of reality, and only in ecstasy <term>Ekstase: a state of mystical union where the physical senses are suspended</term> does the inner eye become seeing. The effect of this teaching on the entire thought and action of the peoples who pay homage to it can hardly be overestimated. It primarily resulted in all observation of nature being regarded as superfluous, if not actually misleading, and thus it vanished from human thought.

With us in the West, this was also the case during the scholastic Middle Ages <note>Scholasticism was a medieval school of philosophy that prioritized logic and traditional authority over empirical observation.</note>—and God alone knows for how long thereafter. It was not so very long ago that the German scholar, in order to inform himself about the nature of the lion, looked it up in Aristotle or Pliny; or, to enrich his geographical knowledge, consulted Herodotus or Strabo—to say nothing of the unchallenged authority of the Old Testament in all matters, even those outside of religion. 

But for us, the Renaissance and the Reformation brought light and air. The Islamic Orient has not been able to free itself from the shackles of authority supported by mystical philosophy, and the typical Muslim thinker is that Sufi who, instead of finding edification in the beauty of nature, turns his gaze inward. There, he believes he can perceive everything much better—as a memory-image of the original world of perfection.

<summary>The author explains that the Sufi "way of negation" leads to a rejection of the physical world in favor of internal mystical experience, comparing this historical phase to Western Scholasticism before the Renaissance.</summary>

### The Mystic in the Garden. (Masnavi, Book IV.)

> A mystic sits within a garden's space
> In Sufi style, his head upon his knee, <note>This posture, with the head resting on the knees, is a traditional pose for deep contemplation and "muraqaba" (meditation) in Sufi practice.</note>
> Completely lost in deep and dreaming thought.
> Until his comrades shook him from his trance:
> "Why do you drowse? Just look around yourself,
> How nature sprouts and springs on every side,
> How trees and bushes stand in fruit and bloom,
> Oh, see the signs of Allah’s loving grace!"
> He spoke: "The signs are felt, O man of senses! <note>original: "Sinnenmensch". This refers to someone who relies solely on the five physical senses to understand reality.</note>
> In all their depth only by the inner man.
> The outer world can never reach that far;
> And outward signs are only outward signs.
> To the inner gaze, the gardens green
> Are like reflections cast upon the pool.
> It only seems like truth, this shifting form,
> The image mirrored by this quiet pond.
> In the heart’s depths are the true fruit and tree;
> What you see out there is but a dream."

And in another passage, Rūmī <note>Jalal al-Din Muhammad Rumi (1207–1273) was a 13th-century Persian poet, jurist, Islamic scholar, and theologian.</note> explains how man—simply due to the brevity of his earthly existence—is incapable of forming even a remote idea of world events in the immeasurable eons of the past and future from what he perceives here:

> "What does the gnat know of how long this garden has stood?
> She, who arrived in spring and by autumn is already gone!"

Thus, the only pure source of knowledge <term>Erkenntnis: here meaning spiritual realization or gnosis</term> is meditation (<term>murāqiba</term> <note>Arabic/Persian: a Sufi term for meditation or mindfulness of the heart</note>, known as <term>dhyān</term> <note>Sanskrit: a term for meditation used in Indian traditions like Yoga and Buddhism</note> among the Indians), and it is at this point that those influenced by mysticism...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how mystical focus on the internal world led to a stagnation in the exploration of the physical world. It contrasts the development of Western rationalism against the persistent influence of mysticism in the East.</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>

...governed peoples have come to a standstill. <note>The author is referring to the historical development of societies under the influence of certain mystical or religious frameworks.</note> 14 Thereafter, the further development of a path of knowledge is cut off for them. Whoever has attained the state of proximity to God already possesses everything worth knowing within themselves and has no need to search further.

To reach this longed-for state, however, one must travel a long and arduous path "upon which one ascends step by step," from the first stage of renunciation <note>original: *tabattul*; refers to the Sufi concept of detachment from the world to focus solely on God</note> to the final stage of self-annihilation <note>original: *fenā*; the "passing away" or extinction of the individual ego within the Divine</note>. We have already seen (pages 8 and 9) how even the...

---

14. Turning away from the world led throughout the entire Scholastic Middle Ages <note>Scholasticism was a medieval school of philosophy that used logic to defend religious dogma; the author argues it delayed scientific progress.</note> to a turning away from the observation of nature—aside from astrology and alchemy, which then gradually led back to a knowledge of nature—and thus prevented any progress in the knowledge of the real world. It was the same for us in the West: the curriculum of our humanistic secondary schools is mainly a remnant of this Scholastic period. In the East, mysticism was stronger than it was here. While it did not hold the masses in its spell as much, it did hold nearly all the leading minds in its grip, preventing them from returning to the free exploration of the physical world. This is likely primarily attributable to the apparent "scientific" nature of the artificial interpretation of the Quran. In this interpretation, a complete philosophical system stood as a support for religious dogma. Christian dogma did not have such a strong and compelling auxiliary power at its disposal in its Scholastic philosophy, and therefore the peoples of the West could more easily work their way through to criticism and rationalism. Through this, new nourishment was also provided to religious life itself here, while in the East, as a result of overly long stagnation, a state of decay often set in. During a visit I paid to the tomb of Jalal al-Din Rumi in Konya <note>A city in modern-day Turkey, the center of the Mevlevi Sufi order.</note> in 1904, I found among the Mevlevi Dervishes <note>Followers of Rumi, often known as the "Whirling Dervishes."</note> who guarded the sanctuary of their order's founder, only one who was proficient in the Persian language at all and capable of understanding the master's words. The others did indeed know individual parts of the *Masnavi* <note>Rumi's primary poetic work, written in Persian.</note> by heart, yet they cared little for their meaning. The entire *dhikr* <note>original: *zikr*; a ritualized "remembrance" of God through prayer and repetition.</note> (liturgical service) including the *sama* <note>original: *samāʿ*; the "spiritual listening" or mystical whirling dance.</note> (the mystical dance) had become a hollow and unintelligible formula to them.

<summary>The author argues that Sufi mysticism's focus on internal divinity led to a decline in scientific inquiry in the East, contrasting it with the rise of Western rationalism. He illustrates this decay with a personal anecdote from 1904 about Mevlevi Dervishes who performed Rumi's rituals without understanding his Persian verses.</summary>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general discussion of mystical detachment to the specific figure of Rūmī. It addresses the common critique that his teachings lacked a rigid, step-by-step structure, contrasting this with his focus on divine love and his potential influences from early Christian thought in Anatolia.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>

Contemporaries criticized Rūmī for not establishing a precise ladder <note>The author uses "ladder" (Stufenleiter) to refer to the formal stages or "stations" (*maqāmāt*) usually found in Sufi manuals.</note> of progression, unlike other spiritual leaders, through which one could reach the goal with certainty, even if not without difficulty. In the flowing surge of mystical thoughts and feelings, the sober-minded observer missed the mathematical lines of a clearly recognizable system, while the enthusiast found his entire *philosophy of life* within it. In reality, Rūmī gave his disciples and successors much more than a mere *system*. He led them onto the path of divine love—a path that perhaps none of the many Persian, Turkish, and Hindustani mystics walked with such total devotion. To him, the macrocosm and the microcosm <note>original: "die große und die kleine Welt"; refers to the universe and the individual human being as reflections of one another.</note>, this world and the hereafter, are nothing but the inevitable foundation upon which he built his mystical doctrine of union with the World-Soul. Every one of the many tales in the *Masnavi* <note>original: *Mesnevi*; Rūmī’s massive six-volume poetic masterpiece of spiritual teaching.</note> has only this one goal; the *Quran* <note>original: *Koran*</note> and all religions have only this one meaning. All images and parables borrowed from the sensory world suffer from the imperfection inherent to them, but he uses them because the human spirit is not perfect or purified enough to conceive of the Absolute without them. In the intense passion with which he loses himself in this thought—and often in the way he builds his theories upon established truths of salvation—Rūmī frequently reminds one of the Apostle *Paul*, whose work he undoubtedly became acquainted with in *Konya* <note>original: *Konia*</note>, the ancient *Iconium* <note>A major city in central Turkey where Rūmī lived; it was also a significant center in early Christian history visited by St. Paul.</note>.

Yet as much as the convergence between *Sufism* and Rūmī specifically with Christianity is striking, there remains a deep chasm separating them, especially in the field of ethics. Although Rūmī, in con—

<summary>This page explores Rūmī's preference for divine love over rigid spiritual systems and notes his similarities to the Apostle Paul, likely due to his life in the historically Christian city of Konya.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Rūmī’s theological position on free will and transitions into the historical influence of Greek philosophy on Sufism. The text begins mid-sentence, completing a comparison between Rūmī’s mysticism and other contemporary Islamic and Christian viewpoints.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>

[In contra]st to many of his Muslim contemporaries, he proceeds from the prerequisite of a free will within certain limits, which leaves humans with sufficient responsibility to still make virtue possible; yet, at the same time, resignation to fate or to the will of a higher power prevails so strongly in his thought that his moral teachings never soar to the heights and practical activity of Christian ethics. “<term>Islam</term>” means “devotion” <note>original: "Islām" and "Hingebung"; the author defines the term "Islam" here specifically as surrender or submission to the Divine.</note>, and Rūmī’s ethics do not go beyond this devotion.

Meanwhile, Sufi ethics is a vast field and a long, as yet almost unwritten chapter, which cannot be discussed in more detail here, as important as it is. Explaining it would far exceed the scope of this brief sketch. The reader will gain a fairly complete picture of it from the work of Georg Rosen <note>Georg Rosen (1820–1891) was a German diplomat and orientalist known for his early translations of Rūmī.</note>. My intention was also—merely as a supplement to what Georg Rosen had presented from the first book of the **Masnavi**—to use the remaining five books of the **Masnavi** and the **Divan** <note>original: "Mesnevi" and "Diwan"; the latter refers to the *Diwan-e Shams-e Tabrizi*, Rūmī’s collection of lyrical poems.</note> to offer an insight into the connections between Rūmī’s worldview and Greek philosophy, specifically with Platonic and Neoplatonic teachings. 15 This was intended to...

***

15. Many readers will find themselves asking how this Platonic philosophy reached Persia of all places. Where was the bridge between the two so different worlds of Hellenism and the “barbarian world” <note>original: "Barbarentum"; the author uses this in the classical Greek sense to refer to non-Greek lands, specifically the Persian Empire.</note>? There was, of course, not just one such path; however, one particular process must be regarded as especially important: as is well known, ancient Greek philosophy long outlived the political downfall of its homeland. The deep impression that Plato, above all, had left behind brought about a continuation of his philosophy, the so-called **Neoplatonism**. The Neoplatonists intended merely to cultivate and explain the master’s teaching. However, they gradually mixed it more and more with Pythagorean theories, especially the philosophy of numbers—

<meta>This page continues the introduction from page 27, completing the author's justification for studying Rūmī within a global historical context. It then provides a detailed historical sidebar—continuing from the footnote on the previous page—explaining the migration of Greek Neo-Platonic philosophers to the Persian Sassanid court after the closure of the Academy in Athens.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>

...the gaze of the philosopher, the religious scholar, and the cultural historian be directed toward these interesting connections. And it was a matter of integrating the work of the great Oriental mystic—which often seems so strange and alien to us—into the continuous tapestry of the intellectual labor of all those peoples who were shaped by the culture of classical antiquity. In doing so, I have intentionally refrained from [mentioning] parallel developments, such as those found in Christianity and also

[philo]sophy and permeated with mystical elements from Greece and later also from the Orient, seeking to reconcile them with the often contradictory teachings of Aristotle. Moreover, more and more men of non-Greek origin, such as the Phoenician Porphyry and the Syrian Iamblichus, emerged within the Neoplatonic movement. Nevertheless, the study of Plato's works remained their primary concern. In them, they also sought a counterweight to the triumphantly advancing Christianity, against which they had an increasingly difficult position. Their school of philosophy persisted in Athens until Emperor Justinian brought it to an end through an edict in the year 529, which confiscated its assets and forbade the teaching of philosophy in Athens. Several of the last philosophers of Athens thereafter decided to emigrate to Persia, where the powerful and famous Sassanid ruler Khosrow Anushirwan—or simply Nushirwan <note>Khosrow I, also known as Anushirvan the Just (r. 531–579 AD), was one of the most celebrated kings of the Sassanid Empire, known for his reforms and patronage of learning.</note>—reigned at that time. It was known that Nushirwan protected and cultivated Greek education. To him migrated Damascius the Cilician, Simplicius the Lydian, Priscianus, Eulamius from Phrygia, the Phoenicians Hermias and Diogenes, and Isidorus of Gaza. In his capital, Ctesiphon, these Greek philosophers hoped to find something resembling the Platonic ideal state; however, despite the friendly reception they received there, they did not long endure in the “barbarian land.” Yet this journey was of great significance for the continued existence of <term>Neoplatonism</term> <note>A late school of Greek philosophy that combined Plato’s ideas with mysticism and religious elements, significantly influencing both Christian and Islamic thought.</note> after its decline in Athens. At that time, it struck such deep roots within the Persian spirit that, when a century later Islam toppled the Sassanids from their throne, Greek philosophy did not perish alongside <term>Zoroastrianism</term> <note>The ancient monotheistic religion of Persia prior to the Islamic conquest.</note>, but was instead able to merge closely with the teachings of Muhammad and even serve as a support for them.

<summary>The author describes how Rūmī’s work fits into the broader history of human thought and recounts how Neoplatonic philosophers fled to Persia in 529 AD. This migration allowed Greek philosophy to survive the fall of Athens and eventually merge with Islamic theology.</summary>

<keywords>Neoplatonism, Sassanid Empire, Khosrow Anushirwan, Ctesiphon, Justinian’s Edict, Aristotle, Plato, Persian philosophy, Islam and Greek thought</keywords>

The author argues that mysticism is not an obscure sideline but the key to understanding the internal logic and "soul" of Eastern nations.</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>

...to point out the connections offered by Indian religions in their mysticism. Perhaps this will yet be done by someone more qualified.

I would like to say just one more word here about the significance that the study of a work—one that is characteristic and authoritative for the entire intellectual orientation <note>original: *Geistesrichtung*</note> of large groups of peoples—possesses for a deeper knowledge of their historical development. If ever the historian—and with him the statesman—should set about understanding the history of the Orient from its inner life, then the study of such "ferments of decomposition" <note>original: *Zersetzungsfermente*. The author suggests mysticism acts as a catalyst that can break down rigid structures or permeate a culture from within.</note> like mysticism, which he now mostly barely notices, will be indispensable to him for judging the past as well as the deeper foundations of the present. 

In such intellectual currents and in their centuries-long cultural struggles, he will find the bond that strings together and connects events that are seemingly loose and disconnected. He will thereby gain the touchstone <note>original: *Prüfstein*; a standard or criterion by which something is judged.</note> by which he recognizes which social and political changes can be organically developed or assimilated, and which, being only externally imposed, are perceived by the "soul of the people" <note>original: *Volksseele*. A term common in 19th-century thought referring to the collective spirit or character of a nation.</note> as foreign bodies and must be expelled again in the natural course of history. 

In short, he will arrive at some point where he can answer the question of which state and social organisms in modern international life are capable of living and developing, and which are not. The external history of the Islamic states <term>Islamic states: specifically referring here to the Ottoman, Persian, and potentially Mughal historical contexts</term> will then hold fewer surprises for him. But whoever does not know these driving or inhibiting ideas and forces within Islamic communities has not yet set foot on the path that leads to an understanding of the history as well as the politics of the Orient.

But the value of these studies is not limited to that: We can also learn much from it for ourselves,

<meta>This page begins the "Preface to the First Edition" by Friedrich Rosen. # Preface to the First Edition

## To the Reader

The present work is intended to help make one of the most famous products of Persian mysticism better known in Germany. It is a work which many Muslims <note>original: *Muhammedanern*</note> regard as almost equal in sanctity to the Quran and the Sunnah <note>The Sunnah refers to the body of traditional social and legal customs and practices of the Islamic community.</note>, and which, like them, bears the honorary title of *sharif* <term>sharif: a term meaning "noble" or "sublime," used here to denote the sacred status of the text</term>. However, in presenting this publication, I cannot help but claim the indulgence of my esteemed readers in several respects. 

First, it will appear striking to the specialists among them—for it is to these that I naturally address myself first—how little consideration has been given in the explanatory notes to existing meritorious works on these same subjects. Unfortunately, a stay in the Orient that has already been extended beyond my wishes is the reason that I have been unable to follow the progress of Middle Eastern studies in my homeland in the way that would have been my duty during a stay in Germany. I therefore ask that you do not apply the standard of a work created under the sheltering wings of the Berlin Library to this one, which was written in Constantinople <note>The city now known as Istanbul; at the time of writing, it was the capital of the Ottoman Empire.</note>. To be sure, the maxim "no one is obligated beyond their ability" <note>original Latin: *ultra posse nemo obligatur*</note> only excuses ignorance and not a publication ventured under such circumstances; however, since an extraordin-

<summary>Friedrich Rosen introduces his translation of a major Persian mystical text, comparing its status to the Quran. He apologizes to scholars for his lack of access to European libraries while writing from Constantinople.</summary>

<keywords>Persian mysticism, Quran, Sunnah, sharif, scholarship, Constantinople, Berlin Library, Friedrich Rosen</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Preface to the First Edition" by Friedrich Rosen. The previous page ended mid-sentence with "publicat[ion]." Here, Rosen concludes his thought regarding his qualifications and addresses the massive scope of the Mesnevi, explaining why he chose to provide a selection rather than a complete translation.</meta>

...ion. However, since a truly profound connoisseur of the three Near Eastern languages <note>original: *vorderasiatischen Idiome*; these refer to Arabic, Persian, and Turkish</note>, during a visit to Leipzig in the autumn of 1847, had described the *Mesnevi* <term>Mesnevi: also known as the Masnavi, this is the six-volume masterpiece of spiritual couplets by the 13th-century Persian poet Rumi</term> as a work whose preparation was particularly desirable given the current state of Middle Eastern studies <note>original: *orientalischen Wissenschaften*</note>, I found no sufficient reason in the aforementioned circumstances to condemn myself to silence.

Another reproach—namely, that of incompleteness—can only be made against me by those unfamiliar with Eastern literature. In fact, the *Mesnevi* is a work of too vast a scope for one to ever hope for the appearance of a complete translation of it into German. For who would wish to devote a significant portion of their lifetime to translating thirty to forty thousand Persian couplets <note>original: *Doppelverse*</note> of very unequal poetic value into German? Even in one’s third decade of life, a person rightly shudders at this "long art," with which a short life might perhaps not keep pace. 

Nevertheless, I have found so much enjoyment in the task itself that, in the event of a favorable reception of the present sample, I would easily decide upon a continuation—though certainly using the method already applied toward the end of the shared section: that of skipping over certain passages that resist any translation. 

I have more reason to fear the criticisms that will be made of me regarding the frequently deficient construction of the verses than I do this previous censure. Perhaps, with some more attention, I might have succeeded in avoiding a large number of rhythmic errors; but I was often surprised by the ease with which the Persian poet’s thought could be rendered with equal brevity in German. I therefore proceeded from the false principle that, through a more loose treatment of the verses, I could approach the tone of the [original]...
