Only when proofreading the earlier galley proofs <note>original: *Aushängebogen*; these were preliminary printed sheets for review before final binding</note> did I become aware of the great difference that exists here between the German and Persian linguistic genius.

Of earlier attempts to translate the *Mesnevi*, only that of Hussard <note>Baron von Hussard was an early 19th-century diplomat and translator</note> in the *Mines of the East* <note>original: *Fundgruben des Orients*; a famous early journal of Oriental Studies published in Vienna</note> has become known to me, thanks to the kindness of my current superior, Count A. von Pourtalès. Unfortunately, I only encountered it at a time when the printing of my own work was already half-completed. Hussard’s sample pieces extend to the fourth story and thus comprise approximately two-thirds of my work. It was a matter of chance that I arrived at the same idea as its author regarding the transformation of the trochaic <term>trochaic: a poetic meter consisting of a long syllable followed by a short one</term> verses of the original into the five-foot iambic <term>iambic: a meter consisting of a short syllable followed by a long one, very common in English and German poetry</term> meter, which is more comfortable for the German language. The meter of the Persian text is as follows:

Each line contains three repetitions of the pattern 'long-short-long-long' (— ◡ — —) separated by spaces, followed by a final foot consisting of a long mark (macron) with a small circle above it and a short mark (breve) below it.</image-desc>

->Listen to the reed, how it tells a tale,<-
->Of separations, it makes a complaint,<-
<note>original Persian: *bíshnou ez nei | tshún hikájet | míkuned, / ez dshudái | há shikájet | míkuned*. This is the famous opening "Song of the Reed."</note>

> Hear the tone of the reed, what it says to you,
> Hear the flute, how it laments because of the separation;

For which I have said:

> Listen to the flute’s reed, what it proclaims,
> Hear how it laments, ignited by the pain of longing,

And Mr. von Hussard:

> The flute hear, how it mournfully laments
> At the sighing image (?) of its separation.

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Rosen's preface to his translation of Rumi's Masnavi. On the previous page, Rosen compared his choice of poetic meter (iambic pentameter) to a previous translation attempt by Baron von Hussard, noting that while he arrived at the same meter by chance, he only discovered Hussard's work after his own was well underway.</meta>

While, however, Mr. von Hussard makes use merely of so-called blank verse <term>blank verse: unrhymed poetry, typically in iambic pentameter</term>, I have consistently applied rhyme, which seems to me absolutely indispensable when imitating Oriental <note>In the 19th century, "Oriental" was the standard scholarly term for the cultures of the Near, Middle, and Far East</note> poetic works. In any case, even a superficial comparison will convince anyone that this later work stands independent of the earlier one, just as the interpretation of many passages is completely different.

To move on now to the content: the question of to what extent the *Mesnewi* <note>The *Masnavi-i Ma'navi* by Jalal al-Din Rumi, a masterpiece of Persian Sufi poetry</note> deserves the high regard it enjoys among all Muslims <note>original: *Muhammedanern*</note> will receive different answers depending on the Eastern and Western points of view. The educated Easterner <note>original: *Morgenländer*, literally "man of the morning lands"</note> sees in it the highest perfection of a book of devotion—a work whose absorption into the spirit and heart surely leads him toward salvation, as he learns to understand it from the text; it is a product of higher spiritual consecration that leaves everything similar in religious contemplativeness and intimacy far behind.

That the Westerner <note>original: *Abendländer*, literally "man of the evening lands"</note> cannot unconditionally agree with this praise likely requires no special reminder. Mysticism is generally not the product of a strong, healthy age; on the contrary, we usually see it raise its head in periods of moral and physical exhaustion which typically follow the highest flowering of nations. It unites, as it were, the inalienable rights that religion otherwise exercises over entire peoples into an unanticipated power within the hearts of a few chosen ones. Such a time in Islam followed the first centuries of the Hegira <note>The *Hijra*, marking the start of the Islamic calendar in 622 CE</note>, during which we see the new religion develop in political and literary unity with a strength that stands almost unparalleled in history. After that, however, began

<summary>Friedrich Rosen defends his use of rhyme over Hussard's blank verse and begins discussing the cultural divide in how Rumi's Masnavi is perceived, linking the rise of Sufi mysticism to periods of political decline.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Rosen's historical and cultural contextualization of Rumi's work. On the previous page, Rosen argued that mysticism typically arises during periods of national decline rather than in "strong, healthy" ages.</meta>

...gradually, through the reawakening of national sentiment among the various peoples subject to the Caliphate <term>Caliphate: the Islamic state led by a Caliph, considered a successor to the Prophet Muhammad</term>, a centrifugal movement began to manifest within the unified whole. Shrewd leaders and governors utilized this sentiment to fight for their independence, ensuring—particularly in Persia—that it was constantly nourished by a newly revived and carefully cultivated native literature. Thus were formed those famous dynasties which, for the most part, appeared with world-shaking force, only to be eclipsed and destroyed after an ephemeral <term>ephemeral: short-lived</term> existence by others rising with equal brilliance, which in turn shattered against the same cliff as their predecessors at the first sign of a storm. 

The generous support that most rulers of these dynasties granted to the sciences delayed the general decline, which nevertheless soon became increasingly apparent. Seljuk <term>Seljuks: a medieval Turko-Persian Sunni Muslim empire that ruled large parts of Central Asia and the Middle East</term> Turkish hordes easily gained the upper hand in lands whose victorious armies had only recently made three continents tremble; meanwhile, the heresies of Gnostic-atheistic sects, such as the Ismailis <note>The author characterizes the Ismaili branch of Shia Islam as "Gnostic-atheistic," reflecting the polemical historical sources common in the 19th century</note>, undermined the last vestiges of religious and moral consciousness among the degenerated peoples. 

This is the field upon which the seed of Islamic <note>original: *muhammedanischer*</note> mysticism germinated. This mysticism reached its most beautiful flowering in the Sheikh Mevlānā Jelāl ad-Dīn Rūmī, the author of the *Mesnevi* and founder of the great Mevlevi dervish order, shortly after that most dangerous outbreak of the "ulcer of irreligiosity"—the state of the *Haschischi* (Assassins) <note>The Nizari Ismaili state, whose members were famously called "Assassins" in European accounts; they were suppressed by the Mongols in 1256</note>—had been eradicated with fire and sword, and just as the pagan Mongols from one side and the Christian Crusaders from the other threatened to annihilate Islam. # The Life of our Master Jalal al-Din Rumi Muhammad, Son of Muhammad, Son of Hassan, the Balkhite,¹ the Bakrite.²

As the model for those who seek Truth in this world, the pure—Our Master <term>Mewlānā: A title meaning "Our Master," here referring specifically to Jalal al-Din Rumi</term> is honored by the great among nations and the small, by the noble and the common. His chaste heart is a treasure of divine mysteries, and his overflowing thoughts are the place upon which infinite lights pour out and flow down. The grace of his speech clarifies the riddles of the invisible world <note>Referring to 'Alam al-Ghayb, the realm of the unseen in Sufi theology</note>; through the tidings of the Clear Conscience, he makes manifest the path to the essence of the Truth. In the valley of glowing aspiration, his path satisfies those who are parched and suffocating with the spicy, refreshing Water of Life of knowledge <note>A reference to the *Ab-i Hayat* or Water of Immortality, a common metaphor for spiritual wisdom</note>, and guides the wanderer out of the ignorance that distances one from the goal, back onto the straight, joyful path. In all circles, his words are highly honored; among all the wise, his knowledge and insight are deeply proven. No reed pen <note>original: "Schreibrohr," referring to the traditional 'qalam' used in Islamic calligraphy</note> has been granted the power to praise his perfection according to its value, or to describe it as is fitting.

->Even if the imagination deems it possible,<-
->To capture his praise in words,<-

->Imagination itself can never grasp the praise of our Master—<-
->How then should it fit into speech?<-

But I will set aside my own weaknesses and failings, and report his appearance and history according to the words of Dawlatshah.

Our Master <term>Mewlānā: "Our Master," the common honorific for Jalal al-Din Rumi</term> was a native of Balkh <note>An ancient city in modern-day Afghanistan, then a major center of Persian culture</note>, where his father Baha al-Din—hailing from one of the most distinguished and respected families of scholars there—had acquired such prestige and trust among his fellow citizens through his instruction in the metaphysical and positive sciences <note>In this context, "positive sciences" refers to traditional religious jurisprudence and transmitted knowledge (Nakli), as opposed to purely speculative philosophy</note> during the time of Sultan Muhammad Khwarazm-Shah <gloss>3</gloss> that the greatest crowds of both the noble and the common flocked to his sermons, his hours of devotion, and his lessons. For these reasons, Sultan Muhammad Khwarazm-Shah began to envy and suspect Baha al-Din; the latter, however, noticed this hostility and took it as an occasion to emigrate from Balkh with his entire family, his friends, and a large number of his followers to embark on a pilgrimage. He swore an oath not to return to Balkh or Khorasan so long as Muhammad remained in power there. The journey led him and his companions through Nishapur, where the Sheikh Farid al-Din Attar <gloss>4</gloss> met him—

***

<gloss>3. Chr." (BC), but the context and dates clearly indicate AD</note>) to 617 (1220).</gloss>

<gloss>4. After Jalal al-Din, the greatest mystical poet that Persian literature has to show. He was born near Nishapur in the year 513 of the Flight (1119 AD; not, as stated in Hammer’s *History of the Fine Arts of Persia*, 613 of the Flight or 1216 AD), thus nearly a century earlier than his successor <note>referring to Rumi</note> who would far surpass him. The meeting recounted here may nonetheless have taken place, as it is expressly attested that he lived to be 110 years old. To his</gloss>

— Attar also gave the boy his book about the secrets. <gloss>4</gloss> Then the caravan of pilgrims set out again from Nishapur and wrapped themselves in the woolen garment <gloss>5</gloss> to begin the journey to the House of God. <gloss>6</gloss> In every city they touched upon their way, the noblemen honored and hosted Baha al-Din and made use of his insights into metaphysical and positive knowledge. Burhan al-Din Muhaqqiq Tirmidhi, unreachable in philosophy and incomparable in Sufi wisdom, joined the travelers and made Baha al-Din and Jalal al-Din his pupils (Murids), explaining <term>Sufism: Islamic mysticism focused on the internal path to spiritual truth</term> to them. When they returned from the land of Hijaz, the mercy-granting, they moved on to visit the gravesites of the prophets—peace be upon them!—traveling to Damascus, where, upon arrival, Burhan al-Din Tirmidhi passed over into eternity. During the journey, Baha al-Din had been advised to go to the land of Rum <note>original: "Rūm"; the Seljuk Sultanate of Rum in Anatolia, named after the "Romans" (Byzantines) who previously ruled the area</note>, advice which he followed with his family and his companions. It was during the reign of Ala al-Din Kayqubad <gloss>7</gloss> when he arrived in Konya, which city he chose as his place of residence. There he occupied himself with teaching and preaching and acquired such universal love and veneration that Ala al-Din

> It was not known to anyone that a corpse
> Was departing from the fellowship of men;
> Though his parting from this world was painful—
> Jalal <gloss>10</gloss> was appointed as his successor!

<meta>This page continues the biography of Jalal al-Din Rumi, detailing his transition from a traditional scholar to a mystic following his father’s death and his eventual meeting with Shams al-Din Tabrizi.</meta>

Later, Jalal al-Din surpassed his father twofold in knowledge, virtue, and fame, and as is claimed, he used to deliver his lectures before a circle of four hundred listeners. However, he found no pleasure in the positive sciences <term>positive sciences: the traditional, exoteric academic disciplines like law and theology, based on established facts rather than mystical experience</term> and contemplated how to penetrate from the bonds of form into the realm of the spirit. For this purpose, he associated much with Sheikh Salah al-Din Zerkubi <note>original: "Ssalāh ed dīn Zerkūbi"; the name means "the goldsmith"</note>, an excellent man, and the famous dervish Akhi Turk, until he finally threw himself into the arms of the learned Sheikh Husam al-Din Chelebi from Konya. To the latter, among others, the following verses in the *Masnavi* are addressed (at the beginning of the third book):

->So arrange then, Husam, for the third time<-
->A book, for three is a sacred number!<-
->My song, it was delayed a while,<-
->For time requires that blood transform into milk.<- <note>This metaphor refers to the maturation of spiritual experience into shared wisdom, much like blood becomes nourishment (milk) in a mother.</note>

After some time had passed in this manner, Shams al-Din Tabrizi appeared in the later period of Mevlana’s <note>Mevlana: "Our Master," the honorific title for Rumi</note> life. Regarding the circumstances of his life, I note the following: His father, Khwand Ala al-Din, an Ismaili <gloss>11</gloss> Da'i <term>Da'i: a missionary or emissary who invites people to a specific faith or branch of Islam</term> from the tribe of Buzurg-Umid, had renounced the sect of his ancestors, burned the heretical tracts and books, established the validity of Islam in the cities and castles of the "heretics," and had secretly sent his son, Shah Shams al-Din—of whom we speak here—to Tabriz for instruction in the sciences and morality. There, Shams al-Din devoted himself to his

Yet this is of no consequence, for one should not look upon the form, but upon the spirit, since only intimacy with the world of the soul grants true fulfillment, and not physical birth.

From this point, the lineage splits into two branches. **Shams al-Din** thus became a disciple of the aforementioned **Rukn al-Din**, who had attained the rank of Sheikh through the sequence mentioned above. The trust, consideration, and care bestowed upon him by this Sheikh on one hand, and his own renunciation and diligence on the other, allowed him to partake in **Truth** and **Knowledge** and to reach the highest spiritual rank. One day, Sheikh Rukn al-Din said to him: "In the land of **Rum** <note>Rum refers to the territory of the Seljuk Sultanate of Rum in Anatolia, derived from "Rome"</note> is one seized by the fire of love; you must go there and fan this ember into a bright flame." Shams immediately submitted to this command and set off for Asia Minor. Upon his arrival in **Konya**, he beheld the **Mevlana** <note>original: "Mewlānā"; a title meaning "Our Master," referring here to Jalal al-Din Rumi</note> riding upon a mule, surrounded by a great crowd of scholars who walked beside his stirrups, escorting him home from the school. <meta>This page continues the narrative of the meeting between Jalal al-Din Rumi (Mevlānā) and his spiritual catalyst, Shams al-Din Tabrizi. The previous page ended with Shams arriving in Konya and meeting Rumi, who was surrounded by students and scholars.</meta>

"What is the purpose of this effort and renunciation, this constant preoccupation with **positive knowledge** <note>original: "positiven Wissen"; refers to traditional academic and legalistic study ('ilm) as opposed to mystical insight</note> and this diligence?" — "The purpose of my striving," answered **Mevlānā**, "is the transformation prescribed by tradition, ethics, the Sufi order, and divine law." — "All this," countered **Tabrizi**, "is merely the **surface**!" — "But what then lies beneath it?" asked **Mevlānā**. — "Only the perfect union with that which is known," replied **Shams**, "do I call **knowledge**." Thereupon, he quoted to him the following verse by **Sana’i** 12:

> Only when your knowledge frees you from yourself,
> Do I call knowledge better than ignorance.

These few words made the most vivid impression on **Mevlānā**, so that he immediately besieged **Shams** with questions and, in constant conversation with him, sought out lonely and desolate regions. But because he neglected his teaching for this reason, his students and followers mocked and persecuted **Shams**, who, as they said, had arrived bareheaded and barefoot to lead the "model of the faithful" <note>A title for Rumi, suggesting he was the exemplar of Islamic orthodoxy</note> astray from his path. Such censure and mockery caused **Shams al-Din** to flee to **Tabriz** without **Mevlānā’s** prior knowledge; the latter, however, unable to control his love and longing for that **pole of the worlds** <note>original: "Weltenpole"; a translation of the Sufi term 'Qutb', referring to the highest spiritual authority in the invisible hierarchy of saints</note>, followed him, found him, and led him back to **Asia Minor** himself. They then spent some time again in intimate companionship; but when **Mevlānā’s** students and followers continued their hostility against **Shams**, the latter found himself compelled to make a journey to **Syria**, where he remained for two years. Meanwhile, **Mevlānā**, in his pain of separation, practiced day and

->They search in vain, for naught their longing be!<-
->Yet surely from the temple rings a cry,<-
->When they embrace its threshold, drawing nigh:<-
->"Why do you pray to clay and stone, you fools?<-
->Venerate the House where purity's spirit rules!<-
->The House of the Heart, the House of the True, the One;<-
->Oh, blessed are they who into this temple have run!<-
->Hail to those who rest at home like **Shams** 13<-
->And taste not the desert path, the long, weary sun!"<-

In the *Masnavi* <note>The *Masnavi-i-Ma'navi*, Rumi's six-volume spiritual epic</note>, the following verses <gloss>14</gloss> refer to Shams al-Din:

> No being equals the Sun in majesty, —

When **Mevlānā** <note>A title meaning "Our Master," referring to Rumi</note> had reached the age of 69, he died in **Konya** <note>A city in central Turkey, then the capital of the Sultanate of Rum</note> in the year of the **Hijra** <note>original: "Flucht"; the migration of the Prophet Muhammad from Mecca to Medina, marking the start of the Islamic calendar</note> 672 (1273 AD). How very rich and visited his blessed burial place has become—a refuge for all the world, a goal for pilgrims, and a "bay of hope" for those driven by love—is known by great and small in every nation and every land. <gloss>15</gloss> After his death, he was succeeded by his son **Sultan Walad**, whose book ***Walad-nāma*** <note>The "Book of Walad," a historical and spiritual account of the early Mevlevi order</note> bears witness to his scholarship, his insight, and his penetration into the Truth. Shams Tabrizi died after Mevlānā and was likewise [buried] in Konya—

The report by **Dawlatshah** <note>A 15th-century biographer known for his "Memoirs of the Poets"</note> regarding the life of **Jalal al-Din** differs from that of **Mullah Jami** <gloss>16</gloss>, which is found in his book ***Nafahāt al-Uns*** <note>original: "Nafahhāt-ul-uns"; literally "Breaths of Fellowship" or "Breaths of Intimacy," a classic hagiography of Sufi saints</note>; we therefore also provide this account in translation.
As **Jami** relates, **Mevlānā** was born in **Balkh** <note>An ancient center of learning in modern-day Afghanistan</note> on the 6th of **Rabi' al-Awwal** <note>The third month of the Islamic lunar calendar</note> in the year of the Hijra 604 (1207 AD). According to tradition, from his sixth year onward, spiritual forms and invisible beings hidden in the "vaults of glory" (the heavens)—namely, well-meaning angels, pious jinn <note>original: "Genien"; spiritual beings in Islamic theology</note>, and holy men—revealed themselves to him and appeared in bodily form.
**Baha ud-Din Walad**, **Mevlānā’s** father, recounts the following anecdote in the collection of his works which he wrote down himself: When **Jalal al-Din** had reached his sixth year in **Balkh**, he was walking about on our roofs one Friday with a group of children. By God! If this breath is granted to you and you taste this bliss, consider it a gain and thank God, for that is I."

Someone told **Mewlānā** that a certain person claimed to be a servant with soul and heart (of the pious).

<meta>This page continues the biography and sayings of the Sufi mystic Jalal al-Din Rumi (referred to as Mewlānā), following a discussion of his spiritual presence and his relationship with his disciple Hussām ed dīn Tschelebi.</meta>

### 4 The Masnavi <note>original: "Meßnevi"; the title of Rumi's most famous poetic work of spiritual teaching</note>

Do not be far from Him for even a single moment;
For in distance, your misfortune grows.
Be with Him always, as long as you exist,
For it is only through proximity that love sprouts.

Another saying of **Mewlānā** <note>A title meaning "Our Master," referring to Jalal al-Din Rumi</note> is as follows: "Even if the bird that soars from the earth into the air does not reach heaven, it is still far from the net and therefore free. If someone becomes a **Dervish** <note>A member of a Muslim religious order, often known for vows of poverty and austerity</note> and does not reach the highest degree of dervish-hood, he is still distinguished among the great mass of earthly-minded people, as he stands above worldly troubles and gains lightness. For the light ones escape, but the heavy ones perish."

A "son of the world" <note>A term for a person preoccupied with worldly affairs rather than spiritual pursuits</note> begged for forgiveness in **Mewlānā’s** high presence because he had not paid his respects. "You do not need to apologize," **Mewlānā** replied to him, "others are obligated by your coming, but I am obligated by your not coming." — When he saw one of his friends saddened one day, he said: "All constriction of the heart has its root in clinging to this world. As often as you feel free from the earthly and recognize that you are but a stranger here below—in every color you see and every food you taste—you shall remember that all of this is transitory; then, wherever you are, you will never be sad." — Likewise, he said: "A free man is he whom the insults of men do not pain, and a hero is he who does not insult those deserving of insult." —

As the story goes, **Mewlānā** was not on the best of terms with **Sirādsch ed dīn** <note>Siraj al-Din</note> from **Konia** <note>Konya, the city in modern-day Turkey where Rumi lived</note>, a noble man who was highly respected by his contemporaries. One day, the latter was told that **Mewlānā** had remarked that he was in agreement with all seventy-three sects <note>Referring to a famous tradition (Hadith) suggesting that the Muslim community would split into 73 sects, with only one being "saved." Rumi’s claim of agreement with all of them indicates his radical universalism</note>.

<meta>This page continues the biography and sayings of the Sufi mystic Jalal al-Din Rumi (referred to as Mewlānā), recounting his interactions with critics and his views on asceticism and spiritual purity.</meta>

...ed dīn, <note>Likely referring to Mu'in al-Din Parwana, a powerful official of the time</note> a man fond of intrigue, decided to use this to offend and insult **Mewlānā**. He therefore sent a scholar from his entourage to ask **Mewlānā** in a large gathering whether he had made that statement, and in the case of an affirmative answer, to scold and mock him. That scholar therefore went to **Jalal al-Din** <note>original: "Dschelāl ed dīn"</note> and asked him: "Did you say that you agree with the seventy-three sects?" <note>A reference to a well-known tradition (hadith) stating that the Muslim community would split into seventy-three sects, only one of which would be "saved." By claiming to agree with all of them, Rumi was asserting a radical, universal inclusivity that challenged orthodox boundaries.</note> — "Indeed," replied **Mewlānā**, "and I say it still." Immediately, the scholar began to revile him and heap insults upon him. But **Mewlānā** said, smiling: "I also agree with what you are saying right now," — whereupon the scholar left in shame.

**Sheikh Rukn al-Din** <note>original: "Rukn ed dīn"</note> relates: "Among the sayings of **Mewlānā**, I have always particularly liked that when his servant answered in the negative to his question of whether there was anything to live on in the house for the day, he spoke with great joy and thanked God, saying: 'Today my house resembles that of our Prophet'; but when his servant told him: 'the kitchen is well supplied,' he would grieve and say: 'From our house rises a Pharaonic scent!'" <note>In Sufi thought, Pharaoh represents the ultimate "nafs" or ego—arrogant, self-sufficient, and attached to worldly power and luxury.</note> — For light during the meal, he lit only oil lamps and said: "This (referring to wax candles) is for kings, — this is for beggars (**Dervishes**)." — It is told that once, during his meal, someone spoke of **Sheikh Awhad al-Din Kirmani** <note>original: "Auhhad ed dīn Kirmānī"; a Sufi poet who practiced "shahid-bazi," the contemplation of Divine beauty in human forms</note>: "He surely loves beautiful youths, but in all purity, so that he has never committed anything unseemly." — "If only he had done it," said **Mewlānā**, "so that it would be over" <note>Rumi's witty retort suggests that the mental preoccupation with maintaining "purity" while gazing at beauty can be more of a spiritual distraction than the act itself; he favored direct spiritual focus over the potential fixations of "pure" aesthetic contemplation.</note> (d. h., so that his imagination would be clean again).

Infinite, brother, is the canopy of heaven,
Forward to God, wherever you wander in the world! —

<meta>This page continues the collection of anecdotes and spiritual teachings of Jalal al-Din Rumi (Mewlānā). The previous page ended mid-sentence discussing a comparison between oil lamps and spiritual light; this page begins with the conclusion of a story about the music of the spheres or the sounds of paradise heard during the Sama (whirling dance).</meta>

"...the sound of the creaking of the gate of Paradise, and we hear it!" — A mocker who heard this said: "I hear that sound too; but why does it not set me aflame as it sets **Mewlānā** aflame?" — "God protect and preserve us!" replied **Mewlānā**, "what we hear is the sound of the gate opening, but you hear the sound of it closing." —

**Mewlānā** once told this story: "Someone entered the cell of a dervish and asked him: 'Why are you sitting here so alone?' but the dervish replied: 'Now that you have entered, I am indeed alone, for you separate me from God.'" —

A congregation requested that **Mewlānā** accept the office of **Imam** <term>Imam: A leader of congregational prayer in a mosque</term> while **Sheikh Sadr al-Din** <note>original: "Ssadr ed dīn"; referring to Sadr al-Din al-Qunawi, a famous Sufi philosopher and Rumi's contemporary</note> was present. **Mewlānā** replied: "I belong to those dervishes who sit down and stand up anywhere; the office of Imam," he added, pointing to **Sadr al-Din**, "requires a man steeped in **Sufi** teachings and possessed of self-mastery." Consequently, **Sadr al-Din** became the Imam. **Mewlānā** was also fond of saying: "A God-fearing Imam acting as a prayer leader is the perfect representative of the Prophet."

One day, while **Mewlānā** was performing the sacred dance <term>original: "Reigen"; refers to the Sama or whirling dance of the Mevlevi order</term>, a dervish decided to ask him what poverty <note>18</note> was. **Mewlānā** replied, continuing his dance, with the following stanza:

> Poverty is the true essence—take heed,
> All else but this will perish, like a weed.
> Poverty is healing, forever and ever,
> While all else is sickness, a failing endeavor.
> Full of lies and deceit is the world we behold,
> This hidden treasure alone holds the world in its fold.

---

18. Poverty in the mystical sense is always to be understood as "neediness before God" <term>Arabic: *faqr*</term>; it consists of a person renouncing everything earthly and yearning only for the celestial and the divine.

<meta>This page continues the teachings and anecdotes of Jalal al-Din Rumi (Mewlānā). The previous page ended with a stanza about the nature of spiritual poverty. This page continues with Rumi’s advice on conduct and his final words regarding spiritual lineage.</meta>

**Mewlānā** was asked: “Can a **dervish** also sin?” — “Oh yes,” he said, “if he eats without hunger; for to eat without hunger is a great transgression for a **dervish**.” — He likewise said: “Socializing is a sacred thing; therefore, only engage in conversation with ‘sons of your own kind’ (that is, your equals). In this sense, my master **Shams Tabrizi** said: ‘The sign of those accepted among the **murids** <term>Murids: Disciples or initiates following a spiritual guide in Sufism</term> is that they never converse with strangers; and if a stranger suddenly interrupts their conversation, they sit there like a hypocrite in the mosque, like a child in school, and like a prisoner in a dungeon.’” —

In his final illness, **Mewlānā** spoke to his friends: “Do not be sad or distressed by my departure from this transitory world. The light of **Mansur** <note>19</note> dawned upon the spirit of **Sheikh Farid al-Din Attar** <note>A famous Persian poet and hagiographer who lived about a century before Rumi</note> after one hundred and fifty years and became his guide on the right path. Be with me at all times and remember me, and I will help you, in whatever form I may be—”

19. That is, (Ibn) **Mansur al-Hallaj**, one of the earlier mystics, who was executed in Baghdad in the year 309 of the Hijra <note>922 AD</note> for heretical teachings. He is said to have first spread the doctrine of the unity of the knower with the known, which he expressed in the words “I am the Absolute Truth” <note>original: "ana 'l-haqq"; Hallaj’s famous ecstatic utterance identifying himself with God</note>. As the story goes, his orthodox contemporaries tried in vain to persuade him to recant this assertion; even under the agonies of torture, he repeated his sentence, and after he was finally executed as a heretic, his blood even pooled into the shape of the script for “I am the Absolute Truth.” The poet **Fehim Efendi**, a **Mevlevi dervish**, alludes to this with the following words in a **ghazal** <term>Ghazal: A short lyrical poem, often dealing with themes of love or mystical longing</term>:

> The nightingale of the rose garden of “I am the Absolute Truth” <note>original: "ana 'l-haqq"</note> reveals the secret to me (that is, the doctrine of absolute unity proclaimed by **Mansur** and sealed red with his blood allows me to know God); — the flute <note>The 'ney' or reed flute, central to Mevlevi music</note> of **Shah Mansur** opens the gate of the hidden realm to me.

<summary>Mewlānā discusses the spiritual discipline of dervishes, emphasizing the importance of keeping company only with fellow seekers, and offers comfort on his deathbed by referencing the enduring spiritual influence of the martyr Mansur al-Hallaj.</summary>

As **Sadr al-Din** and the friends present gave vent to their grief through weeping and lamentation, **Mewlana** spoke this stanza:

> “Oh, if you only knew of the King to whom the cradle of the spirit
> Has carried me, so that I may nestle here against His breast!”

In his last will, **Mewlana** commanded his friends the following: “I recommend that you fear **God**, the All-True, — in secret and in the open, — and that you guard yourselves against excess in food, sleep, and speech, — further, to avoid the throngs — of those who offend against the one God, and to separate yourselves from the rabble of sinners and the punishable, — to be diligent in fasting and in renunciation, — to stand upright often <note>Referring to the standing position in prayer (Qiyam)</note> and to renounce all desires forever, — to endure insults from all the world, — to renounce — the lowly and the common, — and to seek out the pious...

<meta>This page follows the description of Rumi's death and the appointment of Hussam al-Din Chelebi as his successor. ## The Masnavi <note>original: "Mesnevi"</note>

> ->Listen to the reed flute, 1 what it proclaims,<-
> ->Hear how it complains, kindled by the pain of longing:<-
> ->"Since I was cut from the marshy reed-bed,<-
> ->The whole world has wept at my lament.<-
> ->I seek a heart torn by longing, into whose wound<-
> ->I may pour the news of my sorrow of separation:"<-

1. Regarding the origin of this introduction, the dervishes of the **Mevlevi** <note>The Sufi order founded by Rumi's followers, famous for their whirling dance</note> order—founded by **Mewlana** <note>Jalal al-Din Rumi</note>—relate the following: **Hussam al-Din Chelebi**, who was himself a famous mystical poet and the witty student and confidant of the Master, had repeatedly asked him to set down the teachings he preached in writing. When he pressed him urgently one day, **Mewlana** replied that he had already complied with his wish through divine inspiration. He then pulled a piece of paper from his turban, on which the first eighteen couplets <term>couplets: a pair of successive lines of verse, typically rhyming and of the same length</term> were found written. Because of their mysterious origin, these verses are held in the highest esteem by the **Mevlevi** and have become the subject of extensive commentaries, in which their mystical meaning has likely been stretched far beyond the original intent of their author. — The flute <note>The Persian *ney*, a reed flute</note> is one of the primary instruments of the melting, melancholic music that inspires the dervishes in their mystical dances. All its sounds are a lament—a lament, as it says here, over its separation from the reed-filled pond; and thus it is the image of the enlightened human being, whose life should also be nothing but a lament—a lament over their separation from the Divine, over the separation of the part from the Whole for which they long, until the individuality—regarded as illness and sin—is destroyed and the pure spirit is reabsorbed into the Great Unity.

<term>Masnavi: A Persian poetic form of rhyming couplets; the title of Rumi's six-volume masterpiece.</term>
<term>Mewlana: "Our Master," a title of respect for Jalal al-Din Rumi.</term>
<term>Mevlevi: The "Whirling Dervishes" or followers of Rumi's path.</term>
<term>Hussam al-Din Chelebi: Rumi's primary disciple and the scribe of the Masnavi.</term>
<term>Dervish: An initiate of a Sufi path, often practicing poverty and austerity.</term>
<term>Mysticism: The pursuit of achieving communion with, or conscious awareness of, an ultimate reality or God.</term>
<term>Sufism: The mystical tradition within Islam.</term>

<summary>This page introduces the "Song of the Reed," the opening verses of Rumi's Masnavi, and recounts the tradition that these verses were divinely inspired and produced from Rumi's turban.</summary>
<keywords>Masnavi, Rumi, Song of the Reed, Hussam al-Din Chelebi, Mevlevi, Sufi symbolism, reed flute, Divine Unity</keywords>

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page continues the translation of the opening verses of Rumi’s Masnavi (the "Song of the Reed"), following the previous page's introduction to the reed's lament over its separation from the reed-bed.</meta>

For whoever remains far from the joy of togetherness,
Always longs to return to their homeland.
Thus, wailing, I traveled through the wide world,
Joined now with the wicked, now with the good;
I was seen by everyone as a friend and companion,
— And yet no one asked what weighed upon my heart.
And yet — my secret is not so far from my lament,
It is only that the senses lack the light of understanding.
Just as soul and body are clear to one another,
Yet to what eye has a spirit ever revealed itself?"

No mere breath, but fire escapes from the reed.
Woe to him who is not ignited by this glow!
It is the glow of love that whistles in the reed, <note>2. i.e., in the flute.</note>
The sighing of love that seethes in the wine.
She <note>The flute/the melody</note> is the companion of separated lovers;
Her melody tears through one’s innermost being.
Incomparable as both poison and antidote,
Unattainable in compassion and longing,
She gives us tidings of the "path of blood," <note>3. The path in blood is love.</note>
She sings many a poem of **Majnun’s** <note>4. Surnamed "the Madman"</note> love.

---

2. i.e. in the flute. 3. The "path in blood" is love. Thus **Hafiz** <note>The famous 14th-century Persian lyric poet</note> calls out to his beloved in a *ghazal* <note>original: "Gasel"; a form of amatory poem or ode</note>: "Keep your garment far from dust and blood when you pass by me; for on this path (the one I walk, i.e., the path of love) there are many of the slain—your victims!" — 4. The love of **Qays** <note>original: "Kais"</note>, surnamed **Majnun** <note>original: "Medschnun"</note>, the Madman, for **Layla** <note>original: "Leila"</note> is an Arabian desert legend that has been utilized endlessly by the romantic and mystical poets of the Orient. **Majnun’s** passion is shared by **Layla**, yet it remains unhappy, as the girl’s father refuses to give her to the youth who has gone mad from love. **Majnun** then lives among the beasts of the field and enchants them, just as he does the people who come near him, through his eloquent lamentations, until at last happiness seems to smile upon him and unites him with his beloved. But a new obstacle, more terrible than all previous ones, thwarts his hopes once more: **Layla** dies at the very moment when he could have called her his own, and soon... <note>The text cuts off here as it continues on the next page.</note>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the opening section of Rumi's Masnavi, specifically concluding the legend of Majnun before moving into the core philosophical verses regarding spiritual thirst and the vanity of material wealth.</meta>

Only the "fool" is familiar with this sense, <note>5</note>
Just as the tongue’s witness is only the ear.
In sorrow our days have flown away,
And with those days, hardships have followed!
And if the days depart, let them depart in peace,
O You, the Purest of the Pure, endure! <note>6</note>
Only the fish is never satiated by the flood, but long
Are the days of the needy, long and anxious. <note>7</note>
But let my word be brief; for the
Unrefined man does not understand what the well-tried man speaks.

Be free, my boy, <note>8</note> and break through the barrier,
Gold and silver have been your thoughts for too long!
For if you poured the sea into a jug, —
What would it hold? Barely enough for a day.
The eye of the greedy is never full; yet
Contentment bestows pearls upon the oyster. <note>9</note>

...thereupon **Majnun** <note>original: "Medschnun"</note> also breathes out his soul upon her grave. This is the thread of the narrative, adorned with many charming episodes, of a love that the people of the Orient <note>Eastern cultures</note> consider the true expression of the most inward passion. 

5. That is, the one who is elevated above the worries of ordinary people and therefore appears as a "fool" to them. 

6. This exclamation is addressed to **Sheikh Shams-ed-din Tabrizi**, the teacher of **Mawlana** <note>original: "Mewlānā"; meaning "Our Master," referring to Rumi</note>. "May you endure," or "May you never be absent," are common greeting formulas in the Orient. 

7. This verse is mystical. The formless flood, which resists all outward shaping but is clear within itself, represents "God-enthusiasm" <note>divine ecstasy or *ishq*</note>. In the face of this, humanity falls into three classes: some live entirely within it, like the fish in the sea, and never grow tired of it; these, forever thirsting for truth, are the most perfect human beings. Others are content merely to have tasted that flood—this is the second class. The third and lowest class has no share in true knowledge; their days pass slowly in earthly anxieties and fears. 

8. According to the view of the **Mevlevi Dervishes** <note>The Sufi order founded by Rumi's followers</note>, the person addressed here is **Husam-ed-din** <note>Husam al-Din Chelebi, Rumi's companion and scribe</note>, who, after the delivery of the first eighteen verses, asked for further enlightenment. 

9. Based on the belief that the oyster only succeeds [in producing] pearls... <note>The text cuts off here, referring to the folk-belief that oysters produce pearls from raindrops.</note>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the opening verses of Rumi’s Masnavi, specifically the metaphors of spiritual transformation and the nature of divine love.</meta>

Whoever has their garment torn by Love, <gloss>10</gloss>
Is free from greed and every shame.
O Love, you my sweet sorrow, hail to you,
The balm for my ailments, forever and ever!
You heal my soul of shame and arrogance, <gloss>11</gloss>
O you, the most beautiful blossom of the ancient sages! <gloss>12</gloss>
Love caused the body of dust <gloss>13</gloss> to soar toward heaven,
The mountain <gloss>14</gloss> shattered, which Love caused to tremble;

...to produce <note>referring to the pearl mentioned on the previous page</note>, if it <note>the oyster</note> closes itself against the abundance of the sea water and receives only a single raindrop.

10. This refers to the love of the creature for the Creator. The "garment" that Love is meant to tear apart is selfishness and self-will, through which individuality expresses itself. Mystical poets always use expressions originally meant for earthly love and friendship to describe divine love.

11. These are the two impulses of selfishness; these were what moved Iblīs <note>Satan</note> to the rebelliousness that proved fatal to himself when God commanded him to prostrate himself before man.

12. In the original text: “You, our Plato and Galen!” Eastern scholars divide the philosophers of antiquity into two classes: the *Ishraqiyye* <note>Illuminationists</note> and the *Mashshaiyye* <note>Peripatetics or Aristotelians</note>. The former drew their wisdom from abstraction, the purification of the inner self, and the asceticism of the outer self; the latter did not succeed in this, which is why they devoted themselves to experimentation and study. The leader of the Illuminationists is Plato <note>original: "Iflātūn"</note> and that of the Peripatetics is Galen <note>original: "Dschālīnūs"</note>. The comparison is meant to say that love leads to true knowledge no less than the most celebrated teachers of worldly wisdom.

13. Divine love was strongest in Idris and 'Isa <note>Enoch and Jesus</note>, whose earthly bodies it carried up to heaven.

14. The Quran (Surah 7, 139) <note>Modern editions usually number this as Surah 7:143</note> tells the story of God's revelation to Moses as follows: *And when Moses came at the time We (God is the speaker) had appointed for him, and his Lord (God) spoke to him, he said: "My Lord, show Yourself to me, that I may look upon You!" He (God) said: "You shall not see Me, but look at the mountain; if it remains firm in its place, then you shall see Me." And when God revealed Himself to the mountain, He turned it to dust, and Moses fell down unconscious.* Compare Exodus 33:18 ff. The mystical poets give this passage a meaning aligned with their own concepts—

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues the translation and commentary of the opening section of Rumi's Masnavi, moving from the metaphor of the reed flute to the mystical union and the story of the King and the Handmaiden.</meta>

Oh Friend, Horeb has become drunk with love,
And Moses has fallen down in a faint! —
If only I were joined mouth to mouth
With the one I love — oh, how much I would reveal.
But even one who knows a hundred songs is silent
If he is separated from the one who understands him.
When spring has fled, when the time of roses is gone,
You no longer hear the nightingales wooing. <gloss>15</gloss>
Love weaves two beings into one thing;
The lover is dead, only the Beloved lives. <gloss>16</gloss>
Like a bird that has lost its wings,
Is the one to whom love is foreign—woe to that fool!
How could I care for what is before or behind me,
If the Friend’s <gloss>17</gloss> all-radiant light were hidden from me? —
Love speaks this word, for Love is like a mirror;
Is a mirror of use if it is silent about flaws?
The soul is a mirror covered in rust
If love does not proclaim its faults!

### Tale.

Hearken to this parable, friend, which I tell,
For it corresponds to the state of my soul.
In ancient times, there once lived a king,

<note>This is an allegorical interpretation. Moses longs to see God’s face, just as one longs for the sight of the object of one’s love, and the mountain—here called Mount Tur or Horeb, and according to some commentators (see the scholar Al-Baydawi on this Quranic passage) it was Mount Tebir—trembles, intoxicated with love, as God reveals Himself before it, until it falls to pieces. The final words of the Quranic passage <note>original: "And Moses fell down unconscious" from Surah Al-A'raf 7:143</note> are inserted literally into the original Persian text. 15. An allusion to the beautiful poetic tradition of the nightingale’s love for the rose. [At the same time, these words contain a reference to the Platonic doctrine regarding the "non-being" of the world of appearances and the sole true existence of the transcendental.] 16. This occurs because the love of the lover kills their own self-will and allows it to be absorbed into the will of the Beloved. 17. Referring to God.</note>

<meta>This page continues the narrative introduced on page 59, moving from the philosophical introduction of the Masnavi to its first major illustrative parable: The King and the Handmaiden.</meta>

Both world and faith were subject to him. <gloss>18</gloss>
And this king once mounted his horse,
To go on a hunt with his entourage.
He traveled through mountain and valley to catch game,
—But the nets of love themselves ensnared him.
He met a maiden on the open highway,
—The king's heart became this maiden's slave.
His heart raged in his breast, a little bird
In a cage — he gives gold — the maid is his.
How overjoyed he is after the purchase!
But alas, he found her sick, the beautiful maid.
The donkey's master, he must go without the saddle;
He finds it only when the wolf has torn his beast.
He has the jug, but finds no water;
And when he meets water, his jug breaks. <note>These two metaphors illustrate the tragedy of "ill-timing": acquiring the solution only after the problem has become terminal, or having the need and the resource but never at the same time.</note>

The king sent for many physicians;
"Two lives," he said, "are in your hands.
My life is nothing! She is the life of my soul!
Only her health can give healing to me, the sick one.
Who will heal her, my soul, my beloved?
I shall make him rich with pearls and with gold!"
They said: "Our heads <gloss>19</gloss> are at stake!
Combined, our art shall surely lead us to the goal;
For we are indeed a Messiah <gloss>20</gloss> upon the earth,
We know the remedy for every complaint." —
Out of pride, not one added: "With God's blessing!" <note>In the original Persian context, this refers to the phrase "Inshallah" (If God wills). The physicians' failure to acknowledge divine providence is the "hamartia" or fatal flaw that leads to their upcoming failure.</note>

---

<margin>18. A ruler who is simultaneously a spiritual and worldly leader, such as the Caliphs <note>The Caliphs were the successors of Muhammad, holding both political and religious authority over the Islamic empire.</note> were.</margin>
<margin>19. That is, we pledge the healing with our lives.</margin>
<margin>20. Messiah (<note>original: "Messih"</note>), the Anointed One, is also a title for Jesus among Muslims, after whom Christians are also called "Messihites." To Islam, which has embraced the miraculous elements of the Holy Scriptures with great fondness, Jesus is considered, among other things, the wisest of physicians, whose breath conquers all illness and even death itself; compared to him, Galen <note>Galenus of Pergamon (129–c. 216 AD) was a Greek physician whose medical theories dominated both Western and Middle Eastern medicine for over a millennium.</note> is merely like an ordinary man compared to the Son of God.</margin>

<vocab>King, Hunt, Love, Slave, Physicians, Messiah, Caliphs, Messihites, Islam, Jesus, Galen</vocab>

<summary>The King falls in love with a handmaiden who immediately falls ill, prompting him to hire physicians who arrogantly claim they can cure any ailment. The story serves as a lesson on the limitations of human skill and the necessity of divine grace, contrasting worldly medicine with the miraculous healing power attributed to Jesus.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative from page 60. Having promised a cure through their own skill alone, the court physicians find their treatments backfiring, leading the King to seek divine intervention.</meta>

Therefore, God soon revealed their helplessness.
I speak here of their rigid minds,
For from the mere word <gloss>21</gloss> comes no profit.
That word—many do not carry it on their lips,
Though it lives in the depths of their hearts. —
Upon every healing draught, the illness increases,
All prayers remain unheard.
The sick maiden becomes a hair, a shadow,
The King's eye becomes a stream of tears from grief.
This inner fire cannot be conquered
By oxymel <term>Oxymel: From the Latin *oxymeli*, a medicinal syrup made of honey and vinegar, used since antiquity to balance the body's "humors."</term> or almond oil;
Through solvents <gloss>laxatives</gloss>, the blockage only grows —
The water, like pitch, only feeds the fire.

When he now recognized the physicians' impotence,
The King ran barefoot to the temple;
Before the prayer niche <gloss>22</gloss> he sat himself down,
With hot tears he wetted the floor,
Until he pulled himself up from his storm of grief
And gently opened his mouth in praise of God:
"O You, to whom the kingdom of the world is but a small gift, <gloss>23</gloss>
What more shall I say? You already know what I lack."

---

21. As is well known, people of the East are very strict in observing the custom of adding the words: *in shā' Allāh* <note>original: "in schā'llāh" — Arabic for "If God wills."</note>, to every mention of something future or intended. They call this *istishnā-i-mutlak*, the absolute exception. Our Sheikh <note>The author, Rumi.</note> rails against this outward formality in cases where the "exception" is not grounded in the unconditional surrender of the heart to the will of God. 
22. Prayer niche, *Mihrāb*, a niche—usually richly decorated with gilding, arabesques, and inscriptions—in the center of the side of mosques facing Mecca, which indicates the direction to be maintained during prayer (*Qibla*). It corresponds roughly to the *Synthronos* <note>original: "Synthronos" — a semicircular stone bench for the clergy in the apse of early Christian and Byzantine churches.</note> of the Greek churches, which is also generally used for this purpose where such churches have been converted into mosques. 
23. Allusion to the passage in the Quran (Surah 4, verse 77): Speak (God commands Muhammad): "The possessions of this (earthly) world are small."

You, the eternal asylum of my desires,

The **Ocean of Grace** <note>original: "26. Die göttliche Gnade wird als ein Ozean dargestellt..." — Divine grace is compared to an ocean for its infinity; it is said to "well up" when it reaches human consciousness.</note> welled up.
Sleep put an end to his weeping,
And in a dream, he saw an old man appear,
Who said: "Hail, Lord, your plea has been accepted.
A stranger will come to you tomorrow;

He is a wise physician, skillful and faithful.
His remedies will seem magical to you,

    And when the time came, and it dawned,
And the sun's brilliance chased away the host of stars,

To see if what the dream promised would come to pass.
And in the distance he saw a man, serious and simple,
Like the sun breaking through the shadows,
Barely visible, like the crescent moon before night—
A nothingness that *imagination* <term>Phantasie: Here referring to the Sufi concept of *Khayal*, the faculty that gives form to spiritual realities.</term> turns into being.
For imagination is as nothing in the spirit,
Yet within imagination, the universe orbits;
Peace and war are constructs of imagination,
And honor and shame spring from imagination;
Even the joys of Eden reflect from it,
Where it entwines the minds of the holy and pure. <note>original: "27. Die muhammedanischen Philosophen setzen zwischen die..." — Note cut off: Likely referring to how Muslim philosophers place imagination between the physical and spiritual worlds.</note>

# 63

It stood there now, realized in the form of his guest.
He hurried through the palace to the gate
To meet the mysterious visitor,
And both felt an immediate recognition—
Stranger souls, yet kindred to one another.
The King <note>28. The King.</note> spoke: "It was you, and not the maiden, who was my true aim;
Yet in life, one thing always leads to another.
I gird myself for your service, O Master;
To me you are as Ahmed, and I your most faithful follower." <note>29. Literally: "You are to me like Ahmed, and I to you like Omar." Ahmed, like Mahmud, is a name for Muhammad—specifically the name by which he is known in heaven, while the upper world knows him as Muhammad and the lower world as Mahmud. Omar Ibn Khattab is well known as one of Muhammad's most zealous and faithful companions. The King's submission to his guest prompts the Sheikh [the poet, Rumi] to begin the following digression on obedience to the Divine and to spiritual greatness—a theme he returns to often.</note>

We pray to the Lord for spiritual discipline!
For whoever lacks it is far from God’s grace.
He who lacks discipline does not harm himself alone;
No, he sets the whole world ablaze.
Once, a table <note>30. The poet supports the previous claim with stories from antiquity, beginning with the well-known story from Exodus regarding the Israelites' dissatisfaction in the desert with their uniform food. The Quran refers to this in various places.</note> descended from high heaven,

...between the material world of bodies and the abstract world of spirits, there is a third world—neither as subtle as the latter nor as substantial as the former—which they call the **World of Images** <term>'ālem-i-miṯāl: The world of archetypes or (Platonic) ideas.</term>. This world is divisible like physical matter yet intangible like spirit; it reproduces all phenomena of the material world in a spiritual form, and those of the spiritual world in physical images. In this realm, the favorites of God—even while still human and bound to the Earth—can see the abstract bliss of the soul merging into the Godhead as if in a mirror.

### Page 67
#### 5*

The humors <gloss>40. The original text specifies black and yellow bile. Medieval medicine, following the Greco-Arabic tradition, believed health depended on the balance of four humors: blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile.</gloss> were not the cause of the illness—
For just as the fragrance of the wood <gloss>41. This refers to aromatic, resinous woods like aloe or oud, which were burned as incense.</gloss> is revealed in the smoke,
So her pain <gloss>42. In Persian poetic imagery, pain is often depicted as a fire, while sighs are the smoke or vapor that rises from that internal heat.</gloss> revealed her inner suffering to him—
Her body healthy, yet her heart sick and sore.
Indeed, heavy sighs betray love; —
No suffering comes close to the suffering of love,
No other patient resembles the love-sick:
Love directs the thoughts upward toward God;
Whether it be transitory or eternal, <gloss>43. This refers to the distinction between earthly love (the bridge) and Divine love (the destination). The poet alludes to the proverb: "The metaphorical is the bridge to the Real." Jāmī expresses a similar sentiment in *Joseph and Zuleika*, noting that even worldly love leads one toward the Divine.</gloss>
Love always makes the mind receptive to God.
Whatever I have ever said or fathomed about love,
I am ashamed of it as soon as love ignites me.
While the word is the great revealer,
Love without words is even clearer. <gloss>44. Silent love is considered the most profound in this tradition. In his work *The Rose Garden* (Gulistan), the poet Sa'di contrasts the noisy nightingale with the silent moth.</gloss>

40. In the original: the black and the yellow bile. As is well known, the Arabic physicians also held the theory of the four cardinal humors (the two mentioned, phlegm, and blood), upon whose mutual balance the well-being of the body rested, and from whose deficiency or excess illnesses were derived.
41. One should think here of resinous types of wood, such as aloe, which Orientals use for incense. 42. In the conception of the Oriental poets, pain is a fire, and its expression, the sigh, is a smoke or vapor rising from this fire—hence the comparison. 43. That is, whether it clings to something transitory and earthly or to the one, Unfading Being. The poet plays here on the Arabic proverb: "The image <note>original: "das Bild," referring to *majaz* or the metaphorical/phenomenal world.</note> is the bridge to the Essence <note>original: "Wesen," referring to *haqiqa* or Reality.</note>." Similarly, Jāmī says in the poem *Joseph and Zuleika*: "Do not turn your face away from love, even if it is the metaphorical <note>extra-divine</note> one; for this leads toward the essential love." 44. Silent love, which does not dare to speak, is considered by the Persian poets to be the most intimate. To the nightingale that talks so much of its passion, Sa'di calls out in the *Rose Garden*:

> "O nightingale, your sobbing and your song
> Shows that love you have not yet known!
> Learn from the moth, which without complaint
> Silently in the candle's glow was consumed! —"

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 67, where the poet discussed the silence of profound love and compared it to the moth’s sacrifice, the text now explores the inability of reason or even the "divine pen" of destiny to fully describe the nature of love.</meta>

Destiny's reed pen <gloss>45. See footnote 45 for the symbolism of the reed pen in Islamic thought.</gloss> moved forward without ceasing,
But when it reached love, it was split in two.
Reason here is like a donkey stuck in the mire—
Only love itself can grasp the nature of love. 46
The sun’s essence is revealed by the sun;
Look at the sun, if its nature is not clear to you. 47

The moth <note>The preceding text, now lost or on the previous page, likely introduced the moth metaphor.</note> that silently flutters around the light and finally seeks death within it is the sensory image of this love, regarding which Sa’di <note>A major Persian poet of the 13th century, famous for his works *The Rose Garden* and *The Orchard*.</note> says in another passage:

> "Whoever chose the path of love,
> Was indeed struck down by the Beloved!
> The dead bring forth no sound. —"

45. Muslims conceive of all human destiny as being recorded from the beginning of eternity by God upon a tablet <term>louh; original: *lauh al-mahfuz*, the Preserved Tablet</term>, over which they believe the reed pen, the *Kalem*, <term>Kalem; from Arabic *qalam*, meaning pen</term> continues to work. This reed, which was capable of writing everything else, shattered of its own accord when it came to the subject of love, out of terror before such a sublime matter. According to an Arabic myth cited by the commentator on this passage, the *Kalem* was the first thing God created. "To it," the story goes, "God commanded: 'Write!' and it wrote the past and the future. Then God spoke: 'Write: There is no god but God,' and it wrote it. Then God spoke: 'Write: Muhammad is the Messenger of God'; but it was unable to write it. — For Muhammad is regarded as the bearer of divine love." Exaggerations of this kind are not uncommon among Oriental poets; for instance, Hafez <note>A 14th-century Persian lyric poet.</note> depicts Zohre <term>Zohre; the planet Venus, associated with music</term> as the lute-player of heaven singing his own Ghazals <term>Ghaselen; a form of rhyming lyric poetry</term> and allows the solemn Messiah <note>Jesus</note> to perform a dance to this music. — Regarding the thought itself, I should also note that the reed used by people in the East for writing is brittle enough to shatter easily during any spiritual agitation affecting the writing hand.

46. This means that reason is unable to comprehend or explain love; for to become conscious of it, one must feel it. The same thought is expressed by a Turkish poet of more recent times, Fehim Efendi, as follows: "In the valley of love, reason goes astray, even if it be a Ptolemy <term>Batlamiōs; the Hellenistic astronomer and philosopher</term> in matters of philosophy."

47. The comparison of love and the sun is all the more natural to a Persian because in his language, a single word — *mihr* — encompasses both concepts <note>original: "mihr" (Persian: مهر), which means both "sun" and "affection/love/mercy."</note>.

<summary>This page explains the limitations of human reason and the celestial "Pen of Destiny" when faced with the sublime nature of love, using metaphors of the sun and the shattered reed pen. It draws on Persian and Turkish poetic traditions to illustrate that love can only be understood through experience, not intellect.</summary>

<keywords>mysticism, Sufi poetry, Sa'di, Hafez, Pen and Tablet, symbolism of love, Reason vs. Emotion, Fehim Efendi, Persian linguistics</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from page 67, where the poet discussed the limits of reason and the "shattering" of the divine pen when attempting to describe love, this page transitions from the metaphorical "sun" of love to the historical person of Shams al-Din Tabrizi, whose name literally means "Sun of the Faith."</meta>

Even the shadow gives a report of her—
And so love always fills the spirit with light. <gloss>48</gloss>
No being equals the sun in majesty—
Love, the sun of the soul, never sets.
Only the sun illuminates the physical world,
Yet its likeness may well be painted by an artist;
The soul's sun leaves no external trace,
Nothing in the spirit, nothing in nature equals it.
Has imagination ever been able to grasp love,
Or been able to fit an image to it?

Since this discourse thinks of the Sun of Faith, <gloss>49</gloss>
The sun of the fourth heaven <gloss>50</gloss> lowers its face.
And since Tabrizi's name has appeared here,
I should speak here of that pure, pious man.
Wafted by the scent of Joseph’s garment, <gloss>51</gloss>

...united. So it is also said in *Joseph and Zuleikha* <note>A famous Persian epic poem by Jami, detailing the spiritual and romantic longing between Joseph and the wife of Potiphar.</note> (Rosenzweig, p. 80):
> “When she heard of Joseph and his beauty, the sun (love) caught fire upon the moon of her countenance.”
Our poet avoids this word <note>likely referring to the common Persian word for sun, *khurshid*</note> here and chooses instead, in both meanings, the Arabic name for the sun, *Shams* <term>Shams; Arabic for "sun"</term>, to prepare for the praise that follows for his teacher and friend, Sheikh Tabrizi, who bore the title *Shams al-Din* (Sun of Faith). 

48. Just as the shadow allows one to sense the sun, so the light filling the spirit allows one to sense love. 
49. Referring to Sheikh Tabrizi, who held this honorary title. 
50. As is well known, Muslims conceive of seven heavens layered one above the other. The fourth of these is the sphere of our sun. 
51. This is a metaphorical description for the premonition of a high joy or spiritual ecstasy. Upon the return of Jacob’s sons from their last journey to Egypt, the Quran <note>The central religious text of Islam.</note> has Joseph speak thus to his brothers, to whom he has finally revealed himself (Surah 12, 93):
> “Go forth with this shirt of mine and cast it upon my father's face, so that he may see again; and come to me with all your family.”
Then it continues: But when the caravan had departed (from Egypt), their father (who had remained in Canaan and was blind) spoke: “Truly, I perceive the scent of Joseph—if only you [did not think me senile].”

<summary>The poet uses the sun as a metaphor for the inextinguishable light of divine love, using wordplay to transition into a tribute to his mentor, Shams al-Din Tabrizi. The text explains the symbolism of Joseph's scent as a metaphor for spiritual intuition and the arrival of a beloved teacher.</summary>

Hussām <gloss>52</gloss> also takes hold of my garment and pleads:
“By this friendship that has united us for years,
Speak to me of the much-praised Friend,
Oh speak, so that the whole universe may laugh,
And my eye and spirit may grow a hundredfold!”
— “Do not press me! My strength fails,
My spirit falters; I dare not attempt his praise.
You might well bring a sick man to speak,
But he would never succeed in speaking well.
In my veins glows a wild ecstasy;
Can the praise of the Friend, the Peerless One, succeed for me?”

...did not call me childish.” <note>This fragment completes a sentence from the previous page, likely referencing Quran 12:94 where Jacob says he senses Joseph's scent "if you did not think me senile."</note> They said: “By God, there you are again in your old delusion.” — Sadi <note>Saadi Shirazi (c. 1210–1292), one of the greatest Persian poets, author of the *Gulistan* or *Rose Garden*.</note> also used this Quranic passage for a poem in the *Rose Garden*, where he contrasts this state of spiritual vision with the state of human weakness, due to which Jacob did not notice the crime committed by his sons <note>The brothers of Joseph who threw him into a well.</note> even in his proximity.

> “When Jacob found his son, who once had vanished,
> Finally after a long time of separation,

> You had recognized the scent of his shirt
> While he tarried far away in the land of Egypt. —

> If the same light always shone for the Dervish, <term>Dervish; a member of a Muslim (specifically Sufi) religious order</term>

### 71

"Let it be! Of my longing and my grief, <gloss>53</gloss>
I shall speak to you at another time."
He said: "Give what my parched heart desires,
And quickly! Time is like a sharp sword. <gloss>54</gloss>
As a <term>Sufi<note>original: *sūf*; from the Arabic word for wool, referring to the simple woolen garments worn by early Muslim mystics who lived lives of asceticism and contemplation.</note></term> 55 you have become a 'son of the time,'
Delay is not permitted in our order.
Are you not of the order of self-renunciation?
Existence turns to non-existence through postponement."
I said: "Friend, you must listen to my parable,
For it veils the Sheikh’s secret teachings,
Just as a poem, veiling the truth,
Speaks of the mysteries of love."
He said: "Give me the truth, pure and unveiled,
Master, do not deny me total clarity!
Give it to me naked and lift the veil entirely—
I will not celebrate the wedding night in clothes!"
I said: "If your eye were to see it naked,
You, friend, would perish into nothingness before it; <gloss>56</gloss>
The wish you harbor is excessive—
A straw can never carry the mountain's weight!
If the sun, which illuminates the universe,
Did not stay far away, the whole world would burn."

***

53. Regarding the general relationship between <term>Mawlānā<note>A title meaning "Our Master," referring here to the poet Rumi.</note></term> and <term>Tabrizi<note>Shams al-Din Tabrizi, Rumi's spiritual mentor and the "Friend" mentioned in the text.</note></term>, I refer to the biography attached to the introduction.
54. That is, time destroys mercilessly and therefore demands wise and immediate utilization.
55. From the Arabic *sūf*, wool; thus, strictly speaking, one who dresses in wool—that is, a sage living in asceticism and contemplation of the Deity. He is called a <term>"son of the time"<note>original: *Ibn al-waqt*; a Sufi concept of living entirely in the present moment, being perfectly responsive to God's will without being distracted by memories of the past or anxieties about the future.</note></term> here because, unconcerned with the past and the future, and as mindless as a child in a father's arms, he should live for the inspirations of the moment that come from God.
56. For this teaching is that of absolute unity <note>A reference to *Wahdat al-Wujud*, the "Unity of Being," where the individual self is seen as a mere shadow of the One Reality.</note>, in which not only the individuality of the spirit but also of life itself is meant to dissolve. However, that such doctrines can be exploited to the world's detriment is proven by the sect of the <term>Assassins<note>The Nizari Isma'ili state, known for their devotion to a grandmaster; the author suggests their radicalism was a perversion of these mystical ideas.</note></term> who flourished shortly before Mawlānā.

<summary>In this passage from the Masnavi, Rumi’s companion demands a direct explanation of divine Truth, but Rumi warns that the human soul is often too fragile to face the "naked" light of God without the protection of metaphors. He introduces the Sufi concept of being a "son of the moment," emphasizing that spiritual seekers must act immediately on divine inspiration.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from page 71, where the poet was discussing the overwhelming nature of the "Sun" (Shams al-Din Tabrizi), he now pivots back to the framing narrative of the King and the sick handmaiden. The spiritual guide, appearing here as a physician, begins his diagnostic process.</meta>

"Your desire leads to strife and battle and murder,
Therefore, speak to me of <term>Tabrizi<note>original: *Tebrisi*; referring to Shams al-Din Tabrizi, Rumi's spiritual mentor. The "strife and murder" refers to the overwhelming, ego-destroying power of mystical love.</note></term> no more!"
This subject is endless; therefore, to hear of the sick girl
Once again, gather your thoughts.

The physician spoke: "Great <term>Shah<note>A Persian title for a king or monarch.</note></term>! Leave the hall
Together with all your courtiers.
Let no one listen in the corridors,
So that the maiden may lend me a willing ear."
When the crowd had been driven out,
And the physician remained alone with the maiden,
He asked her gently: "From where do you come? Speak!
For my cure, this is necessary. <gloss>57</gloss>
Tell me, did you leave relatives behind at home?
Did you leave friends and acquaintances there?"
Feeling her pulse, he thus questioned the maiden
About her misfortune and her heart's grief.

Whoever feels a thorn in their foot, they pull
The foot to their knee to see what presses them there,
And probes the wound with a sharp needle,
And, if they find nothing, wets it with their mouth;
It is already so difficult to find a thorn in the foot—
How much more so to fathom the one in the heart!
If the fool saw the heart's thorn so quickly,
Where would the omnipotence of sorrow remain? —
What use is it if one gives thorns to a donkey 
To carry on its tail? It only jumps and kicks.
It leaps, and the thorn only penetrates deeper,
For only reason can free it from the pain.
It wishes to rid itself of its torment—
It kicks out and becomes bloodied everywhere.

The stranger was a master of his craft—
He probed around, making everything clear to himself,
And through conversation, he lured from the maiden’s mouth
The news of her kinsfolk.
Without restraint, she now spoke of her homeland,
Of its inhabitants and its noble class;
To all of this, the old man listened intently,
While simultaneously watching her pulse without distraction,
To see if a name might set her blood into a localized surge,
Should her speech happen upon her beloved.
She named acquaintances from her native city
And then spoke of another city.
The physician asked: “When you left your home,
Where were you first taken in?”
She named the place, and this did not move her;
Her pulse remained steady, as did her countenance.
She spoke of lords and citizens as the topics arose,
Of their houses, their salt and bread. <gloss>58. A proverbial expression for one’s way of life and hospitality.</gloss>

Her pulse did not twitch, nor did her cheek turn pale.
The blood flowed gently through her veins—
Until he spoke of blissful Samarkand. <gloss>59. The ancient capital of the land of <term>Mā-werā-en-nahr<note>Also known as Transoxiana, the region "beyond the river" Oxus in Central Asia.</note></term>, one of the most famous inland cities of the Orient. One can imagine how the splendor of Samarkand—which may be described as one of the starting points of Islamic culture—under the rule of the powerful <term>Muhammad Khwarazm-Shah<note>The ruler of the Khwarazmian Empire (reigned 1200–1220) shortly before the Mongol invasions.</note></term>, occupied the imagination of the boy <note>Referring to the poet Rumi.</note> living in nearby <term>Balkh<note>An ancient center of Buddhism and later Islam in present-day Afghanistan; Rumi's birthplace.</note></term>. Water flowed from her eyes like a stream.

In Samarkand, a goldsmith purchased me.
He held me in his arms for six months,
And then he sold me without mercy.”

“Let your heart be like a grave for your secret,
And your wish shall be fulfilled without delay.
Into the silent earth one sows the seed,
And behold, the garden’s beauty sprouts from it.
And would gold and silver ever grow in the mine
If they did not sleep in the hidden night?” —
These promises, this kindness,
Removed every fear from the sick maiden.
Truthful promises win the heart,
While lying ones shatter all peace;
The word of an honorable man is a treasure that always flows, 64
But the word of the base is soul-affliction.

Then the physician went to find the King,
And briefly told him how the matter stood.
He said: “The best way to heal the girl
Would be if that goldsmith were to come here without delay.
Send a messenger who, with such grace,
Might stir his desire and invite him to you;
Summon him here from that distant land,
And make him proud with gold and robes of state,
So that through him the maiden’s heart may heal,
And this difficulty may be resolved through him.”

The King, when he heard this from the physician,
Agreed entirely with the advice.
He sent two messengers out to Samarkand,
Two princes of his court, wise and clever;

They set out, and to the goldsmith’s door
The <term>Emirs</term> <note>Arabic/Persian title for commanders or princes</note> arrived with their message:
“You, O most skillful master, whose fame has rung out

65. A sum of money through which one buys oneself out of blood-guilt according to the law of retaliation (<term>jus talionis</term> <note>Latin legal term for "the law of the claw," or "an eye for an eye"</note>). Koran 5, 49: “We have prescribed for them therein (in the <term>Torah</term> <note>original: "Thora"</note>) that they shall take soul (life) for soul, eye for eye, nose for nose, ear for ear, tooth for tooth, and that they shall practice retaliation for wounds.” Yet, according to Muhammadan law, all these offenses can be converted into a monetary debt. 66. The Angel of Death. Oriental poets portray him mocking humans and their far-reaching plans, coming to claim their souls when they least expect it. 67. The King saw him and greeted him graciously,
And placed all his gold into his hands. —
Then the old man <note>the wise physician</note> spoke to the <term>Shah</term> <note>Persian for "King"</note>: “Grant the maiden
To the stranger, Sire, so that she may belong to him!
Only when united with him will she grow strong, and the glow
Of love be extinguished in the flood of fulfillment.”
The Shah gave the moon-faced one <gloss>68</gloss> to him,
Thus uniting two longing hearts.
For six months they lived in pleasure and joy,
Until the maiden had fully recovered from her suffering.
Then the physician mixed a potion for the goldsmith,
Through which he collapsed to the ground before the maiden;
And he grew ill, and his beauty vanished,
And so the magic that had bound the maiden ebbed away.
For as he became pale and ugly and weak,
The maiden’s love grew cold little by little.
That which arises from beauty alone is not love;
Such love is brought to shame in the end.
Oh, had he never possessed such charm,
Never would such misfortune have been his lot!
Streams of blood <gloss>69</gloss> he wept in his distress, —
His own countenance had brought him his death.
Just so, the peacock's enemy is its beautiful plumage,
And many a king has been cast down by his own splendor.
He spoke: “I am like the poor deer <gloss>70</gloss>, slain

---

the court of the king. 68. The expression "moon-face," so frequently used by Oriental writers, does not refer to a particular roundness, but simply signifies a face that is distinguished by beauty among its peers, just as the moon stands out among the stars. 69. That is, tears of blood. 70. The musk deer. People of the East are extraordinarily fond of the scent of musk, and its trade among them is so significant that merchants who generally deal in fragrant substances, such as rose oil and aloe wood, call themselves musk-sellers after that most principal article. Musk comes to Persia...

<meta>Continuity: Page 78 concludes the dying goldsmith’s lament from the previous page, where he compared his fatal beauty to that of a musk deer.
