By the hunter for the musk it carries;
I am like the ermine, slain
In the desolate heath <gloss>71</gloss> because of its pelt’s splendor;
Like the elephant, whose blood was shed
By the hunter's murderous projectile for its tusk.
He who took my life for a lesser good, <gloss>72</gloss>
Did not consider that my blood never sleeps. <gloss>73</gloss>
If it is my turn today, tomorrow it is his, the fool,
Who does not know that my blood is never truly lost. <gloss>74</gloss>
However far the shadow of a wall may fall,
The time will eventually come when it draws near to it. <gloss>75</gloss>
The world is like a mountain, <gloss>76</gloss> the deed like the sound,

80. Khidr <note>original: "Chydhyr"</note> (Al-Khidr) is a mythical figure who transitioned into Islamic tradition from the nature-worship of pre-Islamic Arabs, who likely honored under this name the spirit of eternally rejuvenating nature; Oriental writers themselves do not have a uniform conception of him. Khidr is depicted in blossoming, imperishable beauty as a youth who nevertheless combines the ornament of age—the white beard—with his other charms. His name, which is root-related to the Arabic word *akhdar* <note>meaning "green"</note>, gave rise to the further legend that wherever he sets his foot, green meadows form beneath his steps. At the same time, Khidr is the guardian of the Water of Life <note>the *Ab-e-Hayat* in Persian lore</note>, from which he drew eternal youth, while Alexander <note>Alexander the Great, often identified as Iskandar in the East</note> traversed the world in vain to find the spring. His omniscience is surpassed only by the divine; yet not all scholars grant him the rank of a prophet, as the Quran does not [explicitly] name him. However, for the story alluded to here, I refer the reader to the Appendix. 81. That is, as Ishmael offered his neck to the sacrificial knife of his father Abraham. Islamic scholars are not in agreement as to which of his two sons Abraham intended to sacrifice. Agreeing with the account attested by the Pentateuch <note>the first five books of the Bible, which specify Isaac as the intended sacrifice</note> (*Rauzat-es-Safa* <note>The "Garden of Purity," a famous history of the world by the 15th-century Persian historian Mirkhwand</note>, p. 179) are, among others, Omar ben Khattab, Ali ben Abi Talib, and Said ben Jubayr; while the opposing view—that this honor was granted to the ancestor of the Arabs <note>Ishmael</note>—is represented by Abdallah ben Abbas, Abu Huraira, Abdallah ben Omar, Abu Tofail, etc.

to fall into the hands of the hypocrite. 115. <note>Abu Musaylima was the most dangerous among Muhammad's opponents who sought to fight him with his own weapons by also claiming to be messengers of God. He was nearly as talented an orator as Muhammad and had an almost equal number of followers who were devoted to him with fanatical dedication. It is said he wished to approach Muhammad and wrote him the following letter: "Musaylima, the Messenger of God, to Muhammad, the Messenger of God. Let half the earth be mine, and the other half yours"; to which Muhammad replied: "Muhammad, the Messenger of God, to Musaylima, the Liar. The earth belongs to God, who gives it as an inheritance to whichever of His servants He wills; but the end is for the God-fearing." — Muhammad defeated Musaylima without being able to master his party, and his successors had great difficulty in destroying him. He was finally killed by Wahshi, an Arab who had previously slain Hamza, Muhammad's uncle, at the Battle of Badr and could therefore boast: "I killed the best of men as a pagan and the worst as a believer." The Muslims primarily call him *al-Kadhdhab* <note>original: "el Kezzāb"</note>, the Liar, and therefore he is mentioned here as the model of all heretics.</note> 116. <note>A figurative designation for a religious doctrine not grounded in truth, with an allusion to the Islamic prohibition of drinking wine.</note> 117. <note>The narrative that follows serves as evidence for what has been said about heretics and false prophets. The king knows how to gain their trust to such an extent that they grant him the

still specific dogmas into each tribe and to initiate a
war of all against all among them. — **Mewlānā** <note>Literal: "Our Master," referring to the poet Rumi.</note> could easily have de-
rived the thought for this story from the fragmented state of the Chris-

which persecuted each other with such hatred at all times
that they, one after another, became easy prey for the
common enemy. The poet’s fiction attributes this hatred and the
doctrinal differences that justify it to the cunning of a
fanatical Jew who sacrificed his own life to harm his ene-

but is entirely borrowed from the philosophical-religious views of the en-
vironment in which our Sheikh lived. 118. <note>God has sent down His own revelation to every age, the bearers of which were the various prophets. Through them, the measure of divine knowledge intended for humanity was revealed: Adam received the first, after him Enoch (<term>Idrīs</term>), Abraham, Moses, Jesus, etc., received others, and finally Muhammad received the last of the revelations. Each epoch is named after the prophet who illuminates it, and thus the first centuries of our era until the appearance of Muhammad are the Age of Jesus. For this time, only the Christians are believers, just as the Jews were during the epoch of Moses and the Muslims <note>original: "Islamiten"</note> since Muhammad.</note> 119. <note>The revelation of all prophets is one and the same; for it stems from the One, and its bearers are the representatives of this One. Their difference, therefore, rests only on the form, not on the spirit, which is a reflection of the divine unity. Thus it says in the Quran (Surah 2, 130 <note>Modern verse 136</note>): Speak (to those who urge you to become Jews or Christians): "We believe in God and that which has been sent down to us, and what was sent down to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and the tribes, and what was bestowed upon Moses and Jesus, and what was bestowed upon the prophets from their Lord. Yet the path of both, the single, correct one,
Was severed by the Shah, the double-sighted one. 120

### Parable. 121

When to fetch the bottle from the house for him
His master commanded the double-sighted servant, 122
The servant asked: "Tell me, which of the two
Do you desire? Please inform me exactly!"
The master said: "It is one bottle, not two;
So do not see double; bring it here to me."
The servant said: "Master, you rebuke me unjustly!"
The master said: "Then break one of them, servant!"
And he smashed it, and both vanished—
The human eye grows dim in hatred and envy.
It was only one, which he saw as two,
And when it broke, no second one was there.

Anger and greed take from us the clarity of the eye
And rob the mind of straightness and truth.

<note>...strength is cited as an example.</note> 120. I translate the Arabic word <term>ahwel</term> <note>Usually translated as squinting or cross-eyed, from the Arabic *aḥwal*.</note> in this way, which is usually rendered as "squinting." This expression denotes the morbid condition of the eyes where they see the same object twice. Whether this ailment occurs to such a high degree in Persia that the sufferer truly believes they see two different objects before them, I do not venture to decide; what is certain is that a good number of stories in Persian anecdote collections refer to it. — The meaning is therefore: The King considered the teaching of Moses to be different from that of the Messiah <note>Jesus</note>, while in essence it is the same faith. 121. This story, which is repeated several times by various Oriental writers, serves here actually only as a closer definition of the quality of "spiritual double-vision" attributed to the King. 122. The Bombay edition <note>A significant 19th-century lithograph edition of Rumi's *Mathnawi*.</note> inserts the following verse here:
> Then the double-sighted man went into the house quickly,
> And saw two bottles in the single spot.
> He said to the Master: Say, etc.

To the <term>Sufi</term> <note>A practitioner of Islamic mysticism who seeks a direct, personal experience of God.</note>, who wishes to see union with God regarded as the sole aim of life, selfishness—the constant striving of man to indulge his individuality through self-interest—is the most difficult obstacle to overcome. Every selfish purpose stains the only true worship, i.e., the pure preoccupation with the Supreme Being, and nullifies its merit. 134. “To surpass the service” means: to undertake other voluntary religious exercises in addition to those prescribed by law. ->Alas, many a net is cast for us, and we—<-

<margin>
Muslims <note>original: "medaner", a truncated form of "Mohammedaner"</note> believe in the existence of the <term>Antichrist</term> <note>A false messianic figure who appears before the end of the world.</note>, whom they call *ad-Dajjal* <note>original: "ed-Deddschāl"</note> or *al-Masih ad-Dajjal* <note>original: "el-Messih ed-Deddschāl"</note> (The Liar, the False Messiah) and imagine as one-eyed. This same figure, posing as Christ and riding a donkey just as He did, wanders the earth to lead people astray, but is said to be fought and killed by Christ upon His return at the end of the world. 
137. It is hardly necessary to note here that by the "grain," one should understand the merit of pious works and the self-satisfaction that rewards them; by the "mouse," the whisperings of the demon of selfishness; and by the "storehouse" or "shrine" in which that merit is kept, the heart. 
138. Forty years of good works are useless if true devotion was lacking. 
139. The commentary explains this passage as follows: From the fire-steel (of obedience to God) many a spark falls on the heart—which has been burned (into tinder) by the glow of divine love—at which the heart seeks to ignite; but the thief (of satanic influ[ences])...
</margin>

The spirit 141 <note>141. Persian philosophy distinguishes between the animating part of the soul and the perceiving part. The former is called the <term>animal spirit</term> (original Persian: *rūh-i-haiwān*), or the life-spirit, and the latter the <term>wandering spirit</term> (original Persian: *rūh-i-rewān*). Only this wandering spirit is related to the Godhead; it dwells against its will, like a bird in a cage, within the earthly body which obstructs its movement. It leaves this prison during sleep to rise to God, free from worldly fears.</note> every night from the body's bounds
You set free, taking from it cares and thoughts.

Nor kings of their lands at night;
No one grieves for profit or loss,

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the discussion on the state of the soul during sleep, moving from the general concept of the spirit's nightly release to the specific Quranic and mystical allusion of the "Sleepers in the Cave" (the Seven Sleepers of Ephesus). It completes the sentence from the previous page: "Man is aware [of no state or being, nor does he have any] suspicion of his own nature."</meta>

...suspects of its nature. 145 <note>145. This is an allusion to the legend of the seven youths of Ephesus, which passed from Christianity into Islam. The legend tells of seven young men who, in the year 250 AD during the reign of Emperor Decius, fled into a cave and slept there for 184 years until the reign of Theodosius the Younger. The Quran recounts this story as follows (Surah 18, Verse 12): "They were youths who believed in their Lord, and we guided them even further. (13) And we strengthened their hearts when they stood up; therefore they said: 'Our Lord is the Lord of the heavens and the earth; never will we call upon a god besides Him: truly, we would then be speaking an exorbitant lie. (15) And (they spoke to one another) since you have withdrawn from them (the heathens) and that which they worship besides God, then flee to the cave; God will spread His mercy over you, and create for you a benefit from that which you suffer.' (16) And you could have seen how the sun, when it rose, inclined away from their cave to the right, and when it set, turned away from them to the left, while they lay in the open space of the cave. This was one of the wonders of God; whomsoever God guides, he is well-guided, but whomsoever He allows to go astray, for him you will never find a supporter or a guide. (17) You would have thought them awake, although they slept; but we turned them to the right and to the left. And their dog lay stretched out with its forelegs at the entrance. Had you come upon them, you would have turned from them in flight, and would have been filled with terror of them. (18) And likewise, we awakened them so they might question one another: — One of them spoke: 'How long have you spent (here)?' — They said: 'We have spent a day, or part of a day.' (Others) spoke: 'Your Lord knows best how long you have spent (here).' (21) They (those of later times) will say: 'They were three, and the fourth was their dog.' Others will say: 'Five, and the sixth was their dog,' guessing at the hidden. Others will say: 'They were seven, and the eighth was their dog.' Say: 'My Lord knows best their number; only a few (besides Him) know them.'" Compare the Appendix. The mystics give the narrative a symbolic secondary meaning, according to which the <term>Sleepers in the Cave</term> are understood to be the pious who behold God in their spiritual ecstasy. The words of the Quranic passage that are most important here (Verse 17) are explained there: You would think them awake,</note>

->Do not worry about the sleepers in the cave! 145<-
->Like the <term>reed-pen</term> <note>original: "Schicksalsschreibrohr." Refers to the "Qalam" or Divine Pen which, in Islamic theology, writes the decrees of God upon the "Preserved Tablet."</note> of destiny in God’s hand,<-

Who only snatches at shadows!—

<meta>Continuity: This page concludes the allegory of the hunter chasing a shadow, warning that life is wasted when one pursues worldly illusions. It transitions into the solution: finding a spiritual guide who acts as the "Shadow of God" on earth.</meta>

->But he remains free from shadow and from delusion,<-
->Whom God’s shadow 159 leads upon his path,<-
->The pious one, constant in God’s service,<-
->He, who is dead to the world and alive in God.<-
->O do not hesitate, hold fast to the Saint’s garment, 160<-
->That he may deliver you in the final hour! 161<-
->As the shadow of God, let him be a guide for you<-
->To the highest sunlight, to the All-Ruler! 162<-
->Wander only under the blinking of such a guiding star;<-

...the evil spirits of the ancient Parsi religion <note>Zoroastrianism</note>, which are thought of as the most hideous beings in creation. 159. **Shadow**, i.e., the image or reflection of God, is an expression among Eastern peoples for the highest thing that exists on earth. It is therefore a common title for **Sultans**, which serves to designate their loftiness over all other earthly inhabitants. In our passage, however, it is to be understood not as worldly but as spiritual greatness, and signifies a God-enlightened leader of faith. Among the religious-philosophical sects of the time in which our poet lived, there is almost none that did not put forth the principle that man, left to himself, necessarily goes astray, and that he must therefore choose a master to lead him on the right path. Mewlānā <note>A title for Rumi, meaning "Our Master."</note> demands the same here and names as a suitable <term>Weli <note>From the Arabic *Wali*, meaning "Friend of God" or Saint; someone who has attained spiritual proximity to the Divine.</note></term> (One near to God) his teacher **Shams al-Din Tabrizi**, whose name—**Sun of Faith**—gives him occasion for various wordplay in contrast to "God’s shadow." (Compare Note 48 and 49.) 160. Compare Note 52. 161. The **final hour** is the time preceding the Resurrection, in which, according to the view of Islamic scholars, many torments await even the believer if he has not found the right path. 162. An allusion to a passage in the **Quran** (25:45), in which God speaks to Muhammad:

> "Have you not turned your vision to your Lord?—how He lengthens the shadow! If He had willed, He could have made it stationary; then we do make the sun its guide; then we withdraw it to ourselves, a gradual withdrawal." <note>original: „Sahest du nicht auf deinen Herrn, wie er den Schatten dehnte? Hätte er gewollt, so würde er ihn bleibend gemacht haben. Dann machten wir die Sonne ihm zum Führer, und dann nahmen wir ihn zu uns zurück auf leichte Weise.“</note>

The mystics wish for "shadow" in these words to be understood as the material world, and for "sun," by contrast, to be understood as reason or intellect; they translate the expression "to lengthen" as "to bring forth," rendering it thus:

> "Did you not see how God created all beings? If He had willed, He would have let them remain in nothingness; but as it is, He gave them reason as a guide."

Speak with *Khalil* <note>original: *Chalil*. This is the title "Friend of God," the traditional epithet for the prophet Abraham</note>: 163 “I love not those that set.” 164

163. That is, the Friend of God, the usual epithet of *Abraham*, which he bears because it is attested of him alone among mortals that he entertained God as his guest and conversed with Him as with a man. 164. These words are borrowed from the passage in the Quran in which the story of *Abraham's* youth is told. (Remember the time, *Muhammad* is told by God in *Surah 6, 74*.) When *Abraham* said to his father *Azar*: “Do you take idols for gods? Verily, I see you and your people in manifest error.” — And thus we showed *Abraham* the kingdom of the heavens and the earth, and this so that he might be one of those who firmly believe. When the night covered him, he saw a star; then he said: “This is my Lord.” But when it set, he said: “I love not those that set.” When he then saw the moon rising, he said: “This is my Lord.” But when it set, he said: “Verily, if my Lord does not guide me, I shall be one of those who go astray.” When he then saw the sun rising, he said: “This is my Lord; this is greater.” But when it set, he said: “O my people, I have nothing to do with that which you associate with God (the idols); I turn my face to Him who created heaven and earth, as a true believer, and I am no idolater.” The connection of these worthless objects of worship to false teachers, whose wisdom does not stem from the Eternal, All-True One, is clear. 165. That is, if you place yourself on the same level as your spiritual guide and say of him, as *Iblis* <note>Satan</note> said of *Adam*: “I am better than he.” 166. Of *Iblis*, who, having himself succumbed to envy, forces man into his yoke by stirring up this passion. 167. Which consists only in obedience toward God.

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the discussion of envy as a spiritual obstacle. Envy dwells within the body of man as its house,
Therefore no man is ever spared from envy.
Yet though the body may be envy’s house,

can. 168. That is, man is originally pure of envy and therefore has the power and the duty to restore this purity by imitating the saints in fighting against self-will. 169. The cube-shaped building at the center of Islam's most important mosque in Mecca</note>, the Arabian national sanctuary in *Mecca*. But the mystics interpret the passage symbolically and understand by the "House of God" the heart of man, which is to be kept pure from desires, passions, and the promptings of the Devil, since according to a saying of *Muhammad*, the believer is more esteemed before God than the *Kaaba*. 170. Just as a talisman made of poor material carries inscriptions of a salvation-bringing meaning, so the earthly body of man hides within itself the God-related soul. 171. Symbolic of the perceptive powers of the spirit. Faith is a fragrance from man's true homeland, and by following this fragrance, the sense of smell finds the right way there, while the spiritual ear listens to the warnings of the conscience that protects man from satanic temptations.

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the metaphor of the spiritual "sense of smell." The previous page established that envy deprives a person of their "nose"—their ability to scent spiritual truth. He is scentless, he who lacks the sense of smell;
Faith is the fragrance that animates the heart.
He whose heart did not glow with gratitude when enjoying the fragrance,
Would insolently push away the goodness of the Eternal.
Be thankful, O human, be a servant to the thankful ones, 172
Die before them, 173 stand firm in the Truth!
Do not lead the multitude away from prayer;
Do not lead them astray, as the Vizier did!
He seemed to lead toward the true faith,
But garlic was hidden within his sweets,
So that everyone who understood his word,
Found bitterness there alongside the sweetness;—
Truly wise sayings, yet with double meanings;
Sugar with rose-oil, 174 yet with poison inside.
O, never let such pious words deceive you!
A hundred crimes lie hidden within them.
For indeed he commanded: “Strive for the Truth!”
But within his words lay: “Let it go!”
Just as ore is bright and shiny on the outside,
Yet quickly stains the hand and clothes black;
Just as the fire is red within the flame,
Yet its work, the coal, is black when it is dead.
Just as the lightning is a light for the sight,
Yet does it not blind our eyesight?

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the narrative of the treacherous Vizier, a character in Rumi's Masnavi who infiltrates the early Christian community to sow discord from within. The word, full of deceit, was like precious jewelry.
For six continuous years, far from the King,
The Vizier was the refuge for the servants of Jesus.
They surrendered their hearts and faith to him,

The answer was: “Soon, Lord, I shall succeed
In bringing ruin to Christendom.”

To lead the people of Jesus according to the laws

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the account of the Vizier’s deception. Having divided the Christian community into twelve tribes under twelve princes, he provides each leader with a contradictory scroll of religious law to ensure future conflict. The prose commentary below explains the theological context of these contradictions from a 19th-century perspective.</meta>

And all were in contradiction with one another. 180
In one he declared: “Self-mastery 181
Alone is the foundation of the return to God.”
In the other: “Renunciation helps with nothing;
Only a noble spirit 182 leads to the path of light.”
In the third: “Fasting and a noble spirit
Bring you to insolent equality with God, 183
In joy and sorrow it prepares danger for you,
Only surrender helps you always.”
In another: “Friend, service 184 is needed,
For without service, surrender is dead.”

...shid <note>The conclusion of the word *Murshid*, meaning a spiritual guide.</note>, recognized as a spiritual leader. 180. The following section is an enumeration of these contradictions. I have already noted how our Sheikh <note>Referencing Rumi, the author of the poem.</note> does not concern himself with actual Christian dogmas. Faithful to his principle that Jesus and Muhammad drank from the same source, and therefore the teachings of both agree in all essential points, he allows Christianity here to suffer from those theosophical problems which occupied the Muhammadan <note>A common 19th-century term for Muslim.</note> scholars of his time. As is well known, God assures us various times in the Quran that this Book of Books allows no doubt to arise (Surah 4, 84: “And were it <note>the Quran</note> not from God, they would find in it much conflict”), a teaching to which the orthodox Muslim holds with zeal. Yet the Arabs very soon discovered a multitude of contradictions in the Quran, the number of which increased when the *hadiths* <note>original: *ehādīṯ*</note>, the traditionally handed-down sayings of Muhammad, were collected and made into a further source of religion. While the sectarians exploit these contradictions according to their theories, the orthodox seek to resolve them through explanations—often very forced—or by assigning a relationship that is sometimes indefinite, sometimes specific to persons, times, and circumstances; he therefore considers them only apparent, and to seek them out and hold fast to them is for him considered sinful. 181. i.e., fasting or other asceticism <note>original: *Askese*</note> which serves to mortify the flesh. 182. i.e., generosity <note>original: *Freigebigkeit*</note>, which shows itself among the rich in the giving of their possessions, and among the poor in self-sacrifice for the benefit of their fellow men. 183. That is, if you count it to yourself as merit and do not regard God as the one who alone can grant you the strength and success for it. 184. The exter— <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely referring to "external service" or "external rituals".</note>

->108<-

<summary>The Vizier creates twelve contradictory scrolls for the Christian leaders, each advocating a different path to God—such as asceticism, generosity, or total surrender—to sow discord. <meta>This page continues the analysis of the Vizier’s contradictory religious scrolls. Having previously discussed the tension between free will and predestination, it now concludes with a section on the "Color of Jesus"—a metaphor for Divine Unity that absorbs all worldly differences—and provides extensive scholarly commentary on the legends and metaphors used in the text.</meta>

And Jesus’s color did not merely dye his garment, 200
But that vat, in which the most colorful garment quickly
Becomes simple and lovely like the morning breeze.
He who strives through it toward the Truth 201
Is never sated by it, just as the fish is never sated by the clear spring. 202
Though the dry land may appear a thousandfold colorful,
The fish loves only the wave, the pure one.
Yet the sea, even with its host, is too small
To be an image of the Almighty Ruler.
For countless seas lie in the dust,
Adoring before the Lord with their hosts.
Does the rain of grace not fall down without end,

...containing similar contradictions, a fact confirmed to him by the circumstance that the shared problems would suggest sixteen books rather than twelve. 200. An allusion to a legend from the youth of Christ, taken only from <term>Muhammadan</term> <note>original: muhammedanischen; a historical term for Islamic or Muslim accounts.</note> reports, which <term>Sheikh Zade</term> <note>A 16th-century Ottoman commentator.</note> relates in a marginal note to <term>Beidhawi</term> (3, 45) <note>Referring to the classical Quranic commentator al-Baydawi.</note> as follows: 
Jesus was placed by his mother as an apprentice to a dyer. One day, the dyer wished to leave on business and said to Jesus: "Here are various garments, upon each of which I have made a special mark; dye them with these specific colors." Jesus, however, threw them all into the same vat. When the dyer returned and inquired, Jesus told him what he had done. "You have ruined the clothes!" cried the dyer; but Jesus pulled out a green, a red, a yellow, and a white garment, exactly as the dyer had wanted. Those present were astonished and became believers, and these are the Apostles. — According to another version, Jesus could also take garments of various colors and make them monochrome in his vat. In our passage, "his color" represents the knowledge of the Unity of God, and the colors that are absorbed by it are the erroneous views of the deity and selfish desires found among men. 201. In contrast to earthly monotony, which produces weariness and disgust. 202. The poet uses here the same image known to us from the 17th verse of the introduction (cf. Note 7). The "Dry" is the visible world, the world of form, which the sage—driven involuntarily in the formless element of the knowledge of God—abhors just as the fish wandering through the waves of the formless and colorless sea abhors the dry land.

So that the sea might grant us pearl-treasures? 203
Do not countless suns glow, to teach
The seas and clouds compassion?
Did not a ray of wisdom fall upon flood and earth,
To become a seed-corn in the earth's womb? —
The earth is faithful; what we sow upon her,
She lets its kind arise without deceit;
From the sun’s faithfulness, her own has flowed,
Which poured the light of Justice over her;
But she keeps her secret silently guarded
Until spring <note>original: Lenz; an archaic or poetic German term for spring.</note> reveals Omnipotence to us.
Who then gave such faithfulness, such understanding
To the inanimate, but the hand of the Highest,
Whose grace awakens consciousness in the lifeless,
Whose judgment strikes the clever man down? 204
Yet this word is too high for spirit and heart—
No ear hears me; so why do I still speak?
Where there is an ear, it becomes an eye everywhere, 205
And where there is a stone, it becomes crystal. 206
Magic never reaches His magic power,
Nor Alchemy reach His Alchemy! 207
I wish to speak praise, yet I find no praise;

---

...the land with its charming variety. 203. See Note 9.
204. That is, He gives consciousness to the soulless so that it recognizes His omnipotence, and He punishes the rational human who closes their eyes to His greatness. 205. Hearing is the lower stage of perceiving God, which man can help attain through his free will; seeing is the higher stage, reachable only through God’s grace. A willing ear must be present if the spiritual eye is to become seeing. 206. The person who is unfeeling like a stone becomes filled with light, like a crystal, if they are only willing to hear. 207. <term>Kimia</term> <note>Alchemy</note>, is to the person of the East <note>original: Morgenländer; literally "one from the morning-land," a historical term for people of the Middle East or Orient.</note> in the literal sense an elixir that converts base metals into noble ones; figuratively, however, the word describes the power of God to create wisdom out of ignorance, rebellion, and disbelief in the hearts of men.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical and mystical exploration of Divine Unity from Rumi’s Masnavi. While the previous page discussed the "vat of unity" and the faithfulness of the natural world, this page addresses the "sin" of individual existence—the idea that as long as one perceives themselves as a separate being (even a worshiper), they are separate from God. It introduces the Persian idiom "blind and blue" to describe the ephemeral nature of the material world.</meta>

114

To praise is to claim existence, 208 and all existence is sin.
Be absorbed into His Being! Vanish before Him!
For all existence is blue and blind before Him. 209
Were it not blind, it would have dissolved before Him;
It would have melted before the heat of the suns,
And if it did not wear the color of mourning—blue—
The structure of the world would not have solidified like ice. 210

---

Like the King, stubborn forever and ever,
The Vizier fought against fate,
Against the Omnipotence that, with a single call,
With a word, created the worlds from nothingness.
Countless worlds are revealed
To the eye that God makes strong and clear;
And if the universe seems infinitely large to you,
It is but an atom in the lap of Omnipotence.
The prison of your soul is this world;

---

To bring forth obedience and faith. 208. Insofar as the one who praises stands outside of the Praised One as something foreign, and, as it were, contrasts his own existence with that of God. <note>In Sufi thought, the greatest "sin" is the ego's claim to exist independently of God.</note> 209. That is, God is the only truly Existing One, and everything that appears to us as defined and imperishable is, before Him, undefined and transitory. *Blind and blue* <note>original: "Kūr u kebūd"</note> is a figure of speech by which the Persian language expresses the concept of the uncertain and the indefinite; the former by transferring the subjective (unseeing) to the objective (invisible), the latter as the color of that which fades into the distance. 210. Just as bluish ice forms from clear water, the world of forms solidified from the ethereal light of the Godhead; it only exists in its separation because the rays of divine glory do not act upon its dullness. As a reminder of its separation from the Divine, it is eternally clothed in the color of mourning. Among the Persians, blue is the color of mourning, just as black was common among the earlier Turkish Sultans following the example of the Abbasids and Fatimids. <note>The Abbasids and Fatimids were major Islamic caliphates; the Abbasids famously adopted black as their official color.</note> The latter seems to have been a general Arabic custom, and when the Persians attribute the introduction of blue as the mourning color to one of their earliest mythical kings, Kay Kawus <note>original: Keikawus; a legendary shah of the Pishdadian dynasty described in the Shahnameh.</note>, it at least clarifies...

<summary>This page explores the Sufi concept of 'fana' (annihilation of the self), suggesting that individual existence is a form of separation from God. It explains the Persian symbolic use of the color blue to represent both the material world's "mourning" and its status as "frozen" divine light.</summary>

Specifically, the passage alludes to the poetic competitions held in Mecca in the pre-Islamic era. Several verses by Labid, one of the most famous poets of Muhammad's time, had been hung on the door of the national sanctuary of the Kaaba and had gained such universal recognition that no other poet dared to pick up the gauntlet. Then Muhammad appeared with the second chapter of the Quran and hung it beside the words of Labid, who felt so completely defeated by it that he secretly tore down his own poem at night and, convinced of Muhammad’s divine mission, converted to his religion. <meta>This page continues the commentary on Rumi's Masnavi, specifically the section contrasting material greed with spiritual reality. The previous page ended with a discussion on how even the most learned or powerful—like Galen or the Egyptian Pharaoh—are humbled by divine truth. This page addresses those who hoard wealth and introduces the myth of Harut and Marut as a cautionary tale about human weakness and the vanity of the world.</meta>

O you who heap up treasures, scraping in corners,
Clinging to delusion like the beard to the bull, 216
What is the bull, whose beard you lend yourselves to be?
What is the dust of the earth, whose weeds you are?
A woman, grown pale 217 due to an evil deed,
Was transformed into the star of Suhra <note>The planet Venus</note> through metamorphosis. 218

human courage and human foresight are nothing. 216. “Bull’s beard” <note>original: "Bart des Stiers"</note> is a proverbial expression in Persian for something base and at the same time worthless, just as the goods of this world are when compared to the spiritual possession of eternal truths. 217. Literally “became of yellow countenance,” i.e., ashamed. 218. The story of the transformation of Anahid or Suhra into the planet known to Orientals by this name, Venus, is told by the commentary on this passage as follows: Suhra, a beautiful woman, had quarreled with her husband and therefore came before the tribunal of the two angels Hārūt and Mārūt (who had descended to earth to learn the weaknesses of the human race). No sooner had they glimpsed her grace and loveliness than they were completely carried away by it and therefore sought to prolong her legal matter. When Suhra returned on another day, the angels invited her into their inner chamber and there declared their love for her. Suhra answered them: “Only if you perform three deeds will I comply with your wish; first, that you worship the idol I revere; second, that you kill my husband; and third, that you drink wine.” Since the two angels knew murder and idolatry to be great crimes, they refused to agree to them; however, they declared themselves ready to drink wine, as they did not know that this is the source of vice, the mother of shameful acts. As the story goes, Suhra then said to them: “You ascend to heaven every evening after finishing your administrative duties; so tell me the word whose divine power enables you to accomplish this.” The angels shared the sublime word with the woman, and by means of it, she ascended to heaven. God, however, transformed her shape and made her into a star. — The Persian version of the story, according to which Anahid remains innocent, is more ingenious, yet the interpretation present here is already found in the Quran (2, 96) <note>Likely referring to Surah al-Baqarah 2:102 in modern numbering, which mentions Harut and Marut</note> and furthermore by a saying of Muhammad (<term>Hadith: a recorded tradition of the Prophet's words or actions</term>) given by <term>al-Suyuti: a famous 15th-century Egyptian scholar and polymath</term> on the authority of Ali, which vali- <meta>The text cuts off here; the German word "bestätigt" (validated/confirmed) is completed on the next page.</meta>

<meta>This page continues the poetic translation and commentary of Rumi’s Masnavi. The previous page introduced the legend of Suhra (Venus) and the fallen angels Hārūt and Mārūt. This page concludes that narrative arc and begins a critique of intellectual pride versus spiritual humility, specifically referencing the Quranic account of Adam's creation.</meta>

Is it a transformation, 219 when a woman becomes a star?
How much more so, sinner, when your body becomes dust! 220
Your spirit seeks to raise you to the horizon,
Yet you remain stuck, low, to the dust and slime.
This lowly mind causes you to strip yourself
Of the dignity for which the angels envied us. 221
If Suhra’s 222 transformation was not for her salvation—
What humiliation shall be your portion!
You ascend to heaven on the steeds of zeal, 223
Yet Adam’s glory is closed off to you.
Are you not Adam’s son? How long, O fool,
Will you prefer the base to the sublime?
How long will you say: "I wish to encompass the All;
The whole world shall not let my fame pass"? —
Even if the whole world perished in snow,
The sun’s glow would melt it in an instant.
Of the Jewish Vizier and his like

...confirmed <note>original: "tigt," the suffix of "bestätigt"</note>, according to which, whenever he beheld *Venus*, he is said to have cried out: "May God curse *Sahra*; for she is the one who led two angels into temptation, *Hārūt* and *Mārūt*." 219. This is how I translate the Arabic word *meskh* <note>original: "meskh"</note>, for whose full concept—the transformation of a being with its transfer from a higher class of creation into a lower one (such as that of a human into a monkey)—the German language has no single expression. Metamorphosis is thus a punishment and misfortune for man, for he is the noblest of God's creatures. 220. This is not to be understood here as physical death, but as spiritual death. Man is only earth and dust as soon as his spirit turns away from God and is consumed by earthly desires. 221. An allusion to the passage in the *Quran* (2, 34 <note>The source cites 2, 32, which follows an older verse numbering; modern editions list the prostration of angels as verse 34</note>): And (remember the time) when We (God) said to the angels: "Prostrate yourselves before *Adam*!" whereupon they prostrated themselves except *Iblis*: he refused and puffed himself up (in pride) and became an infidel. 222. Who, although deprived of her reason, became a luminous heavenly body. 223. Namely through astronomy <note>original: "Sternkunde"</note>. The meaning is: through your earthly knowledge you think to elevate yourself, and in doing so you forget that the true glory of man rests upon a free gift of God's grace, of which he only shows himself worthy through humble recognition.

<meta>This page continues the poetic translation and commentary of Rumi’s Masnavi. The previous page discussed the spiritual transformation of the soul and the dangers of earthly desire, ending with a mention of the Jewish Vizier who schemes against the faithful. This page describes God’s power to transform evil into good and introduces the Vizier’s strategic retreat into a hermit's cell.</meta>

Crimes yield to the flaming swords of God;
He turns mad delusion into wisdom,
And from poisonous sap He grants balm;
From dark premonition He lets clarity sprout;
From hate and envy He lets love flow;
He turns fear into rest—into a **rose grove**
The glow of the furnace became for the **Friend**. 224
Burning for him, my senses slip away;
I become a **doubter**, if I seek to fathom him! 225

Then, devising another plan, the **Vizier** quickly
Exchanged the teaching chair for a quiet cell. 226
Leaving his disciples only longing and lamentation,

224. The story alluded to here is found in the **Quran** <note>original: "Koran"</note> (21:52 ff.). When **Abraham** (**Khalil**) <note>meaning "The Friend [of God]"</note> had destroyed the idols served by his father, the people—realizing they could do nothing against him through reason (v. 68)—cried out: "Burn him and avenge your gods!" — We (the **Quran** then has God say) spoke: "O fire, become cool and harmless for Abraham." From this cool fire, Eastern legend-poetry has made a **rose grove**, regarding which I refer to the appendix. 225. That is, when I think of how God has the power to make anything out of everything, I become a **doubter** of the real existence of things. The word I translate here as **doubter** is the term <term>Sophist</term> <note>original: "Söfistā"</note>, which passed from Greek into Oriental idioms with the meaning of "Skeptic." The commentary explains this as a pagan society of philosophers who denied the real existence of things and was divided into three classes: first, the <term>Inadiyya</term> <note>original: "Inādijjè"</note>, who declared the objects of external perception to be worthless delusions; second, the <term>Indiyya</term> <note>original: "Indijjè"</note>, who did not outright deny the existence of things but made them dependent on thought, so that according to them an object only exists when it is thought, and possesses such and such properties only when one thinks of them as belonging to it; third, the <term>La-adriyya</term> <note>original: "La-adrijè"</note>, who only deny knowledge of the existence or non-existence of things and answer all questions put to them as Skeptics: **I do not know** <note>original: "la adri"</note>. Our poet compares himself here to this last class. 226. In order to [live] in complete withdrawal from...

He remained in the cell for forty days.
The memory of the grace of his speeches
Drove both the young and old to distraction.
Bent from fasting, he sat within the cell,

Like fish we gasp upon dry land—

O you, whose like no age has ever seen,
Help us, for God’s sake! Do not let us perish!”

->They spoke: 245 “Are we not yours, now and forevermore!”<-

[...no one should] accuse [me], even if I were to trade the earth for the heaven. 245. I have already drawn attention to how our poet completely subscribes to the view that man, in his efforts to lead a life pleasing to God, will inevitably go astray if he does not join himself to a leader. Such a leader is called a <term>Murschid</term> <note>original: "Murschid"; a spiritual guide or teacher in Sufism who directs the disciple's path</note>, the Right-Guide, and the disciples who join him are called <term>Murīdīn</term> <note>original: "Murīdīn"; the plural of Murīd, literally "one who desires" or "one who has a will," referring to a committed spiritual seeker</note>, the Strivers. The power of the Murschids—which they enjoyed partly through the number of their followers and their influence over them, and partly merely in their own imagination—is a characteristic feature of that era. This was a time when the complete dissolution of all formal dogma in <term>Islam</term> <note>The author uses "positive faith" to refer to the external, legalistic structures of religion as opposed to internal experience</note> on one side had produced a profound mysticism on the other. This is evidenced particularly by the pompous titles, such as <term>Sultan</term> or <term>Shah</term> <note>Titles traditionally reserved for temporal monarchs (King/Emperor), here applied to spiritual masters</note>, which the Sheikhs bestowed upon one another, and according to which they usually designated their spiritual dignity with the name <term>Dewlet</term> <note>original: "Dewlet"; meaning "State," "Empire," or "Fortune," here signifying a spiritual kingdom or dominion</note>, meaning Reign. The <term>Murīd</term> had to recognize the Murschid unconditionally as his lord, through whom his relationship to God as well as to his worldly authorities was mediated; through this relationship, he was, as far as possible, released from religious and civil laws. The Murschid was considered by his disciple to be identical with God; his commands, whether they concerned earthly or spiritual matters, were therefore just as holy to him as if they had emanated from God Himself, and demanded his most absolute obedience. To show how far this obedience went, or at least was intended to go, I include in the appendix a story from the life of <term>Mewlānā</term> <note>original: "Mewlānā"; "Our Master," referring to Jalāl ad-Dīn Muhammad Rūmī, the 13th-century mystic and poet</note>—not because it seems to me to bear the hallmark of truth, but only because it was written down by a later famous mystic, <term>Molla Dschāmi</term> <note>original: "Molla Dschāmi"; Jami (1414–1492), a celebrated Persian polymath and mystic often regarded as the last great classical poet of Persia</note>, clearly as a model for the disciples of his time. — We have already seen on earlier occasions how Sheikh <term>Mewlānā</term> uses his narrative only as a vehicle for his religious teachings; clearly, the following verses also have the primary purpose of [illustrating] the relationship of dependency—

According to the doctrine of the absolute unity of the representative with the represented, the <term>Murschid</term> is, for <term>Mewlānā</term> <note>original: "Mewlānā"; "Our Master," a common title for Rumi</note>, God Himself. Accordingly, in these verses—following a mode of transference peculiar to our poet—the subject is less the specific relationship of dependence of the student on the teacher, but rather the general dependence of man on God. 248. [...to] perceive, dissolves in him. 249. <term>Mewlānā</term> <note>literally "Our Master," referring to the poet Rumi</note> likely had the standards of the Seljuk rulers of <term>Ikonium</term> <note>modern-day Konya, Turkey</note>, his place of residence at the time, in mind here. As is well known, various Oriental dynasties carried specific imperial emblems that originated from the horoscope of one of their rulers. These were primarily minted as coats of arms on their coins; but the fact that they were also applied as decoration on flags is sufficiently proven by the circumstance that the same custom still exists today among the Ottoman sultans, who decorate their coins and standards with the crescent and star. The Seljuk coins with a lion and a sun above it are well known; concerning the origin of this symbol, I find a note in <term>Abu-l-Faradsch</term> <note>Bar Hebraeus, a 13th-century scholar and historian</note> (p. 487 of the Arabic text): "Sultan <term>Ghijath ed din</term> <note>Ghiyath al-Din Kaykhusraw II</note> (son of <term>Alā ed din</term>) married the daughter of the King of the Georgians. He burned with passion for her and loved her to such an extent that he wanted to have her portrait placed on the <term>Dirhems</term> <note>silver coins</note>. However, he was advised to instead have a lion with a sun above it depicted, so that it might be related to his horoscope and he would thus attain his wish." Even today, the lion with the sun rising above it is the Persian coat of arms. 250. Compare Note 6. 251. That is: you are the primary cause of our life and actions, just as the invisible wind is the cause of the movement of the lion on the flag cloth. 252. That is: the world of forms, in that you give it self-consciousness. 253. That is: the inspiration.

This victory, the first which Muhammad won, is important because it primarily laid the foundation for the subsequent power of the Arabian prophet. Partly because of the many advantages that grew from it, and partly to have an answer to the frequent demands for visible miracles, Muhammad attributed this victory to supernatural divine assistance, claiming that his followers had fought only in appearance, but in reality 4,000 angels under <term>Gabriel’s</term> leadership had defeated the enemy. <note>The prose below explains the historical and theological context of the verses above.</note>

...at Gabriel’s command, a handful of sand was thrown against the enemy, who became so dismayed that they turned their backs and took flight; thus, God was the one acting, and <term>Muhammad</term> himself was merely the instrument. — The <term>mystics</term> <note>referring to the Sufis, followers of Islamic mysticism</note> assign a general meaning to this passage and believe that God indicates through it that all human action occurs only through His will; however, this is certainly difficult to reconcile with the doctrine of human free will, which they also recognize. 

258. Here, the <term>Sheikh</term> <note>original: "Der Scheich," referring to Rumi, the author of the Masnavi</note> guards against an interpretation that might be forced upon his words: that he wants to explain human action as a law of necessity <note>determinism</note>, which would negate free will and thus the moral value of actions. God, in His <term>Omnipotence</term> <note>original: "Allmacht"</note>, also governs this freedom, and without Him, no one forms a good or evil thought. 

259. For a person only pleads to someone who has influence over their destiny. 

260. That is: shame and remorse would be incompatible with the state of unaccountability in which a person would find themselves if they acted without freedom according to a law of necessity. 

261. That is: if a law of necessity guided our actions, a wavering in one's resolve would not be possible. 

262. Because they complained and pleaded, even though they should have accepted the decree of their <term>Murshid</term> <note>A spiritual guide or teacher</note> as unalterable, as if it had come from God. The poet answers this objection with the general law that anyone who [treats] the influence of Omnipotence as...

<summary>Rumi explores the tension between divine omnipotence and human responsibility, arguing that the existence of remorse and the act of prayer prove that humans possess a degree of free will despite God being the ultimate source of all action.</summary>
<keywords>free will, predestination, Rumi, Muhammad, Murshid, remorse, Islamic theology, Sufism, Omnipotence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the response to the theological question posed on page 127. While the previous page asked why humans feel shame if God is the ultimate actor, this page provides the "answer" through the metaphor of illness as a spiritual awakening that reveals human dependence on the Divine.</meta>

<page-num>128</page-num>

->— Here is my answer; if you understood it aright,<-
->You would be free from error, pledged to salvation’s light.<-
->Illness awakens in us lament and grief,<-
->Yet the time of illness is the time of awakening’s relief; 263<-
->Therefore you seek, when you feel sick and weak,<-
->The forgiveness for your sins you humbly seek,<-
->Vice seems hideous to you, and your gaze<-
->Turns back to virtue's path and holy ways,<-
->And you vow, henceforth, only God’s will<-
->In all your deeds and omissions to fulfill.<-
->Thus you convince yourselves that in being ill,<-
->Thoughts become wakeful, wise, and still.<-
->O seeker of truth, hear this truth so clear:<-
->Only the afflicted find clarity near; 264<-
->The more suffering, the more wakeful and bright,<-
->The more knowing, the more pale and weak in might. 265<-
->Will you not plead when you feel the constraint,<-
->Bound in the fetters of Omnipotence, without complaint? 266<-

When one feels the disadvantage of their earthly existence, they plead and reflect, and consequently, in this state, they are clearer about their relationship with God than when the body is healthy. To the <term>Murīds</term> <note>disciples or students in a Sufi order</note>, this clarity was granted through the pain of separation from their <term>Murshid</term> <note>a spiritual guide or teacher</note>, a pain which is constantly regarded as an "illness" by Eastern poets.

263. That is: illness allows a person to come to themselves, shaking off the "sleep of forgetfulness of God," and reminding them of their spiritual duties.

264. Namely, clarity regarding the influence of the Almighty on the destiny of humanity.

265. That is: the more a person is visited by misfortune and misery, the more apparent their dependence on God becomes—a relationship that the healthy and suffering-free person completely forgets. Similarly, <term>Muhammad</term> says in a <term>Hadith</term> <note>a recorded saying or tradition of the Prophet Muhammad</note> to his followers: "I am the one among you who knows God best and fears Him the most."

266. That is: it is the duty of the wise person, who is conscious of the influence of Omnipotence on their fate, to humble themselves before God in prayer and pleading. Thus <term>Anas</term> <note>Anas ibn Malik, one of the most well-known companions of the Prophet and a prolific narrator of his sayings</note>, one of the most reliable reporters of Muhammad’s sayings, relates that he frequently heard the Prophet cry out in pleading:

> “O You who turn the hearts and eyes, strengthen our hearts in faith in You, O God!”

Otherwise, do not say that you were merely succumbing!

Earthly work is constraint for the Seer;

<note>270. The wicked are the lineage of Hell; and since Hell is a prison, they also love the dungeon of this world, from which they shall one day enter the place of eternal punishment, according to the previously mentioned Arabic proverb: <note>original: "Ein jedes Ding kehrt zu seinem Ursprung zurück"</note> *Everything returns to its origin* (cf. And what will explain to you what Illiyyun is? <meta>This page continues the discussion on the necessity of a spiritual guide. Having established that the Prophets represent the invisible God, the text now explains the mystical unity between the Creator and His messengers, using metaphors of light and essence to show that despite outward differences, their spiritual reality is one.</meta>

<page-num>133</page-num>

This is not false! It would be an abomination to separate the two:
The representative and the one represented. 278
They are two to him who clings only to form;
They are one to him who rises above form. 279
Thus the eye is twofold in appearance,
But look upon the light that shines within the eye:
If you look, O man, at the light of both eyes,
You are unable to divide this light. 280
Ten candles are united in one room,
And each seems different in its shape;
But behold the light of all the candles at once—
See if you are able to separate each beam.
You may well count a hundred apples; yet crush
Those hundred, and one juice remains for you.
From separation and divisibility,
From part and number, the spirit 281 is always free.
Sweet is the unity of lovers—therefore hold
To the spirit, and leave the stubborn form! 282

...must therefore subordinate oneself to a religious leader. 278. The doctrine of the absolute spiritual unity of God with His representatives, to which we have already drawn attention at various points in the course of this poem, is based on several passages in the Quran itself, where it says, for example (4:80): <note>The original German text cites 4:82, but in modern numbering, this is typically Quran 4:80.</note>
> Whoever obeys the Messenger (i.e., Muhammad), thereby obeys God;
likewise, God speaks (48:10) to Muhammad:
> Truly, those who pledge allegiance to you, pledge allegiance only to God.
In the same sense, a saying of Muhammad is cited:
> Whoever sees me, sees the Truth. <note>original: "den Allwahren," a translation of the Arabic *al-Haqq*, a name for God meaning The Truth or The Reality.</note>
279. That is, according to outward appearance they are two independent individuals, but according to the spirit they are only one, since both are governed by one and the same will. 280. For the light relates to the eye as the soul to the body, as God's commands to their proclaimers, the prophets. In this as in the following examples lies an allusion to the Quranic verse (2:285):
> We make no distinction between any of His (God's) messengers.
281. That is, the abstract concept in contrast to the concrete appearance. 282. That is, those who [attain] the concept of absolute unity, in which alone man experiences the bliss of merging into God,

->Let the stubborn form vanish!<-

...the resisting world of external appearances. 283. That is: only when you have destroyed the influence of external appearances within yourself through spiritual renunciation will you find the treasure of divine Unity hidden beneath the concrete world of forms. 284. That is, God’s. This passage recalls an earlier one (see note 108); the meaning here is also: some do not succeed in reaching the knowledge of Unity through the ordinary path of renunciation, and to such a person it reveals itself through a free gift of God’s grace, provided they are otherwise filled with true love for God. 285. That is: he heals the heart of the pious penitent, wounded by longing for him, by allowing them to share in his knowledge. 286. According to a saying of Muhammad, the first thing God created was the white pearl, which Islamic theosophists consider the <term>primordial matter</term> <note>original German: *Urstoff*</note> from which God later formed all spiritual and physical beings. <term>Mewlānā</term> <note>Persian for "Our Master," referring to the poet Rumi</note> refers to this and describes this primordial matter as a clear mass of light, which was clouded by the subsequent creation of the world of forms, just as sunshine spread evenly over a surface is interrupted by the falling shadow of a battlemented wall. 287. The abstract world falls victim to divisibility and countability as soon as it passes into concretion, just as sunshine becomes countable when the shadow of the wall's battlements interrupts it. 288. With the projectile of renunciation, destroy the battlements of the world of forms; that is, free yourself through abstraction from the multiplicity of concrete appearances, then you will see the absolute <note>The German text breaks off here mid-sentence.</note>

<summary>This page uses the architectural metaphor of "battlements" to explain how divine unity becomes fragmented into the physical world of forms. It encourages the seeker to look past these "shadows" to find the singular light of God.</summary>

...more fanatical; see Note 123. 294. This concludes the narrative, which has been interrupted so many times, without our poet returning to the <term>Jewish King</term> <note>The antagonist who initiated the plot against the Christians</note> mentioned at the beginning. Such inconsistencies—including the fact that the completely suppressed sect, as the Christians were described at the beginning, appears in the development of the story as a nation capable of civil war—will no longer seem striking to the reader after the repeated remarks on the peculiarities and purposes of <term>Mewlānā</term> <note>Persian: *Mawlana*, meaning "Our Master," the title of the poet Rumi</note>. 295. The image is taken from the nut, whose shell is compared to the body and its core to the spirit. A human who is unaware of their destiny and their relationship to the Godhead resembles a "deaf" (hollow) nut, for they lack the spirit that could attain the bliss of merging with God upon the destruction of their body in death. 296. That is, the soul, which death separates from the body, just as a knife separates the outer skin from the pomegranate seeds. 297. That is, the very concept of a "pomegranate" implies the juicy flesh of the seeds, and where the pomegranate has no [flesh, it is but a name].

<meta>The previous page discussed the soul leaving the body at death, comparing the physical form to a shell or a pomegranate skin that hides the true essence or "core." This page continues that metaphysical reflection, urging the reader to seek the spiritual "steel" rather than relying on the "wooden" outer form.</meta>

<page-num>137</page-num>

->Go, you who are bound by form, strive for the spirit,<-
->For over form, the spirit is the wing. 298<-
->Go, be the delight of those permeated by the spirit,<-
->Thus you will become rich, noble, and great. 299<-
->Like a wooden sword in its scabbard, so does<-
->A soul without spirit prove to be within the body. 300<-
->For only while tucked in the scabbard does it have value;<-
->Drawn out, it is but wood, not a sword. 301<-
->Do not go to battle with a sword of wood,<-
->Test it before the battle is even ignited. 302<-
->Seek another, should you find it to be wood;<-
->But if it is steel, then draw it with confidence and pride.<-
->There is a steel in the armory of the Saints, 303<-
->Which magically destroys all misery!<-
->No wise man has ever lived who did not say these words:<-
->"The Sage is the salvation and refuge of the universe." 304<-

---

...contains such <note>Referring to the "rotten and hollow" soul from the previous page</note>, then it bears the name unjustly. 298. That is, the spirit permeated by the knowledge of God <note>original: *Gotteserkenntnis*, often translated as "gnosis" or "divine recognition"</note> is the wing that carries man upward from mere form to truth. Perhaps the poet also alluded to the idiom: "May the wing of the <term>Huma</term> <note>A mythical Persian bird, similar to the Phoenix, said to never light on the ground and to bring good fortune to anyone it overshadows</note> be over your head," meaning: May you be happy. In this case, the sense would be: Only through the spirit that recognizes God does man enjoy true bliss in his individuality. 299. Namely, rich in spiritual goods, the knowledge of God, etc. 300. This refers to a soul that only provides life but lacks the spirit striving for the higher—a soul that does not meet the requirements one is entitled to expect based on its definition. 301. Just as the soul lost in materialism, when it leaves the human body, stands on the same level as an animal soul. 302. That is: do not let yourself be found so poorly equipped on the Day of Resurrection, but consider in good time what you will need there. 303. Meaning, in their hearts; the weapon which this contains is the love for God. 304. <meta>The text continues the discussion of spiritual transformation and discernment from page 137. <page-num>138</page-num>

If you buy pomegranates, choose the laughing ones, 305
Those whose laughter reveals the splendor of their seeds;
Blessed is the smile where, from the mouth,
A heart of pearls shines from the depths of a pure soul!

Misfortune looms over the tulip's smile. 306
The garden laughs with the laughter of pomegranates, —
As noble words also make you noble. 307
Should you wish to be like a hard fieldstone,
Proximity to the Noble turns you into a gemstone! 308
Plant only love for the pure into your soul,
Choose only the happy ones 309 for your heart!

Flee not to the darkness, for suns still glow! 310

...stretched. 305. In Persian, a "laughing" pomegranate <note>original: *Lachend*; in Persian, the term *khandān* means both "laughing" and "bursting open," as a ripe fruit does</note> refers to one that has burst open upon ripening, whose seeds, covered in whitish or pale red flesh, are then aptly compared to strings of pearls like teeth. In a similar metaphor, <term>Jami</term> <note>Nur ad-Din Abd ar-Rahman Jami (1414–1492), a celebrated Persian poet and scholar</note> in *Yusuf and Zulaikha* calls the mouth opening into a smile the "laughing" (i.e., bursting) pistachio. 306. Just as the gaping fruit reveals the contents of the pomegranate, so does a smile allow a glimpse into a person's inner self. The Noble person reveals a pure heart, which is compared here to the pomegranate seeds or to pearl-white teeth exposed in a smile; in the smile of the wicked, however, a black soul is revealed, just as the laughing (i.e., opening) tulip shows a black interior. This latter image is rare, as the tulip, with its burning red petals and a heart "charred" in the heat, is usually a symbol of the passionate lover. 307. To explain this verse, the commentator cites the following Persian couplet, without naming its author:

> From the gate of the Noble, O seeker, never stay away,
> For he who is not far from this gate is near the Lord of Lords.

308. That is, association with the pious acts upon your soul like an alchemical <term>elixir</term> <note>original: *Elixir*; historically, a substance sought by alchemists to transform base metals into gold or to grant eternal life</note>, transforming raw matter into something noble (cf. Note 207). 309. That is, those people pleasing to God, who alone can be called truly happy. 310. That is, the sincere seeker will not <insert>miss</insert> the guide to true bliss... <note>The sentence is incomplete and continues on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page uses botanical metaphors—specifically the bursting "laughing" pomegranate and the dark-centered tulip—to describe how a person's outward demeanor reveals their inner spiritual purity or corruption. It emphasizes that spending time with "Noble" or holy people can alchemically transform a person's character from common stone to precious gem.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Rumi's Masnavi, moving from the story of the deceptive Vizier to a new narrative about a tyrannical Jewish king who persecuted Christians, identifying him with the historical/legendary figures mentioned in the Quranic Sura "The Constellations."</meta>

<page-num>141</page-num>

Who also decreed the downfall of the Christians. 316
Do you also want tidings of this outrage?
Read of the Fortresses 317 in the circle of heaven!
Yes, this Shah also trod the path,
The evil path his ancestor had paved.

---

...of the Archangel Gabriel, which is here applied to Muhammad. 316. The narrative following here of another persecution of Christians by a Jewish ruler of Arabia is not to be viewed, like the preceding one, as pure fiction, but rather as an adaptation of an old Arabian legend. This is already mentioned in the Quran in the 85th Sura with the words: (4) Slain were the Lords of the Pit <note>original: *Herren der Grube*; referring to the *Aṣḥāb al-Ukhdūd*</note>, (5) of the fire supplied with fuel; (6) when they sat by it, (7) as witnesses of what they did to the believers. (8) They only felt resentment against them because they believed in God, the Mighty, the Worthy of Praise, (9) to whom belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth. And God is witness to all things. — As the core of the legend to which this Quranic passage refers, only this much can be discerned: that in pre-Islamic times <note>original: *vormuhammedanischen Zeit*</note> Christianity—at that time, according to the orthodox view, the religion of the true believers—had spread in Najran <note>an ancient city in southwestern Saudi Arabia</note>, an Arabian region located southeast of the Hijaz, while the inhabitants of Yemen, the southern tip of Arabia, adhered to the Jewish faith, which was likely introduced there from neighboring Abyssinia <note>modern-day Ethiopia</note>. The zeal with which these two tribes sought to guard the interests of their respective religions produced friction, which ended with the Jewish Yemenites marching against the Christian Najranites, defeating them, and then striving to eradicate Christianity there with fanatical cruelty. They demonstrated this cruelty particularly by throwing the Christians who refused to convert to Judaism into pits of fire, for which reason the Quran gives them the name "Lords of the Pit." However, I refer the legend itself to the appendix, noting further that our poet only follows it in general terms and, in particular, completely abandons historical ground. 317. The Quranic chapter (85), in which the "Lords of the Pit" are mentioned, bears this name, and it begins: (1) By the heaven with its Fortresses <note>original: *Burgen*; refers to the Sura *Al-Burūj* or "The Constellations"</note>! (2) and by the promised (Judgment) Day! — The Arab calls the twelve signs of the Zodiac "Fortresses," which he imagines as the stations where the sun stops and rests during its circuit of the firmament.

<summary>This page provides historical and theological context for Rumi's verses regarding a Jewish king's persecution of Christians, linking the poem to the Quranic account of the 'Lords of the Pit' and the religious conflicts in pre-Islamic South Arabia.</summary>
<keywords>Najran, Yemen, Sura 85, Al-Buruj, Lords of the Pit, religious persecution, Zodiac, Abyssinia, Christian-Jewish relations in Arabia</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the legacy of actions, specifically how the evil deeds of the historical Jewish king (mentioned on the previous page) establish a lasting "evil custom" that persists until the end of time, contrasting this with the "sweet water" of the Quranic inheritance.</meta>

<page-num>142</page-num>

Whoever has invented an evil custom,
Abhorrence and a curse strike him at all hours. 318
The good man departs, but his path endures; 319
Injustice and a curse remain from the evil man.
Until the Day of Retribution, that which is produced
From evil remains inclined toward evil; 320
Until the sound of the trumpet 321 the veins
Of bitter water and sweet water flow separately. 322
The inheritance of the good is the sweet and clear—
This inheritance is the Book, the eternally true. 323
Recognize in the prayers of those who strive

318. According to the following saying of Muhammad <term>Hadith: a recorded tradition or saying of the Prophet</term>: "Whoever establishes a praiseworthy custom receives its reward and the reward of those who act accordingly; and whoever establishes an evil custom receives its punishment and the punishment of those who act accordingly." This principle still has practical significance today. As is well known, reading the Quran is among the particularly recommended good works, and for this reason, copies of the Quran are frequently gifted to mosques or <term>Turbes: tomb chapels or mausoleums</term> for public use. According to the general view, half of the blessings earned through reading these falls to the donor and the other half to the devout reader. 319. That is, he dies, but after his death, his conduct remains as a blessed example. Saadi says similarly in the *Rose Garden* <note>original: *Rosengarten*; referring to the *Gulistan* by the Persian poet Saadi</note> (Gladwin, p. 3): "The auspicious name of Nushirwan <note>Anoushirvan the Just, a famous Persian king of the Sassanid dynasty</note> lives on through the good he practiced, even though much time has passed since Nushirwan was no more." 320. According to the Arabic proverb: "Like inclines toward like" <note>original: *Die Art neigt sich zur Art*</note>. 321. Which introduces the Day of Resurrection and Judgment, according to the Quranic passage (50:20): And "the trumpet shall be blown; that is the Day of the Threat" <note>referring to the day when threatened divine punishments are realized</note>. 322. i.e.: good as well as bad actions will continue, just as they were introduced into the world, until the Day of Judgment, without canceling each other out through mixing. 323. i.e.: the sweet water is inherited among the good, to whom God has given His Book, the Quran. These words refer to a Quranic passage (35:32), which states: "Then we gave the Book as an inheritance to those whom we have chosen among our servants; but of these, some wrong themselves" <note>by reading it only superficially or failing to act upon it</note>.

<summary>This page explores the Islamic and Persian philosophical concept of "Sunnah" (custom), explaining how the moral legacy of an individual—whether good or evil—persists in the world until the Day of Judgment. It utilizes Hadith and Quranic references to illustrate that the influence of one's actions continues to flow like separate streams of sweet and bitter water.</summary>
<keywords>Hadith, custom, moral legacy, Nushirwan, Saadi, Rose Garden, Day of Judgment, Quran 35:32, inheritance, Sassanids</keywords>

326. The fact that man is bound to the influence of the stars regarding his character and actions also shows that his destiny for good or evil is something lying outside of himself. Trust in astrology was previously universal among people in the East, and although it has suffered greatly among the educated today, Muhammadan rulers still maintain official court astrologers (<term>Muneddschims: from the Arabic 'munajjim', a professional astronomer and astrologer</term>) to satisfy the demands of general opinion, which remains caught in superstition.

328. The visible celestial bodies have influence over the fate of men, for they are concrete creations of the spiritual attributes and powers of God; yet this influence vanishes as merely secondary compared to that which those attributes and powers themselves exercise. These are the true stars, not in form, but according to the spirit.

<note>342</note> That is to say: for so long had he served his own self-will and his lusts, until these created for him a visible object of worship. 

<note>343</note> According to the Arabic proverbs: “The ‘I’ is the mother of idols,” and: “The ‘I’ is the greatest idol.” Just as the snake is easy to kill, but the dragon is invincible to earthly powers, so it requires only a little effort to shatter a wooden idol, while the extra-divine tendency of the human soul—directed toward the constant expansion of the individual earthly being—can only be destroyed with God’s assistance. 

<note>344</note> That is to say: man can no more root out the drive for evil lying within his heart than water is able to destroy the possibility of spark formation in a lighter <note>original: "Feuerzeuge," referring here to the traditional flint and steel</note>. The flood may well overpower the external glow, 345
But it can never penetrate into steel and stone.
Judaism and Christianity <note>346</note> are but the waves
Of the fire-source sprung from steel and stone.
Water in a skin-bag always grows stale and vanishes,

An idol resembles spoiled water in a skin-bag—
Selfishness is the source of such foul dross.
An idol of wood is but a murky puddle,

### Parable <note>366</note>

A mocker made a face at Ahmed, <note>original: "Achmed," a common poetic name for the Prophet Muhammad</note>
And a distorted face remained upon the wretch;

The mockery has turned back upon myself.”

If God wills that someone appear naked and ashamed,
He makes him a slanderer against the pure.
If God wills to sink a misdeed into the night,
He lets not a breath of it be remembered.
If God wills to stand by us with help and support,
He inspires in us the urge to weep and pray.

<margin>Honor is the idiom; blackness is its shame.</margin> The commentary finds in these verses an admonition to the Muslims to thank God for the blessing that evil always returns to its author. 365. That is, the garment of honor and purity. These verses allude to the saying of Muhammad (<term>Hadith</term> <note>a traditional record of the words or actions of the Prophet</note>) which is almost identical to our own proverb: “He who digs a pit for his brother falls [into it] himself.” The <term>Shah</term> <note>the King</note> wanted to bring shame upon the Christians by forcing them into <term>apostasy</term> <note>the abandonment of one's religious faith</note>, and fell victim to scorn himself by having to abandon his plan in failure. <note>367</note> This suggests that from the pain of longing for God, the joy of His knowledge and recognition emerges. 
<note>368</note> To the servant of God, meaning mankind. The absolute designation of man based on his relationship of dependence on God is completely common to the person of the East, who constantly has his relationship to the Highest Being on his lips. 
<note>369</note> A large water-lifting wheel <note>often called a <term>noria</term></note> which is set in motion by horses or mules and serves as a water lifter to moisten crops and plantations. The rubbing of its axle against the support beams produces a creaking sound, which is frequently heard in the garden-rich lowlands near Eastern cities. At a distance, this sound softens into a dull, melancholy, and by no means unpleasant tone, which holds a special charm for the Easterner, as it reminds him of sweet water, of the reservoir surrounded by shady trees from which the wheel draws, and beside which there is a cool place for rest and enjoyment.
