<meta>This page continues the philosophical reflection on the nature of elements and divine will, following the fire's explanation that it only burns by God's command. The previous page established that even the most destructive elements are merely servants of the Creator.</meta>

For by the Creator's will, pain works its purpose,
Which from the eye of sorrow <note>378</note> creates joy,
And freedom from the eye of the dungeon night!
Earth and flood and glow and winds are servants,
Living for God, <note>379</note> but dead to the children of men.
At all times the flame stands before the Highest,
Like a soul-filled lover dancing early and late.
You strike the stone against steel, and out it springs:
It is God’s command that forces it out.
O, do not touch the steel and stone of the coast—
Like man and wife, their union breeds disaster! <note>380</note>
Though steel and stone appear as force <note>381</note> before you,
Look to the higher power, O man, the One, <note>382</note>
The primal power, of which those are but the result,
Without which there is no power in nature.
Mightier than earthly force and higher
Is the power that directs the path of the seer, <note>383</note>
The spiritual power, which makes the former effective
Or soon renders it unsuccessful, without substance. <note>384</note>

---

<note>378</note> In the figurative language of the East, "eye" signifies the actual essence of a thing or a concept. The meaning here is: God is able to transform the greatest sorrow into joy and the narrowest imprisonment into freedom. <note>379</note> By understanding and obeying God's commands. <note>380</note> Namely, rebellion against God and the transgression of His commandments, which are compared here to destructive sparks. <note>381</note> That is, as a mediating cause. <note>382</note> That is, looking toward the invisible spiritual power of God, from which the diversity of visible, earthly causes and effects is derived. <note>383</note> That is, the causes acting upon the <term>world of forms</term> <note>the physical, visible world</note> are outflows of divine powers mediated through the elements, and are therefore only effects of those powers; their direct spiritual influence is revealed in the saints and prophets. <note>384</note> That is: all forces in the world of forms only work by mediating a corresponding spiritual power of God, which brings about their success. Thus, a remedy is only the apparent cause of recovery; for it is God who gives health to the sick and who therefore bears the name <term>Shâfî</term> <note>original: "Schâfî"; the Healer, one of the Names of God in Islam</note>.

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion regarding causality and divine will. Having previously established that the elements (fire, earth, water) are servants of God, the text now moves to a linguistic and metaphorical analysis of "cause" (sebeb) and "fate" (the wheel of heaven) to show that all visible actions are driven by an invisible, divine hand.</meta>

Those the earthly mind beholds,
With this [spiritual power] only the seer is entrusted.
The acting power <note>385</note> resembles a rope, 
That reaches down into the depth of the well:
The turning wheel acts upon the rope, yet 
This wheel only moves through the one who moves it. <note>386</note>

For the same reason, one very often finds the inscription: *Ya Razzaq* <note>original: "Jā Rezzāḳ"; Arabic for "O Sustainer" or "O Provider of Nourishment"</note> above the workshops and stalls (<term>dukkjāns</term> <note>Persian: *dukkān*; a shop or place of business</note>) of Muslims, to indicate that the workshop—the external means of acquiring a livelihood—is not sufficient if God’s blessing is not within it.

385. I had to give up trying to remain somewhat faithful to the original with this verse. The literal translation is: “What is this <term>sebeb</term> <note>Arabic/Persian for 'cause'</note> in Persian? Say: A rope.” The word *sebeb* in the text is the same one I rendered in the previous verses as "power," and which would be better represented in a prose translation as a "mediating cause." The word passed from Arabic into the Persian language, though the latter is unfamiliar with its primary meaning: the rope with which one lowers the drawing-bucket into the well. The transition from the concept of a rope to that of cause and effect is certainly very meaningful; and when our <term>Sheikh</term> <note>A title for a spiritual leader or master, likely referring to Rumi, the author of the *Masnavi*</note> draws attention here to the word’s primary meaning, he intends to convey that all activity and all effect in the world of forms is merely the influence of God’s spiritual powers and attributes mediated through the elements—that God is the invisible ground of all visible causes and effects. — The well, which the person of the East knows as a space that is also occasionally used as a prison, aptly signifies this world, the world of forms, the dungeon of the spirit longing for abstract freedom in God.

386. Regarding this verse, too, I have been unable to provide a translation capable of evoking the same wealth of thought that a connoisseur of Oriental languages finds in the original words. The word <term>tscharch</term> <note>Persian: *charkh*</note>, the wheel, also signifies the celestial sphere, whose turning is commonly believed to guide the fate of mankind. Beyond the literal sense given in the translation, we therefore have a more distant one which will be understandable after the preceding remarks, namely: the celestial sphere, through its turning, directs the threads of human fate; yet that turning [occurs]...

<summary>This page explains the metaphor of the "rope" (sebeb) and the "wheel" (tscharch) to illustrate that physical causes and celestial fate are merely tools moved by God's invisible hand. It highlights how the Persian word for "cause" literally means "rope," connecting the material world (the well) to the spiritual source.</summary>

<keywords>causality, sebeb, Ya Razzaq, Rumi, spiritual vs. material, fate, charkh, Sufi metaphor, symbolism of the well</keywords>

<note>387. For just as man turns the wheel, so God turns the heavenly sphere. The failure to recognize this divine influence leads to the unbelief of those who worship nature, and especially the <term>Sabians</term> <note>An ancient religious group often associated in medieval Islamic thought with the worship of celestial bodies</note>, who assume a direct influence of the heavens and their light-giving bodies upon human destiny and thus fall into the ruin shared by all heretics.</note>

<note>389. Compare Note 355. The "open eye" signifies here the spirit that recognizes the truth.</note> 

<note>390. That is, the wind which knew how to distinguish the transgressors among the Aadites from the pious. According to <term>Abulfaraj</term> <note>original: "Abulfaradsch"; likely the 13th-century historian Bar Hebraeus</note> (p. 159 of the Arabic text), the people of <term>Aad</term> belong to the powerful Arabian tribes of whom nothing certain is known because they perished in prehistoric times. <term>Abulfeda</term> <note>original: "Abulfeda"; the historian Isma'il Abu'l-Fida</note> (Pre-Islamic History, p. 178) calls them a race of giants who, after the confusion of tongues at Babel, settled in the South Arabian province of <term>Hadhramaut</term> and inhabited a land bordering Yemen and <term>Oman</term> called <term>al-Ahqaf</term> <note>meaning "the shifting sand-dunes"</note>. The Aadites were stubborn idolaters and were wiped from the face of the earth by a storm because of their unbelief. Compare the <term>Quran</term> verses (54:19–20): "Truly, We sent against them a roaring wind on a day of lasting calamity, which plucked people away as if they were uprooted trunks of palm trees."</note> 

<note>391. The messenger of God sent to the Aadites, who urged them to convert in vain. The Quran mentions this prophet frequently, including in the passage (7:65): "And to the Aadites [We sent] their brother <term>Hud</term>, who said: O my people, worship God; you have no god but Him; will you not then..."</note>

It cites the Quranic story of the tribe of Aad and the prophet Hud as a historical example of nature (the wind) acting as a divine instrument of judgment.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the account of the Prophet Hud and the miraculous circle he drew to protect the believers from the devastating wind that destroyed the people of Aad. It transitions into a similar anecdote about a later Muslim saint and provides a German translation of related Quranic verses.</meta>

At this line, the whirlwind's fury broke;
But what remained outside this line,
The storm made scatter and disperse.
In the same manner, Sheiban the shepherd 392 drew
A circular line around his flock,
So that when he went to pray on Fridays,
No greedy wolf might reach his sheep;
And neither did the wolf cross the mark,

For the universe, God is therefore identical with the word "become"; and for God—in whom thought and being, will and deed coincide—creation itself is only this word. Toward Him, the Primal Word, the "fragrance of words" <note>This refers to the prayers and praises mentioned on the previous page</note> therefore rises—that is, the words dedicated to veneration and worship—just as the part returns to the whole. This verse refers to the passage in the Quran (Surah 35, verse 11 <note>In modern verse numbering, this is Surah 35:10</note>): *Whoever desires glory: to God belongs all glory; to Him ascend the good words and the righteous deeds, as He raises them to Himself.* 

406. The imperishable, spiritual world, in which only spiritual sacrifices have value and are accepted. 

407. That is, God rewards man for the offered spiritual sacrifice by restoring twice as many holy and pure thoughts to him and furthering him in the knowledge of the Truth. 

408. That is: in every breath that rises from the pious, a gift of pure words and thoughts rises to God, who returns twice as much in their place with every breath drawn back in. 

409. Modern Persian poets love to express passages to which they intend to give special weight in Arabic—as if the extraordinary meaning were incompatible with ordinary language—so that one frequently finds half or whole Arabic stanzas in a Persian poem. This peculiarity is not as striking as it would be in any other language, given the abundance of Arabic expressions naturalized into the New Persian idiom <note>The Persian language as influenced by Arabic after the Islamic conquest</note> through Islam, especially in formal speech. The Sheikh <note>Jalal ad-Din Rumi, the "Elder" or spiritual master</note> has made use of this in the last five couplets; he therefore continues here: "We want to speak in Persian," by [rendering] the sense of the preceding stanzas in the...

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the soul’s mystical journey back to God. While the previous page focused on how pure breaths and prayers rise like gifts to the Creator, this section explains the metaphysical principle of "affinity"—the idea that parts are naturally drawn back to the Whole from which they originated.</meta>

Where our soul has drunk in delight,
Thither man always turns his gaze,
Where once pleasure and happiness were found!
Pleasure is stirred within us by that which is of like origin 410
—For the Whole indeed grants delight to the part—
Or the nature-conforming 411 which, bound 
To its own kind, dissolves into it and pairs with it.
Bread, though not belonging to the human kind,
Becomes of his kind, increasing and nourishing him;
To our kind, the food seems externally foreign,
Yet it is of our kind in another way. 412
If ever delight came to us from a foreign kind, 413
It is because it was similar to our kind.
The "similar" is like something borrowed,
Benefiting the possessor without lasting duration. 414
For even a false song 415 may delight the bird;
But finding a foreign face, he flees in fear.
So a transient mirage delights the pilgrim's gaze—
He seeks the spring in vain and flees back. 416

[The author] summarizes the thoughts: the pure breath, through which man offers the sacrifice of his worship to God, ascends to God because God breathed the delight of true knowledge into the soul. 410. Thus, in the good there is good, in the evil there is evil; in the soul directed toward eternal truth, the divine knowledge revealed to it. 411. That is, that which carries within itself the <term>dynamis; Greek for "power" or "potentiality"</term> of its kind; food is cited as an example of this. 412. Namely, by assimilating into the human body; it thus belongs to the human genus, if not in outward form, then in its inner essence. 413. That is, from a substance which corresponds with the kind it acts upon only in form—unlike the "homogenous" (which matches in form and essence) or the "nature-conforming" (which matches only in essence). 414. Because this only bears the appearance of ownership, and the enjoyment resulting from it lasts only until the moment it is reclaimed. 415. Namely, the specific song of the bird’s own genus imitated by the fowler <note>original: *Vogelsteller*</note> to lure it. 416. The mirage in steppe regions allows the imagination of the Easterner to lead the desert pilgrim astray through the evil spirits (<term>ghouls; from the Arabic *ghul*, shape-shifting desert demons</term>) dwelling in the wilderness...

<summary>This page explains the spiritual principle of affinity, arguing that the soul is drawn to God because it shares His nature, much as food is assimilated into the body or a bird is drawn to its own kind's song.</summary>

The memory of this ruinous <term>mirage; original: "Trugbild"</term> is very familiar to the inhabitant of hot plains, such as the <term>Orient; original: "Morgenland," literally "Morning-land"</term> offers in such abundance, and allusions to it are therefore not rare in the poetic works created there. Thus Hafiz calls out to himself at the end of a <term>ghazal; a rhythmic and rhyming lyric poem</term>:
> You who have set your heart, O Hafiz,
> Upon the faithfulness of the fair —
> Will the thirsty man ever be refreshed
> By the shimmering of the <term>mirage; original: "Morgane," referring to the Fata Morgana</term>?
The Oriental reader, who presupposes a deeper mystical sense in all such images, thinks in the case of the pilgrim in the text passage of the human being who strives toward the life-spring of true knowledge on the thorny desert path of earthly existence and is thereby drawn away from the straight path by false doctrines and carnal desires. Similarly, it is said in a passage of the Quran (24, 39): "And those who disbelieve, their works are like a mirage in a steppe: the thirsty man considers it to be water; but finally, when he comes to it, he finds that it is nothing." 417. This stanza, although still belonging here in terms of meaning, is grammatically connected with what follows: "In order that you never go astray, listen to a narrative" etc. The second verse points to the outcome of the fable that now follows, in which a hare leads a lion into a pit through guile. 418. Referring to the famous book of fables <note>The *Kalila and Dimna*, a collection of animal fables of Indian origin</note>, which was already translated from Indian sources into Persian in pre-Islamic times under the name Bidpai. <meta>This page continues the debate between the lion and the other animals regarding the nature of trust in God (tawakkul) versus human effort (kasb). ->It torments you, and your effort is in vain, —<-
->Therefore, entrust yourself to destiny and proceed!<-
->Do not fight fate, O mighty one,<-
->Lest it bring you yourself into distress.<-
->Be as one dead before your Lord’s command, 422<-
->Or else He might send you misery and want." —<-

->He spoke: "Indeed, submission is beneficial,<-
->But the Prophet also commands striving; 423<-
->For he once gave the audible command:<-

...brings [misfortune], only in surrendering to the will of God—that is, in perfect <term>Quietism; a mystical practice of stillness and total passivity before God</term>—does one seek salvation and bliss. I have already pointed out at an earlier passage of this poem (Note 258) that orthodox Muslims <note>original: "Muhammedaner"; an archaic term for Muslims</note> are by no means averse to Quietism, but they limit it to the final station of the path to godliness and therefore only reject its extension to the entirety of one's life. The role which the poet himself took on in the passage above, he lends here to the Lion, whom he lets represent the orthodox view and combat the theories of the forest animals. During the dispute, both parties naturally frequently use relevant Quranic verses and meaningful sayings of Muhammad, such as the one used for the text passage here: "Cease from caution; for certainly caution does not suffice against destiny." 

422. That is, do not oppose the fate decreed for you through your own actions, but remain will-less before it, like, according to an already cited Arabic proverb, "the dead in the hands of the washer of the dead" <note>This is a common Sufi metaphor for total spiritual surrender, where the seeker has no more personal will than a corpse being prepared for burial</note>.

423. I translate the word <term>kesb; human acquisition or effort</term> (or *iktisab*) in this way, according to Jurjani's <note>original: "Dschordschâni"; refers to the 14th-century scholar Ali ibn Mohammed al-Jurjani</note> *Definitions* (edited by Flügel, p. 193): "the activity which leads to the gaining of an advantage or the averting of a harm," that is, human activity, while "the divine is free from the indicated purposes." 

424. This refers to an anecdote from the life of Muhammad, which the commentator on this passage tells as follows: One day someone came to the Apostle of God and asked him whether he should bind the knees of his camel and take precautions for its guarding, or whether he should let it go with a loosened halter. The Prophet answered: "Bind... [the camel and then trust in God]."

Sie sprachen: “Know, a morsel of deceit, differing
According to the gullet, is all action here below. 426
Trust in God and leave off from striving!
For what is lovelier than submission? 427
Into distress comes many a man, while escaping distress,
Stumbling upon the dragon while fleeing the snake. 428
One sets a net, and it entangles him himself;
That to which he grants life, drinks his heart’s blood.

When Pharaoh, to escape misfortune,

That which he sought 429 was [already] in his castle.
Yet your eye can never fathom the truth, —

[...tie] your camel and then trust in God!" — 425. These are also the words of Muhammad; by the "striver," one is generally to understand the person active in gaining an advantage or averting a disadvantage. Muhammad describes such a person as God's friend, i.e., as a servant well-pleasing to Him. 426. This means that the efforts through which man thinks he can influence his destiny and hunt for greater or lesser advantages according to the measure of his greed is nothing but self-deception, since fate will come to its execution regardless. 427. Based on numerous Quranic passages in which absolute <term>trust in God (tawakkul); the Sufi concept of total reliance on the Divine</term> is recommended. We recall here only the verse (65:3): "And whoever relies on God, He is his sufficiency (i.e., protection)." Muhammad is also said to have prayed every evening before going to bed: “O God! I commit myself to You, entrust my affairs to You, and lean my back against You, seeking protection!” 428. <note>original Latin: "Incidit in Scyllam qui vult vitare Charybdim."</note> He falls into Scylla who wishes to avoid Charybdis. <note>A classical reference to escaping one danger only to fall into a worse one.</note> 429. Namely Moses, whom Pharaoh’s daughter had adopted in place of a child and who was raised in Pharaoh’s own palace. In the Quran (28:8): "And the family of Pharaoh took him (Moses) in—that he might become an enemy and a sorrow to them. Truly, Pharaoh, Haman, and their [hosts were sinners.]"

<meta>This page continues the argument of the forest animals against the Lion’s insistence on human effort. Let it vanish into the Friend’s eye! 430 <note>original: "des Freundes Auge"; a common Sufi reference to God as "the Friend."</note>
What a great reward: His gaze for our gaze!
In His eye, every happiness shines for you.

It does not leave its father’s arms;
But when strength then grows in that same child,
It resists aimlessly and blindly. 431 <note>The German "ins Blaue... und Blinde" mimics a Persian idiom for acting without direction or foresight.</note>
In the Eden of pure trust in God hover
The souls that do not yet animate the body;
But the command of the Most High: "Go down!" 432 <note>original: *ihbitū*</note>
Makes greed and passion their grave.
We are the milk-thirsty little ones of the Magnificent One, 433
For His Apostle called us His own.

430. The horsemen went astray! <note>This likely refers to a line on the previous page regarding those who rely on their own speed and power.</note> That is to say, God’s eye; the meaning is: Since man, despite all applied caution, is unable to ward off a misfortune decreed for him, he does better to close his eyes entirely and let only God see and provide for him. 431. Regarding the meaning of the idiom "blue and blind" in Persian, I refer to Note 209. The thought is borrowed from a prayer of Muhammad, which reads: "My God, protect me as one protects a little child!" Man, in the awareness of his own impotence, should surrender himself to the Providence that watches over and guides him, and like an infant before its parents, be free from every impulse of his own will, which tears him away from the blissful state of innocence. 432. Namely, into the bodies of men—that is, "become individual beings." The word <term>*ihbitū* ("go down" or "descend")</term> is borrowed from a Quranic passage (2:58) <note>Modern editions usually list this as verse 2:61.</note> where Moses cries out to the Jews who were murmuring about the uniform food in the desert: "Would you exchange what is better for what is less? Go down into Egypt; there you will find what you have requested." There is, therefore, a simultaneous reminder here of the divine blessing that the Jews participated in as long as they surrendered themselves entirely to the guidance of the Prophet, and which they forfeited through greed and stubbornness. 433. According to the Quranic passage (11:8) <note>Modern verse 11:6.</note>: "And there is no creature on earth but that its provision is upon God." God thus takes the place of a father for all beings; in the same way, Muhammad is also said to have stated: "The creatures are the Fa-" <note>The text breaks off here; the complete phrase is likely "The creatures are the family of God" (Arabic: *'Ayalu-llah*).</note>

<summary>The animals argue that true safety lies in total submission to God's will, comparing the human condition to a helpless infant in a father's arms. They use the Quranic command to "descend" as a metaphor for the soul's fall from a state of pure trust into the "grave" of worldly desire.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the debate between the forest animals and the Lion regarding the nature of trust in God (tawakkul) versus human effort (kasb). The animals have just argued that since God provides rain and sustenance without human intervention, one should rely entirely on Providence. Here, the Lion begins his rebuttal, arguing that God gave humans tools and intellect precisely so they would be used.</meta>

...family of God." <note>This fragment "milie Gottes" likely concludes a note from the previous page identifying the "Apostle's own" as the family of God.</note> 434. The Lion recognizes the absolute trust in divine omnipotence as well-founded, but reminds us of the sequence of stages in ascetic exercises <term>asceticism: disciplined self-denial used to grow spiritually</term>, through which alone man can reach that degree of proximity to God where he no longer requires further exertion of his powers. 435. That is, through the continued exertion of one's powers. 436. That is, abstaining from all personal activity by believing in a law of necessity <note>often referred to as fatalism</note> which is supposed to lead man to a predetermined goal, whether he strives for his own progress or not. 437. The fact that a tool is handed to someone implies the command to use it in the appropriate manner. The peoples of the East call this and similar things "the language of the situation" <note>original: "die Sprache der Sachlage"; in Arabic, *lisan al-hal*</note>, which according to an Arabic proverb is "more eloquent than the language of speech." 438. An allusion to the Middle Eastern custom of placing the commands of a superior—either the document itself (if issued in writing) or as a symbolic gesture—against the eyes and forehead to indicate one's certain intent to comply. The sense of the verse is thus: If you sincerely dedicate your powers to the purpose for which they were granted to you, i.e., to asceticism and the service of God. 439. That is, through such...

"If He sends down the rain for us from heaven,
Do you think He would not also graciously provide our bread?"

The Lion <term>Lion: Leu; an archaic German term for lion</term> spoke: "True, <gloss>434</gloss> but the Lord of the World
Has placed steps before our feet;
Only step by step <gloss>435</gloss> does one reach the battlements;
It is a foolish wish to conquer them while resting! <gloss>436</gloss>
Hands and feet have surely been granted to you—
Do you wish to withdraw from using them?

Will guess the command without a word being spoken. <gloss>437</gloss>
Like the spade, the hand itself is a command
That your intellect is capable of recognizing.

Wherein you shall find salvation and bliss. <gloss>439</gloss>"

<summary>The Lion rebuts the animals' passivity by arguing that the very possession of hands, feet, and intellect constitutes a divine command from God to exert effort. He uses the metaphor of a master handing a spade to a slave to illustrate that God's gifts imply a duty to work.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Lion’s argument against fatalism. He explains that while human effort is required initially, it eventually leads to a state of divine grace where God "carries" the believer. The commentary explains the transition from active striving to spiritual rest.</meta>

...through continued effort in the service of the Most High, eternal truths will reveal themselves to you; in possessing them, you can renounce striving and surrender yourself to the peaceful enjoyment of the spiritual goods you have won.

You who bore the burden, shall then be borne on and on, 440
You pleasing to Him, as His Word was pleasing to you then.
Receiving His command, you proclaim it, 441
Desiring entrance to Him, you find it!
Just as the thanks for strength—that gift of grace—is action,
So is compulsion <term>compulsion: here referring to fatalism or the belief that humans have no free will</term> ingratitude. 442
Gratitude for that strength increases your strength;
Compulsion snatches the gift of grace away from you. 443
Compulsion is sleep; do not sleep upon the path!
Do not sleep upon the path to His grace! 444
O do not sleep, you who are caught in compulsion,
Until you arrive at that fruit-tree, 445

440. That is: after you have taken upon yourself and borne the full measure of the divine commands, divine love carries you upward to God—just as the wonder-horse Buraq <note>original: "Borāk"; the celestial steed that carried the Prophet in Islamic tradition</note> once carried Muhammad—where you are then as welcome to Him as His commands were to you.

441. Like a seer who preaches the revelation granted to him by God to his own age.

442. Namely, because he who believes in compulsion—that is, in the lack of human freedom in one's actions—and consequently renounces their own effort to worship God, denies the benefit <note>the "benefit" here refers to the potential of human agency</note> bestowed upon him by God through the granting of physical and spiritual faculties.

443. An allusion to the Quranic passage (14:7): "If you are grateful, I will surely give you more; but if you are ungrateful—truly, my punishment is severe!" <note>original: "Wenn ihr dankbar seid, so werde ich euch gewiß noch mehr geben; wenn ihr aber undankbar seid — wahrlich, meine Strafe ist gewaltig!"</note> A person proves gratitude for the faculties granted to them by using them in accordance with the purpose of their bestowal: to carry out the divine commands.

444. Which manifests itself to the striver through the knowledge of eternal truths.

445. That is, the perfect knowledge of the divine essence—as it were, the final station of the earthly pilgrimage of the sage wrestling for truth. The thought is borrowed from a saying of Muhammad (Hadith) <note>original: "Hadis"</note>, which is told as follows: Once Muhammad spoke: "If you find a tree from the trees of Paradise, then sit down in its shadow..."

<summary>The Lion argues that true gratitude to God consists of using one's God-given faculties (action) rather than passively claiming "compulsion" or fatalism. He describes how initial human effort eventually yields to a state of being "carried" by divine love.</summary>

Then the forest animals cried out with one voice: 450
"Truly, man and woman have always, out of greed,

...and eat of its fruits. They (those present) spoke: "How is this possible in this world, O Apostle of God?" — He spoke: "When you find a wise person; for it is no different than if you found a tree from the trees of Paradise." 

446. This image has the following meaning: the acceptance of a law of necessity in human actions and the associated abandonment of one's own effort delivers one defenseless into the power of selfishness and carnal lusts, which are always lurking and threatening destruction—like a pilgrim who carelessly falls asleep in the middle of a perilous path toward a holy goal. 

447. That is, the bird whose wings have not yet grown strong; just as the bird is unable to escape the destruction threatening it from all sides, so too is the person who carelessly exposes themselves to the dangers of Satanic whispers before they have reached the highest stage of earthly perfection. 

448. That is, if you despise the commands of God, which include the granting of your physical powers, you may believe you are facing them with manly strength, yet you are but a weak woman yielding to lusts. <note>In this historical context, "manly" often symbolized spiritual vigor and "womanly" symbolized the soul's surrender to lower desires.</note>

449. Literally: The head from which the intellect flees becomes a tail; that is, the abandonment of the <term>Entelechy: the vital force directing an organism toward self-fulfillment</term> places the noblest and most useful part of the human body on a level with the lowest and most useless.

## Parable. 453

At the hour of dawn, there once stepped a noble guest
With anxious haste into Solomon’s palace,

Measure all earthly deeds by this!
Clear your eye and open it to see!

<note>Notes regarding the text and life:</note>
455. That is, since the destiny decreed over you by God is inseparably connected with you and is, so to speak, one and the same as you, a liberation from it is impossible; the attempt to bring this about is a transgression against the Almighty.
456. The Lion acknowledges the views of the forest animals regarding the pursuit of advantages in the transient, earthly world—that is, earthly endeavor—but draws attention to the difference between this and the divine endeavor, which is directed toward advantages in the eternal, spiritual world. Since this was in use among the prophets and the saints, it cannot be unprofitable or unsuccessful.
457. As a "counter-ruse" <note>original: *Gegenlist*</note> against the seductions of selfishness, in order to make them harmless.
458. The nets they set were intended for the birds of heaven; that is, they applied all their wisdom to the investigation of divine truths.
459. That is, in their earthly, seemingly negative existence, they grasped the positive, Absolute Being.
460. Contrary to what the forest animals had claimed.
461. In a spiritual sense. The meaning is: that striving in the service of God could ever diminish a person’s true salvation is as impossible as that I should ever call myself an unbeliever. It contains an allusion to the Quranic passage (3, 165): God does not allow the reward of the believers to be lost—

<summary>This page presents the Lion’s counter-argument in the debate over free will, asserting that spiritual striving (effort) is not an opposition to fate, but rather a fulfillment of it as practiced by prophets.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Lion's discourse from the previous page. The Lion is arguing against the forest animals' passive reliance on destiny, explaining that true spiritual "striving" is not a defiance of God's will, but the very path God has commanded for the soul to find its way back to Him.</meta>

Your head is not wounded; why the bandages? 462
After a brief burden, turn toward delight and rest! 463
He chooses the bad who builds upon this world,
He chooses the right who looks toward the hereafter.
Dead is the cunning that strives for earthly things—
Prosperous is that which lifts us from the earthly. 464
Escaping from imprisonment, that is what I call cunning;
A fool is he who locks himself in the dungeon.
O you prisoner in the world’s prison,
Seek for yourself a way out of the distress!
What I call "the world" is the forgetting of God,
Not what is measured out to us in possessions and goods. 465
Of well-earned wealth, that gift from God,

---

462. The meaning is: Why do you wish to surrender the free use of your limbs when they do not lack the capacity for work? The word <term>jabr</term> <note>original German: *dschebr*; from the Arabic *jabr*, meaning "compulsion" or "predestination" in theology, but literally "the setting of a broken bone."</note> used by Islamic philosophers to designate that state of man's lack of freedom in his actions has the basic meaning: to set a fracture, which is alluded to in this passage. <note>The Lion is mocking the animals for acting as if they are "broken" or "paralyzed" by fate when they are actually healthy and capable of action.</note>

463. That is, after you have borne the burden of divine commandments during the short earthly life, you can hope for eternal enjoyment of rest and bliss after attaining spiritual freedom.

464. That is, the "cunning" which frees us from worry about the transitory things that fall prey to death. It is prosperous because it procures for us the spiritual goods from which salvation and happiness grow for all eternity, while the other kind of cunning is ruinous because it wins us only brief earthly pleasures, which nevertheless distract our minds from eternal truths.

465. That is, the "world," which is called reprehensible and ruinous in ascetic writings and from which man should free himself, is not earthly wealth itself, but the attachment to it, which drives the thought of the Higher and Divine out of the heart. <term>Sa’di</term> <note>A famous 13th-century Persian poet.</note> expresses the same thought, which testifies to a great purification of religious views, in the <term>Rose Garden</term> <note>The *Gulistan*, Sa’di’s most famous work.</note> as follows:

> Asceticism does not lie in a woolen coat; dress
> As you wish, there are also dervishes in silk. <insert>[Fr. R.]</insert> <note>The initials of the German translator, Friedrich Rückert.</note>

In the same sense, <term>Muhammad</term> is said to have answered the question of what this world is: “Everything that distracts you from your [Lord/God]...” <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence at the end of the page.</note>

<summary>In this passage, the Lion distinguishes between "cunning" used for worldly gain and "cunning" used for spiritual liberation. He clarifies that worldliness is defined by a forgetfulness of God rather than the mere possession of material wealth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Lion’s philosophical discourse from the previous page, where he argues that human agency and "striving" are divine gifts rather than acts of defiance against fate. "...makes one turn away from the Lord, that is your (earthly) world." 466. This saying in full is: "How lovely is righteous wealth for the righteous man!" <note>original: *„Wie lieblich ist das rechtliche Gut für den rechtlichen Mann!“* This is a reference to a Hadith (saying of the Prophet Muhammad).</note> Earthly possessions, therefore, do not harm a person in regard to their salvation any more than their efforts do, provided they know how to value them in their vanity <note>In this context, "vanity" refers to the transitory, fleeting nature of the material world.</note> and do not allow themselves to be drawn away from God by them. 467. That is, just as possessions are helpful to a person in the worship of God and in the good works prescribed by religion, as long as they do not penetrate his heart and defile it, they are likewise destructive as soon as they begin to alienate his mind from God and draw it toward themselves. <note>The poet Rumi often uses the metaphor of water and a boat: water under the boat helps it travel, but water inside the boat sinks it.</note> 468. Not the lack of possessions, but the contempt for possessions is true wisdom, just as pride and defiance based on earthly things is seen as the greatest folly. Therefore Solomon—even though the whole world was his, and not only humans and animals, but also the <term>genii</term> and the <term>Dives</term> <note>original: *Dive*; from the Persian *div*, referring to ancient supernatural spirits, demons, or giants from Zoroastrian and Islamic folklore.</note> recognized him as their king—called himself only poor and in need of God's grace. 469. That is, in earthly passions and lusts. The image is of the inflated...

Would spare them, if to the contract they were true,

So whoever the lot fell upon hurried each day,
As fast as a tiger, to where the Powerful One lay.

Now, when the cup was offered to the Hare, 470

Sacrifice our lives to our pact every hour?

Did not understand the greatness of the little eye-man.” 472

Taken from animal skins, which in the Orient are used for the construction of rafts (<term>Kelek</term> <note>The OCR text says "Relek," but this likely refers to the *Kelek*, a traditional raft made of inflated animal skins used on rivers like the Tigris</note>). 
470. Meaning: when it was the Hare's turn to offer himself to the Lion as food. 
471. Meaning the pupil of the eye. Even the ancient Hebrews called it <term>ishon</term> <note>Hebrew: אִישׁוֹן, literally "little man"</note>, the “little man,” apparently because the person standing opposite sees their own tiny reflection within it—a naming convention that modern Middle Eastern languages have retained. Thus, for example, <term>Abdullāhi Ansārī</term> <note>An 11th-century Persian Sufi poet and sage</note> says:
> A fool is he who considers himself important,
> Who considers himself better than the whole world.
> Learn modesty from the little man of the eye,
> Who looks at everyone else, but not at himself.
<insert>[Fr. R.]</insert> <note>Friedrich Rückert, the German translator and poet</note>
472. The meaning of these two stanzas is as follows: The Prophets appeared, although—as the text says—the path across the horizon...

### 12 Masnavi

Then the people <note>The "people" here refers to the community of animals.</note> spoke: “Pay attention, you donkey-ear! 473
Do not forget that you are only a hare, oh fool!
What chatter is this? To all of us, your betters,
Such a sacrilege has never occurred.
Vengeance, you fop, would soon reach us!
And does such speech suit one like you?”
He spoke: “God revealed the path to me,
He who gives strength of counsel to the weak.
To wild asses and lions was indeed denied
What God taught the little bee,
Who builds houses full of sweet liquid—
The Lord entrusted her with this art.
Similarly, the elephant never understood the art
That God taught the silkworm.
Knowledge, which shines high to heaven’s throne,
He gave to man, the son of the earth. 474
Thus God broke the pride of the angels; and to the penitent
Of six hundred thousand years <note>This refers to Iblis (Satan), who according to tradition worshipped God for hundreds of thousands of years before refusing to bow to Adam.</note> He left,
Since he doubted the Truth, to that blind one,

<column-break/>

perceived outwards (i.e., although they were able to rise above the outermost limits of form into the world of abstract truth), they might appear contemptible and small to their contemporaries, like the pupil of the eye, which, though seemingly insignificant on the outside, is yet the most precious member of the human organism, necessary for recognizing the path of life. 473. The hare is called <term>khargūsh</term> <note>original: "chärgüſch"</note> in Persian because of its long ears, literally meaning “donkey-ear,” which provides our poet with various opportunities for wordplay. I note here that the tame donkey (<term>khär</term>) <note>original: "chär"</note> plays the same contemptible role in Oriental fables as in Western ones, while the wild ass (<term>gūr</term>) <note>original: "gūr"</note> mentioned a few stanzas below—a powerful, untamable animal—is held in such honorable regard that even royal heroes did not disdain to be named after it; even the Bible (Genesis 16:12) says of Ishmael and his tribe: “And he will be a wild ass among men: his hand against everyone and the hand of everyone against him.” 474. I.e., he who was created from clay. Despite this lowly origin, God honored man above all other creatures by giving him the highest of all goods, the knowledge of the...

<summary>The Hare defends his plan to defeat the Lion, arguing that God often grants wisdom and specialized skills to small creatures like bees and silkworms rather than powerful ones. The commentary explains the Persian wordplay on "hare" (donkey-ear) and the cultural distinction between the lowly tame donkey and the noble wild ass.</summary>

eternal Truth, bestowed. 475. The meaning is: the angels, who until then had considered themselves the wisest of creatures, had to admit that the knowledge revealed to Adam surpassed their own; only Iblis <note>The Islamic name for Satan or the Devil.</note> was too proud to prostrate himself before man as God had commanded. Therefore, although he had earned the merit of penance continued through many thousands of years, he was fitted with the muzzle of fallacies and doubts drawn from apparent rational grounds, which keep him forever away from the milk of divine knowledge and the fortress of eternal truths. 476. According to the view of the Easterners <note>original: "Morgenländer"</note>, a drop of blood the size of an eyeball exists in every heart, which is the seat of the animating soul and the perceiving spirit. Of this drop, as the vessel of true knowledge, it is said that it is more precious than all the treasures contained within the sea and the horizon. 477. Abu Jahl <note>original: "Abu Dschehl"</note>, one of the most zealous adversaries of Muhammad and his teaching. Just as Muhammad, because of the revelation granted to him, is very commonly called the noblest of all beings by Eastern writers, so Abu Jahl—whose name, ominous enough, means <term>Father of Folly</term> <note>original: "Torheitsvater"</note>—has become a proverbial designation for a stubborn pagan and opponent of true knowledge. The meaning is thus: Muhammad and Abu Jahl both belonged to the human species in terms of outward form <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing to explain that their spiritual essences were opposites despite their shared human shape.</note>

...and yet, for the former, his knowledge made him the most exalted, while for the latter, his heathen folly made him the most contemptible of all creatures. 478. The text here refers to the dog of the Seven Sleepers of Ephesus <note>A famous legend, also found in the Quran (Surah al-Kahf), about a group of youths who hid in a cave to escape religious persecution and slept for centuries, accompanied by a faithful dog named Qitmir.</note>; compare note 145. Islamic <note>original: "muhammedanische"</note> legend-poetry allows this dog to share in the joys of Paradise for his loyalty; indeed, according to a passage in Sadi <note>Sa'di Shirazi (1210–1291), the celebrated Persian poet.</note>, he is even said to have been transformed into a human (*Rosengarten* <note>original: "Rosengarten," the German title for Sadi's most famous work, the *Gulistan* or "The Rose Garden."</note>, p. 8, Gladw.):

> To Lot’s wife, as she with the wicked
> Associated, was the prophethood <note>In this context, her spiritual standing and salvation.</note> lost;
> To a human became the dog, as the path
> Of the righteous sleepers of the cave he chose.

The knowledge of Truth thus places the despised dog high above the kings of the animal world and gives him equal rank with the noblest being of creation, who after all only elevates himself above the clay from which he was formed through that same knowledge. 479. Literally: The description of form is not in the quills <note>original: "Schreibrohren," referring to the reed pens used in traditional calligraphy.</note>; only “wise” and “just” is in the letters—that is, in the formal titles of address among Muslims <note>original: "Muhammedanern"</note>, the person addressed is never called “great,” “beautiful,” etc., but always “wise,” “understanding,” “just,” and the like. This proof of the superiority of the spiritual over the physical does not appear at all dull or absurd to them—given the sanctity in which the art of writing is held by Muslims—as it might to Western <note>original: "okzidentalischen"</note> readers. The general custom, the Sheikh <note>The author, Rumi.</note> wishes to say, also proves in this case the sublimity of the spirit over the body. 480. In that the spirit [surpasses] the celestial sphere, the size of which... <note>The text ends mid-sentence at the bottom of the page.</note>

Equip yourself with new ears, O fool!
For the ear of a donkey does not understand my word. 481

---

...belongs only to the form, skims over it, and rises toward the Infinite.
481. This means an ear directed only toward that which is perceptible by the sun <note>the material world</note>. The poet intends to say: My words have a deep meaning which only the inner, spiritual ear—not the outer, sensory ear—is capable of grasping; namely, as the following couplet suggests, that no physical power can measure itself against that of the spirit.
482. Oriental legend grants Solomon a magic seal through which he made men and animals subject to him and forced the good and evil spirits of the earth into obedience. The power of this seal, it is said here, is nothing other than true wisdom, the divine knowledge, which relates to the whole of creation as the spirit relates to the body it permeates and enlivens. Thus, he whose heart has been stamped with the seal of wisdom binds the entire world to himself and guides it according to his will. In this same sense, a saying of Muhammad (<term>Hadith</term> <note>a record of the traditions or sayings of the Prophet Muhammad</note>) is cited: "The death of a scholar (<term>ālim</term>) is equal to the death of a whole world (<term>ālem</term>)."
483. The good and evil earthly spirits with which the imagination of the Eastern peoples populates uninhabited regions, deserts, ruins, and unoccupied coasts.
484. Such as the genies, the devils, and <term>Ifrite</term> <note>Ifrits; powerful and often malicious spirits in Islamic mythology</note>, who stir up evil desires within man through...

Upon our hearts, the good and the evil ones. 485
Just as it may happen that when you step into the river
To bathe, a thorn pierces your foot;
Even if you do not see the thorn in the river,
When it stings you, you feel its existence.
Whisperings, temptations, grief, and cares
Come from a thousand beings, 486 hidden from us.
Oh, that with a spiritual eye you might behold those beings,
That the riddles might be unraveled for you,
That you might stay far from the evil word
And choose the good word as your refuge! —

They spoke: "Swift hare, arise! And share
With us what you have devised for our salvation;
You who combat the fierce lion’s power,
Tell us the plan you have thought out.
Counsel provides insight and knowledge;
One person leads another to understanding. 487
Muhammad said: Look for counsel, O wise one,
Look around, and trust him who offers you counsel!" 488

...through which he loses temporal and eternal happiness. 485. According to a saying of Muhammad <note>original: "Hadiß"; a Hadith is a recorded saying or tradition of the Prophet Muhammad</note> reported by Ibn Mas'ud: "Truly Satan has an influence and the angel has an influence on the son of Adam <note>meaning a human being</note>; as for the influence of the angel, it is a promise of what is beneficial and a confirmation of the Truth. Whoever feels this, let him praise God; but whoever feels the other, let him seek refuge with the Lord from Satan!" — 486. Our Sheikh <note>referring to the author, Rumi</note> thus deviates here from the common view of Muslims, according to which the inspiration for good and evil in man is attributed only to the two angels who constantly accompany him on his life's path. 487. That is, in communal consultation, each person shares what seems conducive to their knowledge, and thereby one person helps the other toward correct insight. 488. This saying, reported by Abu Huraira, reads: "The one asked for counsel is trustworthy," that is, if you have confidence in someone...

<meta>This page continues the Hare's response to the other animals. Having been asked to share his plan for defeating the Lion, the Hare begins a discourse on the necessity of secrecy and the dangers of revealing one's intentions too early.</meta>

<page-num>182</page-num>

He spoke: "One does not reveal every plan; 489
Instead of success, failure often falls; instead of failure, success. 490
You see, if you breathe smilingly upon a mirror,
The otherwise so pure mirror is immediately clouded. 491
Of three things the wise man never speaks:
Of gold, of travel plans, and of one's faith; 492
For each has enemies everywhere,
Who set traps for it wherever they scent it.
If even three people have knowledge of the secret,
Then bid it farewell! For then it is in everyone's mouth.
Ahmed <note>Another name for the Prophet Muhammad</note> also, in the circle of his friends,
Always sought counsel in a riddling manner; 493

---

that he will give you good advice, then you should also rely on his pronouncement. 489. According to the Arabic proverb: "The hearts of the noble are the graves of secrets." 490. That is, success often brings exactly the opposite of what one expected. 491. If you entrust the mirror with the secret of your vanity and smile at it, it darkens, as if in anger, from your breath, and the purpose for which you approached it—namely, to see your face in it—is lost through your indiscretion. 492. This thought is also based on a saying of Muhammad <note>original: "Hadiß"; a Hadith is a recorded tradition or saying of the Prophet</note>: "Conceal your gold, your departure, and your path of faith." In Arabic, this combination is even more striking because the words *dhahab* <note>original: "zeheb"; gold</note>, *dhahāb* <note>original: "zehäb"; departure</note>, and *madhhab* <note>original: "mezheb"; school of thought or religious sect</note> are all derived from the same linguistic root. 493. That is, in hints which only intimate friends understand among themselves. The poet here lends the Hare the following line of reasoning: the safest course is always to keep one's secret and plan entirely to oneself; however, if one feels the need for consultation, one should do as Muhammad and his companions did in the manner described here. — I have omitted several couplets <note>original: "Doppelverse"</note> here, which express the same thought through a different image and are thus superfluous to the meaning of the whole. The fact that the present work is already beginning to take on an unintended expansion may excuse this freedom, which I will repeatedly make use of in the future in cases where digressions, which have only a very loose connection to the thread of the narrative, appear to me to be of little poetic value and not very characteristic—

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Hare and the Lion. Following the discussion on the necessity of secrecy and the Prophet's use of allegories, the Hare finally approaches the enraged Lion to deliver his deceptive message.</meta>

<page-num>183</page-num>

"In veils of parables, which not every
Stranger could see through, he hid his speech;
Thus he received an answer from everyone,
And others never guessed the meaning of the question."

After long delay, the Hare then took
His course toward the claw-strong Lion,
Who, awaiting the one who had stayed away so long,
Raged and roared, scratching at the ground,
And spoke: "Did I not say it, that such common folk
Are never able to keep agreements!
Their brazen lies threw me down from my donkey <note>A German idiom meaning to be rudely disillusioned or cheated of one's expectations</note>—
How the lies and deception of this wicked world torment me!" —
Into misery runs the prince who looks neither
Forward nor backward, the ill-advised fool!
Though the path be straight, there are snares within it,
Though the name be beautiful, the meaning is missing! 494
The name is a snare, 495 the sweet word 496

---

appear. 494. The meaning is: Do not let yourself be deceived by outward appearances; for on the path that seems comfortable and level to you, and which you consider a safe means of reaching your goal, many dangers may threaten you. — The second rhyming line is expressed somewhat obscurely; it contains an allusion to the Muhammadan custom <note>Islamic tradition</note> of adopting names with specific meanings, so that the thought can be paraphrased as follows: the circumstance that someone bears the name Ahmed <note>original: "Achmed"; meaning "The Praised One"</note>, Arif <note>meaning "The Knowing One" or "The Wise"</note>, Hassan <note>meaning "The Beautiful" or "The Good"</note> and the like, does not justify the assumption that he is also praised, knowing, or beautiful; these are names whose actual meaning has no relation at all to the individuals designated by them and therefore, in relation to them, as good as does not exist. Likewise, the name "Covenant" <note>original: "Bündnis"</note> is meaningless for the relationship of the forest animals to the Lion if the conditions are no longer kept. 495. That is: the fool lets himself be deceived by such names and considers, for example, the one called Arif (the Knowing One) to be knowledgeable. 496. That is, our word which encloses earthly knowledge and flatters our selfishness, over which we forget the rapidly fleeting, precious time of life, so that it [is caught] therein, like <note>The sentence in the original German source ends abruptly here.</note>

<summary>The Hare finally approaches the Lion, who is enraged by the animals' perceived breach of their agreement. The text warns against being deceived by outward appearances or beautiful names that lack substance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical reflection following the encounter between the Hare and the Lion. ->The sand in which our life’s moisture withers away.<-
->Surely from another sand this moisture springs, 497<-
->Strive for it, the rare one, without ceasing!<-
->For this sand is one who, for the sake of the Holy,<-
->Renounces the Earthly—the Prosperous one,<-
->The pious one, from whom the moisture of faith flows,<-
->This sweet moisture, which fills you with life. 498<-
->The god-forgotten one resembles the parched sand,<-
->In which your life’s moisture always vanished. 499<-
->Therefore, seek the trace of wisdom with the wise man,<-
->For he only makes you knowing and seeing;<-
->The seeker of wisdom becomes the wellspring of wisdom,<-
->Free from the toil of striving, the chosen one; 500<-
->The tablet that protected is now protected,<-
->Spirit is revealed to it from God’s Spirit. 501<-

Water in the glowing sand of the desert dries up. 497. Not every word is like such water-consuming sand; for that which contains true divine wisdom holds the water of eternal life and therefore resembles the sand from which a spring arises. 498. Namely, with spiritual, eternal life. 499. The meaning is: the speeches and deeds of the godless lead you away from God and make you dead to Him, while you should only be alive in Him. 500. That is, when a person, under the guidance of his spiritual mentor (<term>Murshid</term> <note>original: "Murschid"; a teacher or guide in the Sufi tradition</note>), has passed through all stages of the quest for knowledge (<term>Gnosis</term>), he finally attains that perfection where he no longer requires instruction from others, but where divine knowledge reveals itself to him through revelation. While he previously thirsted for wisdom, he then becomes the source himself, from which others draw the water of eternal life. 501. The meaning coincides with that of the previous couplet. The "protecting tablet" is the heart, into which a person writes the divine commandments and eternal teachings of wisdom to guard them against the unbelief that threatens to tear them away. This tablet, when a person has reached the level of earthly perfection, is protected by God Himself against everything that could rob it of its brilliance and purity. The poet alludes here to the Tablet <note>The "Preserved Tablet" or *al-lawh al-mahfuz* in Islamic theology, which contains the original of the Quran and the decrees of God</note> upon which, according to the view of the...

--- Page 200 ---
<meta>This page continues the mystical interpretation of the journey of the soul, moving from the concept of the "well-guarded tablet" to the relationship between human intellect and divine revelation. It uses the "Night Journey" of the Prophet Muhammad as a central metaphor for the limits of reason.</meta>

<page-num>185</page-num>

If in the Intellect man once found instruction, 
Then the Intellect becomes his pupil, 502
And speaks like Gabriel: “Ahmed, I burn, 
If I run one step further with you! 
Leave me behind and hasten on yourself, 
For this is my limit, O Guide of Souls!” 503
O You who secretly nourish the winds, renew 
The devotion to God, but not with words alone! 504

Muslims believe the fate of every human being has been recorded in the beginningless eternity upon a surface which bears the name <term>the well-guarded tablet</term> <note>original: "louh-i-mahfüz"; in Islamic theology, the Al-Lawh al-Mahfuz is the celestial tablet upon which God's decrees are written</note>; he thus means to say: the spirit of man is unerring, like the divine spirit revealing itself to him, after he has progressed through all stations of striving to the highest earthly completion. 502. The Intellect [Verstand] was given to man as a teacher and guide in his earthly actions and omissions and in his earthly science; it is the light that shines before him on his life's path until he reaches the state of the highest earthly perfection. But when man has entered into this state, then a stronger light shines for him—the revealing divine knowledge—which he follows by outstripping the Intellect and pulling it behind him. 503. This refers to the well-known vision of Muhammad, in which he saw himself carried on the miraculous steed <term>Buraq</term> <note>original: "Borāk"</note>, accompanied by the Archangel Gabriel, from Mecca to Jerusalem and from there through all seven heavens, and to which the Quranic verse (17:1) alludes: 

> Praised be He who led His servant by night from the holy place of prayer (Temple at Mecca) to the furthest place of prayer (Temple at Jerusalem), whose surroundings We have blessed—so that We might show him of Our miracles. Truly, He is the All-Hearing and the All-Seeing!

— Just as the Archangel remained behind the enlightened man—who, as the noblest of God's creatures, could venture closest to His throne—so as not to burn in the rays of pure light, so also do the divine revelations, which the seeker partakes of at the last station of earthly life, leave the Intellect behind as the earthly part of the human spirit. 504. That is: convert to God in spirit and in truth, and take the confession of the lips into your heart; otherwise, your faith is only hypocrisy, merely a word without inner meaning.

<summary>This page explores the limits of human reason (Intellect) in the face of divine revelation, using the metaphor of the Archangel Gabriel stopping at the boundary of the divine presence during Muhammad's Night Journey. It emphasizes that true faith must move beyond mere words into the heart.</summary>
<keywords>Intellect vs. Revelation, Archangel Gabriel, Muhammad, Buraq, Night Journey (Mi'raj), Well-guarded Tablet, Sufi psychology, hypocrisy vs. sincerity</keywords>

--- Page 201 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on the limitations of human intellect when clouded by ego and worldly desire. Having previously discussed the "Night Journey," the text now uses the parable of the fly on a puddle to illustrate how individual whim distorts divine revelation.</meta>

<page-num>186</page-num>

Faith withers when the lusts begin to sprout, 505
Which shut the gates of Paradise to you.
The pure Word, you twist it here and there— 506
Leave its meaning be, and change your own mind!
For by explaining God’s Book to suit your whim,
Distorting and destroying its high sense,
It fares with you as with the conceited fly,
Who fancies herself great, the jewel of the cosmos,
Who, intoxicated by herself above the wine, 507
Thinks she is a sun, though she is but a tiny speck!
Who, when told of the eagle,
Cries out: “I am the Anka 508 of this world!”—
Upon a puddle, a little straw of hay
Serves as her ship; as pilot she sits there, proud and glad.
“A ship,” she says, “I know how to steer across the sea,
For I have long pondered this matter;
Here is an ocean, a vessel;—I shall command it,
I shall lead it as an experienced helmsman!”
Thus the fly drives her raft upon this sea,
To her, the small is infinitely vast,
The puddle is without limit to her eye—
For where can mere thought grasp the Truth!
As far as her gaze reaches, that is her world,

---

505. Namely, because the power of one thing in your heart excludes the power of the other <note>meaning that worldly desire and true faith cannot occupy the heart simultaneously</note>. 506. That is, the Quran and the sayings of the Prophet. You turn the word of God this way and that to lend it a meaning with which you might excuse your worldly disposition, which clings to base desires. 507. That is, by only flying over the wine without tasting it <note>a metaphor for someone who talks about spiritual intoxication or "wine" without ever actually experiencing the divine ecstasy themselves</note>. 508. According to the scholia to Hariri (p. 594), a giant bird, known by name but unknown in body, which owes its name (from the Arabic word <term>unq</term> <note>original: "’unk"</note>, meaning neck) according to some to a collar-like white spot on the neck, and according to others to the length of the latter. — The Persians conflate the <term>Anka</term> with a being taken from their mythological tales, the bird <term>Simurgh</term>, which resides on <term>Mount Qaf</term> <note>the mythological mountain at the edge of the world in Islamic cosmology</note> and is considered the king of all birds.

**Vocabulary:** Faith, Gate of Paradise, God’s Book, Anka, Quran, Prophet, Hariri, Simurgh, Mount Qaf, Fly, Universe

<summary>This passage utilizes the famous parable of the fly on a straw in a puddle to satirize those who interpret scripture through the lens of their own ego and desires. It warns that such people mistake their limited perspective for absolute truth, much like a fly imagining its puddle to be a vast ocean.</summary>

<keywords>mysticism, ego, interpretation of scripture, Quranic exegesis, Anka, Simurgh, spiritual delusion, arrogance</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the allegory of the fly, who mistakes a puddle for an ocean and herself for a master navigator. It transitions into a new section of the poem where a Lion, representing the soul or the misled intellect, realizes he has been deceived by the clever ruses of smaller creatures (the forest animals), specifically the Hare from the famous Kalila and Dimna fables often referenced by Rumi.</meta>

<page-num>187</page-num>

The puddle is her sea, for her eye can hold no more.
So too is the mind of the vain interpreter:
Just a puddle with a little straw inside.
If the fly could free itself from its delusion, 509
It would become a Phoenix!
She remains a fly no longer, who rises above delusion,
For her spirit hovers high above her body.
Can the spirit of the Hare—who did violence
To the Lion—be contained in its dwarf-like form? —

---

“Through the mediation of my ear,” the Lion spoke 
In a rage, “my enemy has blindfolded me. 510
The cunning of these tiny captives 511 has bound me;
Their wooden sword has scourged my body.
I will no longer listen to their speeches;
It is but goblin-whispering 512 meant to beguile me!
O do not delay, my heart, tear them apart quickly,
Tear their skins, for they are nothing but skins!” — 513

---

509. Namely, by recognizing how little her subjective thinking was adequate to objective being. The meaning is: if the fly could free itself from its petty narrow-mindedness, it would be equal to the most perfect being of the animal world, the Phoenix (<term>Huma: a legendary bird of Persian mythology said to never alight on the ground and to bestow kingship</term>), which is even able to distinguish right from wrong. Compare Saadi in the Rose Garden <note>original: *Rosengarten*, referring to the *Gulistan* by the Persian poet Saadi Shirazi</note>, translated by Gladwin, p. 21:

> The Phoenix is the noblest of birds,
> Because it eats only bones and harms no living thing.

The reference to humans—whom only freedom from worldly disposition elevates to their intended rank above the angels—is clear. 510. That is: deceptive promises have made me blind. 511. That is, the forest animals. 512. That is, the promises of the forest animals, which moved me to renounce my former means of sustenance, are like the deceptive calls of the <term>Divs: demonic spirits in Persian folklore</term> and <term>Ghouls: desert-dwelling shapeshifters</term>, who lure the traveler away from the straight path into error. 513. This can refer both to the forest animals themselves and to their deceptive promises, both of which are depicted as empty skins or sacks filled only with air.

<vocab>Deutler (Interpreter/Squinter), Phoenix, Huma, Saadi, Rosengarten (Rose Garden/Gulistan), Leu (Lion), Koboldgeraune (Goblin-whispering), Dive (Divs), Ghule (Ghouls), Schläuche (Skins/Sacks)</vocab>

be. 514. An allusion to the saying of Muhammad (<term>Hadith: a recorded tradition of the Prophet</term>): <note>original: "Das Wissen und der Reichtum verhüllen jedes Gebrechen."</note> "Knowledge and wealth conceal every defect." 515. Namely, from the eyes of the common crowd, so that it is only revealed to the Lord’s elect, who strive for it through renunciation and faithful observation of the divine commandments. 516. Figuratively, the stylus with which man writes his vain hopes upon the water of inconstancy. 517. The <term>Khutbah: the public intercessory prayer spoken on Fridays in the mosque for the current ruler</term>, which in the Orient is among the prerogatives of sovereignty. 518. That is, it is subject to change and destruction, while the luster of the visionary is as imperishable as the source from which it comes. 519. That is, upon the death of every ruler, a new stamp with a changed name is cut for the coins of his successor, while Muhammad’s name is retained in every coinage. The latter refers to the custom observed by Muslims in the earlier centuries after the <term>Hijra: the migration or "flight" of Muhammad from Mecca to Medina</term> to strike upon the coins the phrase: "There is no god but God alone; He has no partner; Mu=

--- Page 204 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse on spiritual authority, transitioning from the symbolic "coins" of the Prophet Muhammad to a narrative sequence involving the Hare and the Lion, which serves as a springboard for a deeper meditation on the Universal Intellect (Aql).</meta>

<page-num>189</page-num>

->Yet every coin bears Ahmed’s name,<-
<note>Ahmed is another name for Muhammad, the Prophet of Islam.</note>
->And Ahmed alone names all the visionaries,<-
->Where a hundred stands, there is no need for ninety. 520<-

---

When he had spun out his path for a long time
And had artfully devised his plan,
The Hare set out from that place to announce
Deeply hidden words to the Lion.
For 521 within the Intellect <term>Aql: the faculty of reason or the Universal Mind</term> a world-army stirs,—
Infinitely deep is the ocean of the Intellect,
This sweet sea, which bears all formation
And moves it, as a wave moves a jar.
When the jar is empty, it floats upon the wave,
But once filled, it sinks swiftly to the depths. 522
Upon the Intellect’s hidden sea, the striking of waves
Is but the form within nature.
Should a form seek a path to draw near
To the sea, it pushes it far from this path,

520. <note>original: "Muhammed ist der Apostel Gottes."</note> Muhammad is the Apostle of God. This means it is sufficient to name Muhammad, the last of the prophets, alone, rather than naming all the bearers of divine revelation; since he is like the larger numerical unit that includes all smaller ones within itself, he encompasses all the others. 521. Here, the poet makes a bold leap from the cleverness of the Hare to the Universal Intellect, which encompasses and penetrates all of creation and from which all stirring and movement originates. Just as divine knowledge was previously compared to a sea, here the Intellect is compared to a sea—as an element that is clear in itself but resistant to all fixed "forms." In this sea, to which the world of forms is merely like a wave-crest—forming one moment and vanishing the next—the individual being is driven about aimlessly like a jar among waves as long as it remains a stranger to the water. 522. That is to say, when a person has soaked themselves full of the "water" of the Intellect and has thus become familiar with it, they are no longer tossed unsteadily by the waves of the world of forms, but instead sink down into the depths of the spiritual world, far from all sensory perception.

<vocab>Achmed, Muhammed, Weltenheer, Verstand, Form, Natur, Gotterkenntnis, Schöpfung</vocab>

<summary>This page explores the Sufi concept of the Universal Intellect (Aql), using the metaphor of an infinite ocean where physical forms are merely transient waves. It explains that spiritual maturity involves being "filled" with this divine reason to move beyond the surface of the material world.</summary>

<keywords>Muhammad, Universal Intellect, Aql, Sufi metaphysics, allegory of the Hare and Lion, spiritual depth, world of forms</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of divine knowledge, specifically focusing on how the Intellect (Aql) mediates between the Creator and the created world of forms. It concludes a thought from the previous page regarding knowledge of God that abstracts from sensory perception.</meta>

<page-num>190</page-num>

->Until the heart beholds the Giver of Souls,<-
->Him who sends arrows from afar. 523<-

->Invisible because it is too near, like the moisture<-
->Within the belly of the jar, is the breath of life within us. 524<-

->You would not know what is green, red, or blue<-
->If the three-fold light did not shine upon you. 525<-

->But if your spirit goes astray in red and green, 526<-
->Then red and green will hide that light from you.<-
->The fact that every color is veiled in dark of night<-
->Shows how light is the source of all color's splendor.<-

---

...knowledge of God that abstracts from sensory perception. 523. That is, the Intellect <term>Aql: the higher faculty of reason or Universal Mind</term> rejects every means by which the individual might attempt to penetrate it without first reaching true knowledge of God through the reflection of that Intellect illuminating their spirit. — The second epithet <note>original: "Epitheton"</note> describing God is somewhat obscure; according to the usual rules of grammar, it would have to be translated as: "the arrow of the one who strikes from afar." However, linguistic usage allows Persian poets great freedom, and my translation can also be justified through parallelism <note>The structural balance between lines of poetry</note>. The meaning seems to me to be: He who penetrates the most remote thing—that is, the heart of the individual being—with His effects. 524. The soul breathed into us by God, the imperishable part of our being, for which the earthly body is merely the vessel. We do not see our soul because it is within us, just as one sees the jar, but not the liquid stored inside it. 525. Namely, first, the light of the sun, the moon, etc.; second, the light of the eye; and third, as the following verses seem to suggest, the cognitive and discerning light of the spirit—not that of a lamp, as the commentary suggests, which would exclude itself from the sun's light. Just as the invisible sea of Intellect is sensed in its effects—the surging movements of the world of forms—and as the soul, the divine breath of man, is sensed in life's functions, so too is the likewise invisible light perceived within colors. 526. The concrete appearances of the world of colors and forms are, as it were, a veil that hides pure light from you, and which you can only lift through abstraction <note>The process of mentally separating a quality from the object it belongs to</note>, by freeing your spirit from the confusing variety...

<summary>This page uses the metaphors of a jar's contents and the relationship between light and color to explain how the Divine presence and the human soul remain invisible due to their extreme proximity. It argues that physical colors are only visible because of light, yet they simultaneously distract the observer from the source of that light.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of light and perception, moving from physical sight to the "inner light" of the heart and the necessity of opposites (like sorrow and joy) for understanding the divine. It follows the previous page's exploration of how sensory colors can sometimes veil the underlying light.</meta>

<page-num>191</page-num>

...abstracting from multiplicity.

->Outer color shines forth from the outer light 527,<-
->The inner light 528 paints the images of the imagination.<-
->The former streams from the stars, both great and small, 529<-
->The latter is the reflection of the Light of Glory. 530<-
->The light of the heart is the source of the eye's light;<-
->Only through this light is our eye brightened. 531<-
->Far from the light of the senses and the intellect,<-
->The light of the heart shines forth from the Light of the Lord.<-
->In the dark of night, you see no color,<-
->Thus, the opposite makes the light clear.<-
->First you perceive the light, then the color—<-
->Light becomes clear to you through its opposite.<-
->So God also created sorrow and suffering for a purpose,<-
->So that joy and pleasure might be awakened by their opposite.<-
->The opposite makes clear the invisible—<-

<note>527. This refers to the light that illuminates physical objects for the sake of sensory perception, which is shared by both humans and animals.</note>

<note>528. This refers to the spiritual power through which a person understands how to distinguish good from evil, the beneficial from the ruinous, and generally penetrates the concept of every thing.</note>

<note>529. In the original text: from the sun and the <term>Suhā: "The Forgotten One," a tiny star in the constellation Ursa Major</term>. The Arabs call a small star located next to the middle tail-star of the Great Bear <note>original: "Großen Bären"</note> "El Suhā." In his description of the stars, the scholar al-Qazwini <note>original: "Kaswini"</note> (referencing Ideler, *Star Names*, p. 376) says that people use it to test the sharpness of their eyesight. It is therefore a particularly well-known small celestial body, which our poet contrasts here with the great light of day.</note>

<note>530. The meaning is: the inner light—the reason <note>original: "Vernunft"</note> that distinguishes good and evil—is granted to us through the direct influence of God's corresponding attributes upon our spirit. In contrast, the outer light, even if it stems from the same source, reaches us only through the mediation of the stars.</note>

<note>531. That is: only through reason does a person recognize the true essence of the objects of their perception. Without it, one would stand on the same level as cattle, which are unable to rise from the individual thing to a conceptual understanding, and therefore see in grass only fodder and in water only something to drink.</note>

<summary>This page explores the Sufi concept of "inner light" versus "outer light," arguing that spiritual reason (the heart's light) is required to truly understand the world. It also introduces the philosophical principle that opposites—like suffering and joy—are necessary for human perception of divine truths.</summary>

<keywords>inner light, outer light, Suha star, al-Qazwini, reason, perception of opposites, spiritual vs. physical sight, Rumi</keywords>
