<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion from page 191 regarding the nature of perception. It transitions from the physical necessity of light and shadow to the metaphysical concept that the Divine remains "hidden" precisely because He is an absolute totality without an opposite to define Him. It then moves into a metaphor about the relationship between internal thought and external speech.</meta>

<page-num>192</page-num>

The All-True One remains dark, for He lacks His opposite. <note>532</note>
For as darkness teaches us about the light,
One opposite explains the other;
But the Light of God has no opposite
That could reveal it to our sight.

As the lion leaps from the forest thicket,
So the word breaks free from the thought. <note>533</note>
From the ocean of thoughts, the sound emerges—
The word—but who has ever beheld this sea?
Yet when you see the lovely wave of His speech,
You know that the source itself is clear and bright.
Thought arises in the ocean of knowledge
And enters the boundaries of structure as a word;
In the word, form sprouts <note>534</note> and then passes away,
The waves pull it back home toward the ocean. <note>535</note>
Just as the Universe is in the Lord, so form is lost
Within the Formless that gave it birth. —

The universe is constantly perishing,
Only to arise again, forever new; <note>536</note>

---

...only sees a means to quench its momentary thirst. <note>original: "nur ein Mittel sieht, seinen augenblicklichen Durst zu stillen." This is the conclusion of note 531 from the previous page.</note>

<note>532. The concept of an opposite requires a certain equality on the other side; God, therefore, can have no opposite, for the "All" <note>the universe</note> is within Him and He permeates the All. Because He is thus within the All, He remains invisible to it.</note>

<note>533. The forest represents the Unknown and Inaccessible, of whose interior one gets a glimpse only when one sees the lion leaping out of it and then seeking refuge within it again. Similarly, thought is an unfathomable element, of which the word—as a suddenly appearing and then vanishing concrete phenomenon—gives only an obscure report.</note>

<note>534. Specifically, the sound of the word, which, as something perceptible to the senses, belongs to the world of forms.</note>

<note>535. That is: as soon as the sound of the word fades away—according to the law of transience to which all formed things are subject—the thought reabsorbs the meaning of the word as a property borrowed from it.</note>

<note>536. That is: the world of forms is nothing but a constant succession of concrete appearances, which then disappear just as quickly as they emerged, giving us the impression of something permanent,</note>

<summary>This page explores the Sufi metaphysical concept that God is hidden from human sight because He has no opposite to define Him, while also using the metaphor of an ocean to describe how thoughts manifest as temporary "waves" of spoken words.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse from page 192 regarding the illusory nature of permanence. It concludes the metaphor of the universe as a sequence of discrete moments that appear continuous, much like a point of fire spun in a circle, before returning to the narrative of the Lion and the Hare.</meta>

Changing and yet resting at all times,
It changes its dress every moment.
The world renews itself at all times; yet we do not see
The new birth within that which seems permanent. <note>537</note>
Like a stream, life flows ever fresh
And appears to us uninterrupted and smooth;
It shows itself as a line in rapid flight, <note>538</note>
Like the path of a swung spark;
A firebrand appears as a streak
If one lets it circle and sweep through the air.
That the point appears as a long line is caused by movement,
By the swift stimulation of our senses.
O wise man, you who struggle to explain this,
Only my <term>Hussām</term> <note>Husam al-Din Chalapi, Rumi's close companion and the one who inspired the writing of the Masnavi</note> can instruct you!

---

Then finally the <term>Leu</term> <note>Archaic German term for Lion, used here to maintain the poetic tone</note>, burning with rage,
Saw how the hare approached him from afar,
Coming fearlessly, even boldly,
With a wrathful gesture, wild and furious.
For anxiety arouses suspicion,
But boldness strikes every doubt silent.
When he had reached the vicinity of the throne,
The lion cried: "Woe to you, you wicked one, woe!
Is it not I who subdues elephants,
I who wrestles with mighty lions?
How can such a hare dare
To defy my command, to break it?
Awaken from your <term>hare-sleep</term> <note>539</note>, careless fool!
The lion roars, you donkey, into your ear!" —

---

...make firm. <note>537. That is: the duration of the change prevents us from perceiving the constant renewal.</note> <note>538. While it actually consists only of a sequence of individual points, i.e., momentary phenomena.</note> <note>539. That is, from sleep with open eyes, which robs the person afflicted by it of self-awareness, although he [perceives] the external ap[pearance]...</note>

<meta>This page follows the transition from page 193, where the Lion (referred to as the "Leu") is enraged by the Hare's delay. The Hare spoke: “By God! You will not find me
Guilty, if you only listen to me graciously.”
The Lion spoke: “Fool, what use are pious words?
Is this the time to come before the <term>Shah</term>? <note>original: "Schah"; a Persian title for a king or sovereign</note>
Your head is at stake, you time-forgetting cock! <note>540. An allusion to the Persian proverb: The cock that crows at the wrong time must have its head cut off.</note>
A fool’s excuse—who would listen to it?
Worse than his crime is the fool’s
Excuse, <note>541. This is also a proverbial expression, meaning that a fool makes a mistake look even worse through his clumsy attempts to explain it.</note> a poison for wise ears!
I would be a <term>long-ear</term> <note>original: "Langohr"; a common metaphor for a donkey, implying stupidity or gullibility</note> myself, if I chose to listen
To your empty excuses, Hare!” —
He spoke: “Grant me just one little word,
And hear what shame has befallen me.
By the luck and blessing of your sovereignty!
Do not drive me, a lost soul, from your paths.
For even if every stream receives its moisture from the sea, <note>542. Referring to the natural cycle where vapors rise from the sea to fall as rain and fill the springs that feed the rivers. Despite this vast generosity, the sea is still mindful of even a tiny straw, allowing it to float rather than sink.</note>
There is no straw that the sea does not carry back to the surface;
The sea does not diminish this grace,
It brings it neither benefit nor harm.”
The Lion spoke: “I grant grace according to place and time;
I cut the clothes of the people according to their stature.” <note>543. Meaning: I dispense my favor according to the specific circumstances and the character of the individual.</note>
Then the Hare replied: “Hear me, and see if I am not worthy of grace—
Though the sword of wrath hangs over my head!
Early this morning I set out in another’s
Company, to journey to the royal court.
For the forest animals had provided a second hare
To accompany me for you, my Lord.

<note>Footnote 540: Allusion to the Persian proverb: The unseasonable (i.e., crowing at the wrong time) cock must have its head cut off.</note>
<note>Footnote 541: This is also a proverbial expression, which says that the fool puts a mistake he has made in an even worse light through his clumsy excuses.</note>
<note>Footnote 542: By the vapors rising from the sea, which fill the springs with water as rain. Despite this generosity of the sea towards the rivers and streams, no straw is so contemptible to it that it would let it sink to the bottom.</note>
<note>Footnote 543: That is: I [measure out grace...]</note>

“Suddenly a <term>lion</term> <note>original: "Leu"; an archaic German word for lion</note> came upon us,
Ambushing us, the traveling companions.
I said: ‘We are the King of Kings’
Faithful servants, obedient and submissive.’
He said: ‘And who is he? How do you dare
To speak to me of such a worthless creature?
I shall swiftly tear both you and your <term>Shah</term> <note>original: "Schah"; a Persian title for a king or sovereign</note> to pieces,
If you think of retreating from my threshold.’
I said: ‘Just for today, let me approach the face
Of the Shah, so that I may bring word of you.’
He said: ‘Then leave the other here as a guarantor,
Otherwise I shall deem it lawful to throttle you.’
My pleading was in vain; he kept my companion
Beside him and released only me.
In tender, sweet, fatty flesh, my companion
Offered three times as much as I, my Lord.
The path to you is henceforth blocked by that lion;
This is how things stand, I report it to you faithfully.
From now on, give up all hope of sustenance!
I speak the truth, and—bitter is the truth. <note>544. The Hare uses this proverb to make his lie sound more convincing, as if he is pained to deliver bad news.</note>
If you still wish for your daily meal, then drive
The robber away, so that the road remains clear.’”

He spoke: “Well then, we shall go to him;
Lead the way, and if you speak the truth—we shall see—
Then I shall punish him—yes, a hundred of his kind!
But if you lie, then my wrath shall strike you.”

So as a guide, the Hare led that Lion,
In order to devote him to his doom,
Toward the snare he had laid for him,
By the edge of a well known to the Hare.
Deceptive as water covered with chaff, <note>A metaphor for a trap that looks like solid ground but is actually a deep pit or water. This refers to the Hare’s plan to show the Lion his own reflection in the water.</note>
The Hare approached the well, and with him the Lion.

*Deal with everyone according to their merit. 544. A saying of <insert>Muhammad</insert>* <note>original: "Muham="; an abbreviation for the Prophet Muhammad. The German note reflects a moral maxim often cited in Sufi literature regarding justice and consequences.</note>

<meta>This page continues the story of the Hare leading the Lion to a deep well. The Hare is using clever metaphors and historical/religious allusions to justify how a small creature like himself can overthrow a mighty ruler like the Lion.</meta>

<page-num>196</page-num>

Though the flood may carry the chaff to the plain, 545
Yet it never moves the mountains from their place!
For the lion, the hare’s cunning became a snare—
Oh, that the hare’s deceit might overcome the lion!
Thus Pharaoh with his army and baggage 546
Once pursued Moses into the waves of the Nile;
And a gnat, small and weak of wing, 547
Mercilessly pierced the skull of Nimrod.

<note>original: "Hadiß"</note> <term>Hadith</term> <note>A Hadith is a traditional account of the words or deeds of the Prophet Muhammad; here it likely refers to a proverbial saying or tradition used by the author.</note> 545. Namely, from the <term>threshing floor</term> <note>original: "Dreschtenne"</note>, which is usually placed on a pleasant hill. Under the threshing sledge—which people in the East commonly use to separate the grain from the ears—the stalks are transformed into <term>chaff</term> <note>original: "Häcksel"</note>. In warmer regions, where cattle spend the winter outdoors, this remains lying there until the wind blows it away or the rain showers wash it down into the valleys. 546. Since the Nile is mentioned so frequently in the history of Moses, our <term>Sheikh</term> <note>A respectful title for the author, Rumi.</note> is well to be forgiven for confusing it with the Red Sea, although this cannot be excused by the <term>Koran</term>. Compare page 7, verse 132: “And we took vengeance on them (the Egyptians) and drowned them in the sea.” 547. According to Muhammadan legends, <term>Nimrod</term> was a powerful king at the time of Abraham who distinguished himself above all others through his defiance against God and the true religion. The story alluded to here is told by the commentary on our text passage as follows: Nimrod had been so beguiled by his rule over the earth, his power, and outward glory, that he began to pass himself off as a god. One day, in his arrogance, he spoke: “I will measure myself in battle against the God of heaven,” and therefore had his army march out into the field. But when it had encamped there, God sent a swarm of gnats—the weakest of all creatures—against it. These so overwhelmed Nimrod’s warriors that their resistance was turned into flight; some drank the cup of death because of them, and the survivors reached the place from which they had set out only with great difficulty. On that occasion, half a gnat landed on Nimrod’s face; it crawled into his nose without him being able to ward it off, penetrated his brain, and remained in his head for 400 years. As the Sheikh <term>Attār</term> <note>Farid ud-Din Attar (c. 1145–1221), a Persian poet and hagiographer who heavily influenced Rumi.</note> says in his poem <term>The Conference of the Birds</term> <note>original: "Die Vogelsprache"; literally "The Language of the Birds."</note> in the following verses:

Thus it fared for him who took counsel from the enemy, 548
Thus for him who chose the Envious One 549 as a friend,
For Pharaoh, who listened to Haman,
For Nimrod, who associated even with Satan.

If your enemy speaks to you with a sweet voice
Of grains—know that a net is what he means; 550
He speaks of roses, and he means the thorn,
Of bliss and grace, and thinks of fury and wrath.
A sudden misfortune 551 blinds your sight;
Who is friend and who is enemy, you do not know.
If you feel yourself thus blind, then lament and pray,
Killing your body through weeping and fasting,
And say: "O You, to whom my deeds and thoughts are clear,
Let me escape the rock of false choice! 552

He (God) sent half a gnat onto the head of His enemy. In his head He let it remain for four hundred years. — Moreover, I refer to the appendix. Our poet follows another account here, according to which the gnat is said to have pierced Nimrod's skull at the suture. 548. This refers to Haman. This is what the <term>Muslims</term> <note>original: "Muhammedaner"</note> call the <term>Vizier</term> <note>A high-ranking political advisor or minister.</note> of Pharaoh, who constantly advised him to rebel against God and always knew how to thwart his frequently repeated intention to convert to the true faith. Even if he appeared in outward form as Pharaoh's friend, he was in essence his destructive enemy, as he robbed him of the most precious of all goods, <term>salvation</term> <note>original: "Seligkeit"</note>, through his counsel. 549. This refers to <term>Iblis</term> <note>The name of the primary devil in Islam, similar to Satan or Lucifer.</note>, who is preferentially called "the Envious One" because he begrudged Adam his spiritual advantages. According to legend, he took on human form and first persuaded Nimrod that he was a god. 550. The image is taken from a bird that expects enjoyment from the scattered grains and does not know that a net lurks beneath them, robbing him of his freedom. 551. I translate the word <term>qada</term> <note>The OCR reads "kasā," but the context of "God's decree" indicates the Arabic/Persian term <term>qadā</term>, meaning divine destiny or fate.</note> (lit. decree, i.e., God’s decree) this way, which the <term>Muslims</term> <note>original: "Islamiten"</note> use to designate every unfortunate incident sent by God. In this verse lies an allusion to the following saying of <term>Muhammad</term>: "When misfortune comes, the eye is blind; and when destiny arrives, precaution is in vain." 552. That is, the choice of what is destructive.

That is, the senseless chirping that birds otherwise let be heard where they know humans are nearby, in the conviction that they will not be understood. It is therefore assumed here that these animals have another language in which they discuss their affairs in solitude and which they [reveal] to the one who knows it...

And especially a part in which those opponents—wind,
Heat, earth, and tide—are joined together? <gloss>573</gloss>

Delivered from the lion, the hare ran at once
To the wild animals in the forest clearing;
He ran to the field and turned a somersault for joy,

Has found deliverance from the prison of the dust,
It soars joyfully upward to pure love in dance,
Like the full moon in its spotless radiance.

Hear the glad tidings, you lively throngs!
To hell has that hell-hound traveled!
Hear my joyful word: our enemy has succumbed
To the blows of the wrath of God!"

## The Feeding of the Israelites in the Wilderness with Manna and Quails (Rauzat es-safā, p. 270 ff.) <note>The *Rauzat es-safā* or "Garden of Purity" is a major Persian history of the world and the prophets written in the 15th century.</note>

In the desert of Tih <note>The "Desert of the Wanderings," where the Israelites are said to have spent forty years.</note>, the children of Israel fell into great distress and helplessness. When they presented this to Moses, he became very grieved; but a voice rang out (Sura 5, Verse 26):
> “O Moses, be not grieved over the godless people!”

— Meanwhile, the children of Israel had run out of provisions and complained to Moses about their hunger. When he prayed for them, God sent down manna and quails. Thus it is said in the Sura “The Cow” (Sura 2, Verse 54): "And we sent down to you *menn* and *selwa*; <note>original: "menn und selwa" – these are the Arabic terms for manna and quails.</note> however, there is no agreement on the meaning of *menn* and *selwa*. Ibn Abbas <note>A cousin of the Prophet Muhammad and a highly respected early scholar of the Quran.</note> and others are of the opinion that *menn* was a substance which rained down upon the trees and was consumed by the people; however, Wahb ibn Munabbih <note>An early traditionist of South Arabian origin known for his stories of the prophets.</note> and Imam Suddi <note>A famous early Quranic commentator.</note> claim it signifies bread or roasted cakes. Still others say it was a honeydew in the form of thin, round loaves of bread, which rained down from the appearance of the dawn until sunrise — this is the most widespread view; yet 'Ikrima <note>A prominent early scholar and student of Ibn Abbas.</note> thinks it was like grape syrup or a dark-colored fruit juice.

It is told that every morning everyone took a measure of it that was sufficient for them and consumed it. — But when the Jews complained some time later about the taste of this food, Moses prayed again, and God now sent down *selwa*. Different opinions also prevail regarding the meaning of the word *selwa*. The derivation... <note>The text cuts off here as it moves to the next page.</note>

<page-num>210</page-num>

...it has been interpreted that it signifies something which calms the heart concerning the worry for sustenance; for *selwa* <note>original: *selwa* — the Arabic term for quails</note> is said to be derived from *selwet* <note>original: *selwet* — meaning peace of soul or unconcernedness</note>. Ibn Abbas <note>A cousin of the Prophet Muhammad and a foundational figure in Quranic interpretation</note> says, on the contrary, that it signifies quails; a cloud is said to have appeared and let this species of bird rain down, so that the earth was covered with them for a mile around, and they lay one upon another to the depth of a spear’s throw. 

From these, the Jews took sustenance each time for one day and one night, and only on Fridays did they provide for themselves for two days, because this rain did not occur on Saturdays <note>This refers to the Sabbath, during which work and gathering food were forbidden</note>. The South Wind drove the birds together and, after splitting their throats and bellies, cleansed their insides; then they were cooked by the heat of the sun and eaten with manna. But if anyone desired more than he required, the supply he laid up for himself spoiled. 

The Children of Israel, it is told, numbered 600,000 men, who were distributed into twelve tribes of 50,000 men each and stood under twelve leaders. All of these were in distress in the Desert of Tih <note>The "Desert of the Wanderings"</note>; they traveled a distance of one or two parasangs <term>parasang: an ancient Persian unit of distance, roughly 3.5 miles or 5.6 kilometers</term> daily and then arrived back at the place from which they had set out. Quails and manna served them as provisions; but they did not listen to Moses’s advice and gathered a supply for more than a month. Thus it happened that through their wickedness, this blessing was also cut off from them. When the time for eating had come and they were wasting away from hunger, and thirst began to affect their souls, they came to Moses, humbled themselves before him, and begged him for forgiveness. Moses took pity on them and prayed, whereupon God let the withheld gift of grace rain down upon them anew. 

Thus some time had passed, when the Jews once again stepped before Moses and complained about their monotonous food...

...complained. "How," they said, "are we to get by with this manna and these quails? They asked for onions and garlic, lentils, cucumbers, and leeks, as God relates in the Quran (Surah 2, Verse 58 <note>In modern Quranic numbering, this passage appears in Surah 2, Verse 61.</note>). "O Moses," they murmured further, "we can no longer endure the sameness of our food," — and so they renounced the effortless gift of grace in order to demand instead the exertion of sowing and cultivation. These words of theirs pained Moses greatly, for their ingratitude seemed to him an evil sign, and he spoke (Surah 2, Verse 58): "Will you exchange that which is inferior for that which is better? Go down into Egypt; there you will find what you have requested!" And humiliation and wretchedness were imposed upon them, and they drew upon themselves the wrath of God.

## History of the Heavenly Table Sent to the Disciples of Jesus (Tabari III, p. 14)
<note>Reference to the *Tarikh al-Rusul wa al-Muluk* (History of the Prophets and Kings) by the Persian scholar Al-Tabari.</note>

God says in His eternal word (Surah 5, Verse 112): Remember how the Apostles <note>original: *Apostel* — referring here to the *Hawariyun*, the disciples of Jesus in Islamic tradition.</note> said: "O son of Mary, is your Lord able to send down a table to us from heaven?" — This refers to the following: Jesus — peace be upon him! — traveled to and fro upon the earth, and wherever he went, the Apostles and also a great multitude of people went with him. Of the latter, one part was faithful, but the other followed only to see miracles and to satisfy their curiosity. There, the people accompanying Jesus grew hungry, and since no food was to be found, they turned to the Apostles and

The Apostles said this to Jesus, who answered them (Surah 5, Verse 112): "Fear God, if you are believers!" They spoke: "We wish to eat from it and that our hearts may be at rest," that is, to know with certainty that you are a true prophet; as soon as God sends down a table of food from heaven to us, we will become aware of your value and your excellence before God, and the excellence of your followers, and we shall bear witness to it wherever we are. 

Then Jesus raised his hands and prayed (Surah 5, Verse 114): "O God, our Lord! Send down to us a table from heaven," (by table, <term>maide</term> <note>original: *maide* — from the Arabic *al-mā'idah*, meaning a table spread with food.</note>, a dining table is to be understood upon which food is prepared) "which shall be a feast for us, for the first of us and the last of us," (that is, to bring joy to the first of us and the last of us; the words "first and last" are explained by some to mean the people of that time and those living later) "and a sign from You" (that is, a miraculous sign of Your truth), "and provide us with sustenance, for You are the best of providers." (Verse 115) 

God spoke: "I will send it down to you; but whoever among you then still disbelieves, him I will punish with a punishment with which I will not punish any other of the world's inhabitants." Jesus made this known to the Apostles and the people present; all accepted the condition and spoke: "If we become unbelievers, then He may deal with us as He pleases."

The entire people had their eyes directed toward heaven and saw how a table of food descended from the air and settled before Jesus—peace be upon him! <note>original: "Heil über ihn!" — This is the German translation of the Arabic honorific *‘alayhi s-salām*, traditionally used by Muslims after mentioning a prophet.</note> This table was covered with a cloth. Jesus reached out his hand and took the cloth away; then it was seen that, besides twelve loaves of bread according to the number of the Apostles, a large roasted fish, some crushed white salt, and some cress lay on the table. A great multitude of people was gathered with Jesus at that time; they all sat down, ate, and were satisfied; but as often as someone took a morsel of the bread, the fish, or the herbs, that which was taken was immediately there again, and the food became whole once more. Until the sun declined on that day, they ate of the aforementioned foods, and the previous supply was not diminished by a hair. When evening came, the table folded itself up and ascended again toward heaven. On the following day, it came down again in the morning, only to disappear again in the evening. In this manner, the table descended for three days; on the fourth day, however, it did not appear. The God-fearing recognized that it was from the Lord and believed; the hypocrites, however, who were also there, claimed it was sorcery. For since it no longer appeared after the third day, they said that the power of magic also only lasts for three days. The hypocrites who spoke thus went to sleep that night; but when they stood up the following morning, they found their hands and feet and their entire bodies bent and disfigured, for God had transformed them. <note>This refers to a tradition where the skeptics were transformed into swine or apes as punishment for their disbelief after witnessing a clear miracle.</note>

According to another view, the table of food did not come down from heaven, but rather Jesus asked the Apostles one day whether they had anything to eat, whereupon one of them, Simeon, called the Rock, <note>original: "Simeon, der Fels" — referring to Simon Peter, known in Islamic tradition as Sham'un al-Safa.</note> rose and spoke: “O Spirit of God, <note>original: "Geist Gottes" — a translation of the Arabic title *Rūḥ Allāh*, a common Quranic designation for Jesus.</note> see, I have two roasted fish and...”

## Avicenna and the sick youth (Herbelot, *Oriental Library*, p. 812 under the entry for Sina <note>original: "Herbel. Bibl. Or., S. 812 b. u. d. W. Sina." Referring to Barthélemy d'Herbelot's 17th-century encyclopedia of the Orient.</note>)

— When Qabus <term>Qabus: Shams al-Ma'ali Qabus ibn Ma'shgir, a 10th-century ruler of the Ziyarid dynasty</term>, the ruler of Gorgan <note>original: "Dschordschān," a historical region in north-eastern Iran near the Caspian Sea.</note> at that time, heard of the stranger (the physician Avicenna <term>Avicenna: the Latinized name for Ibn Sina (980–1037), one of the most significant physicians and philosophers of the Islamic Golden Age</term>) who practiced the art of healing with such brilliant success, he summoned him to one of his nephews, whom he loved most tenderly and who was then suffering from an illness whose nature no physician in the land had been able to discover. As soon as Avicenna had felt the patient's pulse and examined his urine, he concluded that the illness must be caused by a very intense love, which the patient hid within his breast and did not dare to confess to the King, his uncle. To be further convinced of this, while he studied the pulse, he summoned the gatekeeper of the palace and requested him to name all the parts and rooms of this beautiful house. Upon the mention of one of these, he noticed a greater agitation in the patient, which moved him to then also the per—

...to have the names of the persons called who inhabited this room. When the patient heard the name of one of these persons, his pulse beat in such an extraordinary manner that Avicenna unhesitatingly recognized her as the one whose love had brought him into the dangerous condition in which he found himself. His judgment was therefore: The only means to heal the patient was to unite him with the person he loved.

## The History of the Meeting of Moses with Khidr (Tabari II, p. 12) <term>Tabari: Muhammad ibn Jarir al-Tabari (839–923), a highly influential Persian scholar and historian</term> <note>Khidr, often spelled "Chydhyr" in older German texts, is a figure in Islamic tradition described as a righteous servant of God possessing great wisdom or mystic knowledge.</note>

As the story goes, one day Moses spoke in a sermon about the wisdom of God and said to the Israelites: "Thank God, the Most High, who destroyed Pharaoh and the Egyptians, who made you, the despised, honored and them, the honored, despised; who gave you the Torah and favored you above all nations through the Scripture, the religion, and the prophethood." (Cf. Surah 45, V. 15: "And so we gave the Children of Israel the Scripture, the Law, and the prophethood, gave them good things for nourishment, and favored them above the [other] inhabitants of the world.") <note>This citation refers to the Quran, Surah Al-Jathiya (45:16 in standard numbering).</note>

When Moses—peace be upon him! <note>original: "Heil über ihn!" – a translation of the Arabic phrase *alayhi as-salām*, a mark of respect used after mentioning a prophet.</note>—mentioned these blessings, a man suddenly rose up and said: "O you who spoke with God, is there any servant of the Almighty in this time whose wisdom is greater than yours?" — Now, the Prophet Moses knew no one who reached his level of wisdom and therefore spoke: "As I believe, no one on earth is as wise as I." — In that moment, God sent him a revelation with the following content: "O Moses, on earth there is one of my servants whose wisdom surpasses your own; his name is Khidr."

According to another account, one day Moses prayed to God and said, among other things: "O Lord, who among your servants is perfect?" whereupon a voice rang out, which spoke the words: "The most perfect is he who, however wise he may be, still desires to learn more wisdom." ### Story of the Sacrifice of Ishmael (*The Garden of Purity*, p. 179 ff.)
<note>The "Rauzat es-safa" (The Garden of Purity) is a famous universal history written in Persian by the 15th-century scholar Mirkhwand.</note>

As the story is told, Abraham had made a vow that if God granted him a son, he would offer him up and sacrifice him in His praise. Following this, Isaac and Ishmael were born to him, but Abraham had forgotten his vow. One day, while he was sleeping at the place of sacrifice in Mecca, he saw a man in a dream who said to him: “It is God's command, O Abraham, that you sacrifice your son.” Upon awakening, he reflected and wavered between his innate faithfulness and piety and his inherent gentleness and tenderness. On the second night, he had the same dream again; likewise on the third, where he additionally heard a voice calling out to him: “O Abraham, how could Satan be able to lead you astray in the service of the true God!” — As soon as he woke up,

### About the Properties of the Staff of Moses (*Rauzat us-Safa*, p. 244)
<note>The *Rauzat us-Safa* (The Garden of Purity) is a famous universal history written in Persian by the 15th-century scholar Mirkhwand</note>

One of these peculiarities is that on his travels, Moses loaded the necessary items of clothing onto the staff, which then carried them behind him; another, that it spoke with Moses and provided him with entertaining company; another, that when Moses needed food, he only had to strike the ground with it, whereupon immediately the necessary nourishment for a

## History of the Companions of the Cave (Tabari III, p. 32 ff.)
<note>Tabari was a highly influential Persian scholar and historian whose "History of the Prophets and Kings" is a foundational text in Islamic historiography</note>

The "Companions of the Cave" <note>commonly known in the West as the Seven Sleepers of Ephesus</note> refers to several youths who lived in a city near Syria. The inhabitants of this city were pagans and had as their king a Greek named Dakianos (Decianus), who claimed to be a god and was worshiped as such by his subjects. This story took place before Jesus—peace be upon him!—and at that time there was no prophet on earth. The Companions of the Cave, six youths of noble descent, were guided by God onto the right path and confessed His Unity <note>original: "Einheit" refers to Tawhid, the Islamic concept of the absolute oneness of God</note>, which had been instilled in their hearts through revelation, so that they sincerely belonged to the believers. But when Dakianos received news of their faith, he had them seized and spoke to them: “So many creatures call me their god and worship me; but you, as I hear, serve a...”

<summary>This page details the magical properties of Moses's staff, including its ability to produce fruit and light, before transitioning into the account of the Companions of the Cave (the Seven Sleepers) and their confrontation with the Roman Emperor Decianus.</summary>
<keywords>Staff of Moses, Miracles, Companions of the Cave, Seven Sleepers, Tabari, Dakianos, Decianus, Monotheism, Islamic History</keywords>

...any other. "Who then is this God of yours, whom you serve?" (As it is stated in the Quran [18:13-14]: *And We <note>God is speaking in the first-person plural</note> strengthened their hearts when they stood up; therefore they said: "Our Lord is the Lord of the heavens and the earth; never will we call upon any god besides Him; truly, we would then be uttering an extravagant lie."*) . . . 

The king had a <term>Kadi: traditionally an Islamic judge, used here by the author to describe a high-ranking legal advisor in the King's court</term>, also of the Greek nation, whose son was devoted to the true religion, though he did not openly profess it out of fear. To this Kadi the king said: "What do you say? What should be done with these youths?" 

"They are sons of noble families," the Kadi answered, "and therefore must not be put to death so quickly. Grant them a reprieve for this night; perhaps they will then heed your words and become obedient to you." 

The king granted them the reprieve and exhorted them urgently; but they paid no heed to his words and remained faithful to the Most High God . . . When night fell, they feared that the king might kill them; therefore, all six of them rose and went out of the city . . . 

In the vicinity of the city, however, there was a mountain named Jachlos <note>This likely refers to Mount Anchilos or Pion near Ephesus, where the legend is traditionally set</note>, toward which they walked. Near this mountain, they encountered a shepherd and asked him: "Is there any place in this mountain where we could hide ourselves?" 

"Who are you?" the shepherd asked. 

"We are," they replied, "worshipers of God; we adhere to a different religion than the..."

I also worship the God whom you revere and profess your religion." 

When they saw this miracle, their faithfulness and their sincere devotion to God became even greater, and so they walked until they reached the entrance of the cave. They stepped inside and now beheld the interior of the cave, which was extraordinarily spacious; there they lay down and slept. The dog did the same, stretching his two front legs toward the entrance of the cave and resting his head on his legs. Thus God tells it in the Quran (18:17 <note>In most modern English translations of the Quran, this description appears in Surah Al-Kahf, verse 18</note>): *And their dog lay stretched out with his forelegs at the entrance.*

He sent people in all directions, who searched for them for a full month without being able to find them. They remained in the said cave for 309 years; every week God sent a...

## Abraham Rejects the Worship of the Celestial Bodies
### (The Garden of Purity <note>original: "Rauzat es-safa," a famous 15th-century Persian history written by Mir-Khvand</note>, p. 162)

As the story goes, when Abraham’s mother noticed the signs of his upcoming birth, she went out into the open to a valley whose stream had dried up, so as not to let the matter become public knowledge <note>This was to hide him from King Nimrod, who had ordered the death of male infants due to a prophecy</note>. There she gave birth and, when she saw Abraham’s face, she was overjoyed. Then she wrapped her lovely child in swaddling clothes, hid him in a nearby cave to protect him from the evil eye, and entrusted him to God. Having fulfilled her duties to the best of her ability in this manner, she went home. 

After a few days, she returned to the spot, not daring to hope that she would still find her cave-dweller alive. There she saw that the little one was sucking milk and honey from his fingers and required no mother or wet nurse. Greatly astonished, she attended to some necessary matters for him and then went home again. Thus, months and years passed, with the mother going to the cave whenever she found the opportunity and nursing the child, noticing many wonderful things about him, until Abraham outgrew infancy and became tall. As several historians—

--- Page 251 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Abraham’s childhood from the previous page, where his mother had been visiting him in secret in a cave to hide him from King Nimrod. The text continues to draw from the Persian history "Rauzat es-safa."</meta>

The chroniclers assure us that Abraham remained hidden in that cave until the age of sixteen. Once, toward evening, his mother visited him, and he asked her:

“My loving mother, is there anything else besides the place where I live?” — “My little heart,” she answered, “truly, this dark, narrow cave should not be the shaft that holds a gemstone like you; but what can be done? Out of fear of the wickedness of the enemies, I have hidden you here; for what places, what fields are there not in the world!” 

Then Abraham decided to no longer remain hidden there but to go out; “for,” he said to himself, “it is useless to linger here; staying in the cave brings no gain; but to move brings blessings! <note>The original German contains a rhyming proverb: "sich regen, schafft Segen! Hin und her eile, und Heil wird dir zuteile!"</note> Hasten to and fro, and salvation will be your lot!” After these words, he spoke: “I must necessarily leave this cave and seek out the Creator of the World <term>Weltenschöpfer: a title for God as the Architect of the Universe</term>, so that I may learn what important task is incumbent upon me and carry it out; so that I may know why I have come into the world and make this my constant labor, and so that I may recognize my Lord.” 

As the story goes, Abraham waited until his mother had gone and disappeared from sight. Then he immediately stepped out of the cave and looked toward the heavens, where he beheld the shining evening star <note>Likely the planet Venus</note>. “Could this perhaps be my Lord?” he cried out questioningly. 

As he was still reflecting on this, the evening star turned toward its setting, whereupon he said: “I love not those that set <note>original: "Das sich Niedersenkende liebe ich nicht." This is a famous allusion to the Quran, Surah Al-An'am 6:76: "la uhibbu al-afilin."</note>; for universal sovereignty <term>Allherrschertum: total dominion over all existence</term>, something mutable and changeable is not suitable.” When the moon then rose, he spoke: “Could this be my Lord?” As he meditated thus, the moon also inclined toward its setting; then Abraham rejected it as well, recognizing that nothing which sinks and vanishes is fit for universal sovereignty. Now when the musk-colored <note>In Persian poetic tradition, "musk-colored" often describes the blackness of the night sky or the deep fragrance of the pre-dawn air</note>...

When the veil of night before the face of dawn—before the brow of the true morning—was torn away by the hand of Omnipotence, and the sun, the world’s delight, rose from the borders of the west (returning home), <term>Abraham</term> recognized the superiority of the light-giving celestial body over the other luminous objects more clearly than the day itself, and he said:

> “Is this perhaps my Lord? It is brighter and larger than the others.”

But when the sun also set, he took from this a proof of its temporal origin <note>Abraham concludes that anything which sets or changes cannot be the eternal Creator.</note> and spoke (see note 164):

> “Truly, I turn my face toward Him who created heaven and earth, as a true believer, and I am no idolater.” <note>original: "Götzendiener" — This passage reflects the Quranic account (Surah 6:76-79) of Abraham using logic and observation to discover monotheism.</note>

## Of Hārūt and Mārūt (Rauzat es-safa, p. 124) <note>The *Rauzat es-safa* (The Garden of Purity) is a famous 15th-century Persian universal history by Mirkhwand.</note>

When <term>Idris</term> (<term>Enoch</term>) had been taken up into heaven, the angels, who believed him to be like Adam, spoke among themselves:

> “What is this one doing among us? For he is also like the forefather of his race. Our nature is innocence, but the actions of men are rebellion; how then could they participate in the joyful feast of Divine Proximity?”

Then the All-Wise spoke to the angels and said:

> “If I were to let <term>Satan</term> and fleshly desire come over you, your transgression would be worse than that of men.”

The angels gave the appearance of agreeing with this speech, but in their innermost hearts they wondered at it and said:

> “How is it possible to follow the flesh and <term>Satan</term> while one has the greatness of divine glory before one's eyes?”

Consequently, from the devout of the holy hosts, three angels were chosen, made subject to desire, and so—

<meta>This page continues the narrative from the Persian history *Rauzat es-safa*. Following the angels' claim that they would remain pure if given human form, God decides to test them by sending them to Earth.</meta>

...sent down to Earth, where they were appointed to rule. Their names were **Ghyrā**, **Ghyrājā**, and **Ghyrār**. These three were established to provide a just government among men; they ate and drank, and shared fully in human nature, so that they were subject to commandments, prohibitions, and desire. Each morning they descended to Earth, where they were clothed in human nature, and in the evening they ascended again to Heaven, where they resumed the nature of angels. 

However, one of the angels recognized that the temptation of this state was great and its outcome shameful; fear seized him, so that he purged the lust for power from his heart, removed the royal crown from his head, and humbled himself—glowing and melting, worshipping and pleading—before the throne of the One who bestows grace unasked <note>original: "ungebeten Gnaden Spendenden" — A poetic epithet for God, suggesting that Divine mercy is granted even when not specifically requested.</note>, so that he might be removed from the place of mutability and temptation and be reinstated to his original angelic dignity. The arrow of his prayer did not miss the target of acceptance; thus, he ascended to Heaven to remain in the meadows and gardens of bliss where, because he had looked toward the end and considered the final outcome, he became a partaker of lasting happiness and enjoyed endless delight. 

The other two, however, although they were inhabitants of the heavenly temple, became slaves to the lust for power <term>Herrschsucht</term> <note>Literally "mastery-sickness," an obsessive desire to rule or dominate others.</note>, and thus fell into the same class as the rebellious children of men, completely forgetting their angelic purity. These two princes, unmindful of their destiny, received the surnames **Hārūt** and **Mārūt**. Negligent and foolish, they gradually allowed every rein to slacken as they followed their lusts, until, in the sleep of pleasures, they were separated from their excellence before God as if by a veil. Thus they rested in pride and lust upon the throne of rule and—

"No," said the angels, "you are our only goal!"

At this moment, a stranger entered their company from outside; dismayed, **Suhra** said: "That man has seen us in this state and will henceforth give you a bad name among mankind; to preserve your sovereign honor, you must kill him." **Hārūt** and **Mārūt** acted according to the words of **Suhra** and killed that innocent man. In the end, **Suhra** ascended to heaven through the power of the mighty word she had learned. 

But God—the Glorious, the Exalted—spoke to the gathered hosts <note>The ranks of angels in heaven who had previously criticized humanity's sinfulness.</note>, rebuking them: "O my angels, look upon those whom you chose from among yourselves! Now, refrain from criticizing the children of men again." 

But the angels spoke in shame: "O Lord, You know Your servants best."

# Abraham’s Deliverance from the Fiery Death (The Garden of Purity,
### p. 163 ff. <note>original: "Rauzat es-safa"</note>)

As the story goes, Abraham, in his exalted wisdom, had resolved to find a suitable way to ensure the people saw with their own eyes the absolute helplessness of the idols and their inability to perform even the smallest deed, thereby turning them away from their worship. He waited, therefore, until the festival of the idol-worshippers arrived. These people had the custom, at the approach of a festival, of acquiring fine utensils, costly garments, delicious food, and sweet drinks and bringing them to the idol temple; they would then distribute the food placed before the idols among themselves, keeping it as blessed offerings <note>original: "Segenspenden," likely referring to the concept of *Tabarruk* or divine grace</note> from their god to enjoy until the following festival. 

When a festival came once again, the people went out to the festival grounds and invited Abraham to join them. He, however, pretended to be ill and said: "I am unwell; I have no strength to walk." While staying behind at home, he said (Quran 21, 58): "By God, I will surely plan a scheme against your idols." <note>original: "euren Götzen einen Possen spielen" (to play a trick on your idols). Abraham intends to expose the futility of worshipping inanimate objects.</note> Some among that crowd who had remained behind heard these words. 

When the idol temple was empty and all the servants and guardians had gone to the site of the festivities, Abraham entered the temple. Seeing the food before the idols, he said: "Why do you not eat?" The idols did not answer. Then Abraham spoke further: "Why do you not answer? You are capable of neither consuming food nor giving an answer!" With these words, he took an axe in hand and smashed them all except for the largest idol; he hung the axe around this idol's neck and then left the temple. When the people returned from the place of the festival celebration to the temple

## History of the Masters of the Pit (Tabari III, p. 87) <note>Refers to the "People of the Ditch" mentioned in the Quran, Surah 85, and recorded by the historian al-Tabari.</note>

(Rimon, a Syrian monk, had come to the then-pagan Arab tribe of <term>Najran</term> <note>An ancient city in southwestern Saudi Arabia, historically a center of Christianity in the Arabian Peninsula.</note>, had converted its ruler, Abdallah ibn Thamir, to Christianity, and had taught him a particularly sublime name of God, through whose blessing he was able to perform miracles and specifically to heal the sick.) For this reason, the people held fast to the Christian faith and zealously adhered to the statutes of the Gospel, so that in the entire land of Najran, no one performed their devotions in any way other than the manner of the Christian religion. Indeed, if a non-Christian entered their territory, the people of Najran either forced him to join their faith or they killed him. Now it happened that a prominent man from the Jews of the land

This Jew was named **Yusuf Dhu Nuwas** <note>Also known as Dunuan or Dhu Nuwas, the last Himyarite King of Yemen, who converted to Judaism.</note> and was a man so respected among his fellow believers that no Jew in Yemen withdrew from his commands. According to this version, that aforementioned Jew supposedly turned to this king and told him what had occurred among the Najranites and how they had killed his two sons. The King is said to have then fallen into a rage and sworn by the **Torah** and the Jewish faith that he would cover the Najranites with war, destroy their churches, break their crosses, and either convert all the Christians there to Judaism and let them live, or kill them and burn them with fire. With this intention, he set out from Yemen with 50,000 men. Having arrived in Najran, he dug pits <note>original: "Gruben"</note> in which he kindled fires; then he had the inhabitants of Najran brought together, granted freedom to those who converted to Judaism, and threw those who did not convert into the fire. These are the **Masters of the Pit** <note>original: "Herren der Grube"; refers to the *Ashab al-Ukhdud* mentioned in the Quran.</note>, whom God mentions in the Quran and whom He curses—namely, Dhu Nuwas and the Jews of Yemen. ***Ukhdud*** <note>original: "Uchdūd"</note> (the rarer word used here in the Quranic text) means [a trench or pit].

He ordered that the body be buried in that very spot and that a marker be erected over the grave. The tax collector did as he was commanded.

## History of the Prophet Hud (*Rauzat-us-Safa*, <note>original: "Rauzat es-ſafa" — "The Garden of Purity," a celebrated universal history written in Persian by the 15th-century scholar Mirkhwand.</note>
## p. 143 ff.)

All chroniclers agree that in the twelve-hundred-year period from Noah to Abraham, no prophets appeared except for Hud and Salih—peace be upon them both! However, various views prevail regarding the origin of Hud; some consider him the son of Abdullah, son of Rebah, son of Hareth, son of Abbad, son of Ghauth, son of Irem, son of Shem, son of Noah. Others, however, consider him identical to Aabir (Eber), the son of Salih (Shelah), son of Arpachshad, son of Shem. Whichever the case may be, it was this Hud whom God sent to the people of ‘Ad <note>An ancient Arabian tribe mentioned in the Quran, known for their great strength and stature.</note> as a leader to guide them onto the right path. According to Mu—

<summary>This page describes the discovery of the uncorrupted body of the martyr Abdullah ibn al-Thamir during the Caliphate of Umar and begins a genealogical history of the Prophet Hud.</summary>
<keywords>Umar ibn al-Khattab, Abdullah ibn al-Thamir, Dhu Nuwas, Yemen, Prophet Hud, Rauzat-us-Safa, Noah, People of 'Ad, genealogy, Islamic history</keywords>

--- Page 266 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the history of the Prophet Hud and the tribe of ‘Ad, following the discussion of Hud’s lineage and his mission as one of the few prophets sent between the time of Noah and Abraham.</meta>

According to Muhammad Ibn Ishaq <note>An 8th-century Arab Muslim historian and hagiographer who collected oral traditions that formed the basis of the first biography of the Islamic prophet Muhammad.</note> and other historians and commentators, this people of ‘Ad was an Arabian tribe distinguished by physical strength, tall stature, and giant-like power, so that the larger among them were a hundred cubits high, and the smaller sixty. <note>A cubit (German: "Elle") is an ancient unit of length based on the forearm, roughly 18 to 22 inches. These measurements describe the ‘Ad as giants reaching heights of 90 to 150 feet.</note> Their forefather was ‘Ad, son of Ghauth, son of Irem; they were idolaters and specifically worshipped two idols, which they called Samūd and Samed.

When the deluge <note>The author uses "Sintflut" (Deluge/Flood) metaphorically here to describe the overwhelming nature of their sins.</note> of their transgressions exceeded all bounds of propriety, and they remained stubborn in their outrages and misdeeds, leading the masses astray, permitting what was forbidden, and undertaking that which is displeasing to God, God sent them the Prophet Hud. For fifty years, he preached the eternal truth to them and showed them the straight path. But his efforts were fruitless, for in foolish pride over their strength and size, they relied on their long lifespans and their terrible power, paying no heed to the wrath of the almighty Avenger, who in His grace grants so much power to a handful of dust and a drop of water. They did not remove the cotton of heedlessness from the ear of their understanding, nor did they pull back the veil of human opinion from the spiritual eye that searches for patterns of goodness. They did not listen to Hud’s wholesome advice and did not consider the terrible downfall of those who, before them, had called the prophets liars; on the contrary, they stretched out the hand of hostility to do harm to Hud. 

Thus, of this numerous people, only very few followed the prophet during that long period and accepted the true faith—which they nonetheless kept secret, like their very souls, for fear of the hostilities of the heathens. Among their princes and nobles, only one belonged to this group: Merthed, son of Saad, son of Ghafir; also Lokman the Aadite was...

<summary>This page describes the legendary giants of the tribe of ‘Ad, their idolatry, and their rejection of the Prophet Hud's fifty-year mission, noting that only a few, including the noble Merthed, converted in secret.</summary>
<keywords>Prophet Hud, tribe of ‘Ad, giants, Ibn Ishaq, idolatry, Samūd, Samed, Merthed ibn Saad, Lokman the Aadite</keywords>

--- Page 267 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the history of the Prophet Hud and the tribe of ‘Ad, following the description of the tribe's physical stature and their rejection of Hud’s early preaching.</meta>

<page-num>252</page-num>

...one of those who believed in secret. The more the wandering people defied and exalted themselves, the more urgently <term>Hud</term> <note>Also spelled Hūd; a prophet in Islamic tradition sent to the tribe of ‘Ad.</note> proclaimed his message, and the more zealously and emphatically he practiced the divine law. The <term>Aadites</term> <note>The people of the tribe of ‘Ad.</note> therefore plotted against him to reveal the hostility within them; however, their plans were betrayed to the Prophet by several believers, who implored God for assistance and victory for the pious, and for wrath and vengeance upon the <term>Aadites</term>. The arrow of his prayer did not miss the target of being heard, and the signs of punishment began to appear. First, God denied them rain; their wells and their springs grew dry; the wheel of the celestial sphere, which otherwise sprinkled dew, was transformed into a mill deprived of water; the beauty of the gardens and meadows vanished, and from the seeds they had sown grew only deception and despair. Their insides scorched by the fire of hunger, their heads swarmed by the locusts of misfortune, unable to gain even a morsel of bread or a drink of water, the wretched people spent seven years weighed down by the famine. <term>Hud</term> did not neglect, as the duties of kinship require, to advise and admonish them:

> “Become faithful and righteous; for in this way you turn God’s wrath away from you and make it vanish; truly, good deeds drive away (atone for) the evil ones”

(<term>Koran</term>, 11, 116 <note>In modern numbering, this verse—stating that good deeds remove evil deeds—is usually found in Surah Hud, verse 114.</note>). But they answered him:

> “We do not turn from our god to yours simply because of your word.”

But when the drought and the longing for water and bread had penetrated to their life force, and the knife had reached the bone, they sent a number of their own to the <term>Kaaba</term> <note>The sacred building at the center of Islam's most important mosque in Mecca. Even in the "Age of Ignorance" before Islam, it was a site of pilgrimage and prayer for various Arabian tribes.</note>, so that they might plead for rain. In those days, everyone who found themselves in a difficult situation—whether they were idolaters—

<page-num>253</page-num>

...servant or believer in the Oneness <note>German: *Einheitsbekenner*; referring to monotheists.</note>, or fire-worshiper or denier of God, without any distinction of sect or nation, would journey to the <term>Kaaba</term> <note>The sacred building at the center of Islam's most important mosque in Mecca; here described in its pre-Islamic historical context.</note>, in order to present his petition there and to become a participant in the object of his hopes. To be sure, at that time there was not yet a trace of buildings on the site of the Kaaba; rather, on the spot where the Holy House was later erected, there was only a small hill of red earth. At that time, the grace-filled city of <term>Mecca</term> was inhabited by a community of the descendants of Amlāk—or as others claim, Amlyq, son of Lawudh, son of Shem—who were called the <term>Amalekites</term> <note>A nomadic tribe mentioned in both the Bible and Islamic tradition as early inhabitants of the region.</note>. The prince of Mecca, named <term>Mu‘awiyah ibn Bakr</term>, was of this lineage; however, his mother, Gul-chehr <note>A Persian name meaning "flower-faced."</note>, the daughter of Khaibari, was an <term>Aadite</term>.

— Meanwhile, the rumor spread that the Aadites were being afflicted by famine. The nobles of this tribe gathered together and chose <term>Kail</term>, <term>Luqman</term>, <term>Laqim</term>, <term>Marthad</term>, and <term>Jahmad ibn Khaibari</term> (a relative of the ruler of Mecca), along with seventy other tribesmen, to travel to the Kaaba under the leadership of the aforementioned Kail and to beg and weep for rain. Having arrived in Mecca, they stopped at the home of the city's prince, Mu‘awiyah, who received them with great marks of honor and not only provided all the requirements of a meal in food and drink, with which one prepares a flattering reception for guests, but also commanded two lovely slave girls to delight them with song and the playing of stringed instruments.

Then it happened that, amidst such bliss and joy, the envoys of the Aadites forgot the heavy distress of their people. A month passed in this manner, while they listened to the sound of the morning draught, the noise and the din of confused chatter, and were carelessly intent on entertainment and pleasure.

<page-num>254</page-num>

This pained <term>Mu‘awiyah</term> <note>Mu‘awiyah ibn Bakr, the ruler of Mecca at the time.</note>, for the distress and misfortune of the <term>‘Adites</term> burned his very soul <note>original German: *Eingeweide*, literally "entrails" or "innards," used here to denote deep emotional pain.</note>, and the emissaries had completely put their own people out of their minds. Yet, he could not speak of it to them, lest he be accused of stinginess or a lowly disposition <note>Mu‘awiyah feared that if he told his guests to leave and finish their mission, it would look like he was tired of hosting them.</note>; and since silence also seemed by no means noble to him, he was in doubt and uncertainty as to what he should do. Finally, he devised the following: He composed a poem in which he depicted the distress and the long waiting of the ‘Adites and the negligence of their emissaries; he taught this to the female harpists and had them perform it in the assembly of the emissaries. In this way, he reminded the emissaries of their duty, so that they now undertook to fulfill the purpose of their journey, slaughtered their sacrificial animals, performed the prayers to which they were bound, and implored for rain. 

Then <term>Marthad ibn Sa‘d</term>, the same man who had accepted the faith of <term>Hud</term> <note>The Prophet sent to the people of ‘Ad.</note> but kept it secret, spoke to the emissaries: "As long as you do not believe in the Prophet of the God who is to give you rain, no rain shall be granted to you." He said this to them in verse <note>original: *gebundene Rede*, meaning rhymed or metrical speech.</note>; but from this, they recognized his faith and renounced him. 

After they had thus prayed and implored for rain, three clouds appeared: a red one, a white one, and a black one, from which a voice called out to them: "O <term>Kail</term>, choose one of these clouds!" Kail said:

> "The black cloud yields white water,
> And no color surpasses the black, —

The other two clouds surely contain only little water"; he therefore chose the black cloud because he wanted much rain and did not consider that the greed for "much" brings harm. "I choose the black cloud," he cried out, "for it is the richest in water." But a—

A voice rang out from the unseen: “You have chosen destructive ashes; vengeance will be taken upon your fathers and children, and it shall leave none of you unharmed.” For that cloud, which God sent over the <term>‘Adites</term> <note>The ancient giants of the 'Ad tribe, known for their height and architectural prowess, who were punished for their pride and rejection of the Prophet Hud.</note>, was full of punishing misfortune.

When the emissaries saw the black cloud turning toward the land of the ‘Adites, they rejoiced, believing that their prayers had been answered, and they returned from the sanctuary happy and glad.

When <term>Hud</term> <note>The prophet sent to the people of ‘Ad; he is often identified with the biblical Eber.</note> beheld that black cloud, he recognized it as the harbinger of punishment. Therefore, by God’s will, he distanced himself from the ‘Adites and gathered the believers around him—four thousand in number—in a place which he enclosed with a circular line. This line, drawn with only one of his blessed fingers, served as an insurmountable fortress for the believers, a protective castle; Hud, however, forbade them from leaving this space. Others claim that he traveled with the believers to an island where a refreshing west wind blew.

When the ‘Adites recognized a disaster hanging over them in the violent movement of the air, they left their dwellings and went out into the open. The women and children, along with their possessions, were then brought together in a place which the men surrounded, holding hands and tying their garments tightly to one another; for they said: “Let Hud’s storm blow ever so fiercely, it can do us no harm.” Then, as the story goes, a dry, <term>glowing wind</term> <note>original: *Glutwind*. This refers to the *Sarsar*, the fierce and cold wind mentioned in the Quran as the instrument of the tribe's destruction.</note> first tore the women, children, and livestock of the ‘Adites away from the earth by God's command, drove them scattered through the air, and then violently dashed them onto the ground, where they were shattered. When the ‘Adites saw this, they fled into their houses and sought protection in the firm and massive buildings. But the dry, glowing wind pursued—

<page-num>256</page-num>

[...swept] them away, threw some down headfirst within their very dwellings, dashed others against the doors and walls, and collapsed the roofs and walls of still others over their heads. Some, who trusted in the strength of their bodies, leaped from their houses with such force that they sank into the ground up to their knees; yet even this did not help them: just as one pulls a hair out of butter, the howling wind tore them from the depths of the earth, hurled them into the air, and then dashed them against the ground, where they found their deaths, shattered. In this manner, the dry, glowing wind blew over the <term>‘Adites</term> for eight days and seven nights, destroying them one and all.

### The History of Karun (*Rauzat es-safa*, p. 266 ff.) <note>original: *Rauzat es-safa* (Garden of Purity), a famous universal history written in Persian by the 15th-century scholar Mir-Khwand.</note>

<term>Karun</term> <note>Known in the Bible as Korah, a figure famous for his rebellion against Moses and his legendary wealth.</note>, called Karuj in the Hebrew language <note>original: *Kārūdsch*</note>, was, according to some, the cousin, according to others, the uncle, and according to still others, the sister’s son of Moses. In any case, after Aaron, he was considered the wisest and most learned, the most righteous and God-fearing among the Israelites. He possessed a beautiful appearance and a radiant face, which is why he bore the epithet “The Enlightened One” among the Jews. His constant occupation was the reading of the <term>Torah</term>, and since Moses devoted the greatest care to his education, he had even learned the secret and wonderful sciences, to which <term>alchemy</term> <note>The ancient art of transmuting base metals into gold; in this context, it suggests Karun’s wealth had a supernatural or scientific origin.</note> belongs, from the Prophet. — —

As the story goes, he found so much pleasure in the accumulation of treasures that he devoted his entire life to it. — Accordingly, in a short time, he gathered such possessions that he required forty camels to [carry...]

to carry away the keys to his treasure chests. Already on repeated occasions, Moses had commanded him to give alms; indeed, he had declared himself satisfied with one gold piece out of a thousand. For **Karun**, however, even this was still too much; the insistence of Moses made him angry; he began to argue and did not cease to show himself stubborn and to defy the commands of Moses. He built himself stone fortresses and high palaces, whose walls and portals he decorated and covered with pure gold, and in which he installed golden doors; inside, he set up a throne decorated with gemstones.

When he rode out, a thousand men from among his relatives and kin accompanied him on horseback, as well as three hundred maidens, beautiful as the moon, with fragrant hair, who wore pearl-studded crowns, fine garments, precious belts, and gemstone-studded anklets. When he came home, the tables were set, and he entertained the **Children of Israel**, making a great display of expense. In his reception room, foolish laughter and immoral talk prevailed, and the crowd—blind to the eventual outcome—who looked upon his ruinous glory with the eyes of beggars, spoke, deluded by their own fancies (Quran 28:79): “O, if only we had the same as what has been granted to **Karun**!” 

Even before Moses imposed the payment of alms upon **Karun**, it is said, the latter came to him one day and spoke: “O Moses, to you the fortune of **prophethood** has been granted, and to your brother **Aaron** the worldly rule; I, on the other hand, have no share in these two powers, although I am apparently more suited to them than you. How should I then humble myself and willingly bear the humiliation? If you appoint me as ruler in some way—well and good; if not, I shall, girded with the garment of exertion, rebel and with the

Moses replied: "O Karun <note>original: *Kārūdsch*; a variant or Persianized form of the name Karun/Korah</note>, do not abandon good conduct and keep within your measure! Thank God for the benefits that have become yours; however, sovereignty and **prophethood** are divine gifts of grace which I am not able to bestow. Beware of offering resistance to **Aaron** and his descendants; for such resistance leads to punishment in this and in the next world." When **Karun** heard these words, his wrath grew a thousandfold; yet he concealed his true intent until Moses demanded the alms <note>original: *Almosen*; referring to *zakat*, the mandatory charitable tax</note>. Then he could no longer hold back his envy and hatred, but let them emerge openly. He therefore summoned the lowliest among the **Jews** and, in order to lead them astray from the right path, explained to them that Moses sought, under the pretext of collecting alms, to rob them of their possessions. 

The fools followed **Karun**, who consulted with them on how one might expose Moses to mockery and contempt. At last they agreed upon the following: **Karun** had an immoral woman, who was among the **Israelites**, come to his house, presented her with a golden bowl and with gemstones, so that when Moses preached in the assembly of the **Children of Israel** and exhorted them to do good, she should accuse him before the whole world of debauchery, falsely charge him with forbidden intercourse practiced with her, and bring the reputation of her own vice upon him, so that he might lose his good name among the **Children of Israel**. 

As the story is told, once every week Moses held a sermon for the **Children of Israel**, on which occasion the entire people gathered around him. **Karun** also appeared in his pomp, sat down opposite Moses, and began to mock and laugh at him. Instantly, Gabriel descended and spoke:

> "Moses, lift your face from the earth; God has heard your prayer and made the earth obedient to you, so that it will act according to your command."

Then Moses was glad and spoke to the Jews:

> "As God let me triumph over the Pharaoh, so has He also delivered Karun into my hand. Whoever sides with Karun may stay with him; but whoever sides with me, let him separate himself from him!"

Then everyone, terrified, withdrew from Karun except for two men named Dana and Niran, who had been his accomplices and companions in all his deeds. Then Moses called out to the earth:

> "O earth, take him away!"

## Moses in the House of Pharaoh (Rauzat es-safa, p. 239f.) <note>original: "Rauzat es-safa"—this refers to the *Rawdat al-Safa* (The Garden of Purity), a famous 15th-century Persian history of the world by Mirkhwand.</note>

A daughter of Pharaoh was afflicted with leprosy, and the physicians were unable to heal this disease—from which may God preserve us! Then the soothsayers and astrologers said to him: "This evil will only depart from your daughter through the saliva of a person who shall be found in the Nile during the time of your reign." Pharaoh therefore built a high castle over the Nile and stationed a number of maidservants on the banks of the river to watch for that person, while he himself waited for him in his castle. — Now, when the mother of Moses had set her child out upon the Nile in the little chest, the chest traveled like a treasure blown by a storm before the wind to Pharaoh’s castle; there it became entangled in the thicket and came to a halt. The maids ran there in haste and brought it to Asia <note>Asiya, the wife of Pharaoh, who in Islamic tradition is revered as one of the four greatest women in history and a believer in God.</note>, the consort of Pharaoh. When she opened it, the light of Moses' beauty shone forth in such a way that Asia’s garden-chamber was illuminated by it, and she beheld a lovely little boy who had placed two of his fingers in his mouth and from them

Asia <note>In Islamic tradition, Asiya is typically identified as the wife of Pharaoh, though some accounts refer to her as a daughter.</note> then, as a test, smeared the leprous spots with the saliva of Moses, whereupon the disease was immediately healed. Her love for Moses increased a thousandfold, and she gave him the name Moses, which in Hebrew means "water and thicket," <note>original: "Hebräisch Wasser und Gesträuch." This etymological attempt is based on the Hebrew words *mi* (water) and *etz* (tree/wood). The source of this story likely mistook the Greek *Mōÿsēs* from the Septuagint—the earliest Greek translation of the Old Testament—as the original form of the name.</note> because Moses was found in the water among the thicket. Then the little chest was brought before Pharaoh, and they told him of his daughter's recovery and the miracles of the child, whereupon Pharaoh loved him even more than the others. But when the event became known among the great ones, the astrologers and soothsayers said: "O Pharaoh, the boy whom we announced to you is this one; kill him without delay!" Pharaoh then intended to kill him, but Asia pleaded (Koran 28,8): "(The child is) a cooling of the eyes for me and for you! Do not kill him! Perhaps he may bring us benefit, or we may adopt him as a child!" <note>The phrase "cooling of the eyes" is a literal translation of an Arabic idiom meaning a source of great joy and comfort.</note> That is to say: This child is the delight of your eyes and mine, a bliss and joy for our breast! It is easy to hope that he will bring us benefit or that we will adopt him as a dearly beloved little son. Thereupon Pharaoh gave Moses to Asia.

### Nimrod’s Downfall

...fixed a day for it, and prayed to God to destroy Nimrod with the weakest and most despicable of all animals. This prayer was heard by God. Nimrod therefore gathered his countless, useless army and appeared on the appointed day at the designated place. From head to foot overloaded with golden coats of mail, weapons, and war gear, they deployed their ranks and stood there in power and glory; on the other side stood Abraham alone with his pure soul, facing Nimrod’s army.

"O Abraham," Nimrod cried out, "where then is your army?"

"Soon it will appear," replied Abraham. 

In that same moment, a swarm of gnats <note>German: *Mücken*. In Islamic tradition, this is often described as a plague of mosquitoes or gnats sent to humble the king who claimed divinity.</note>, like the waves of the Black Sea, filled the atmosphere and plunged like a storm of misfortune sent from heaven upon Nimrod’s army, so that those heroes—strong as elephants and greedy as sharks—instantly became like a field grazed bare by locusts and a testament to the saying: *like a devoured field of crops.* <note>original: "gleich einem abgefressenen Saatfelde." This is a common motif in Islamic literature, mirroring the language used in the Quran (Surah Al-Fil) to describe the destruction of an arrogant army.</note>

As the story goes, Nimrod himself fled from the gnat stings into his palace; but one gnat followed him, entered with him, and—while Nimrod lay there and slept—circled his nose for an hour, to see if he might yet repent and convert to the true faith. Then it crawled into his nose and remained there for three days; then it ascended into his brain and began to gnaw at it. Since this was unbearable for Nimrod, he ordered two particularly strong slaves each to take a mallet and strike his head with it day and night. Yet even this granted him not a moment’s rest. Finally, the arms of the slaves became weary and faint, which is why they smashed Nimrod's despicable, shame-crowned head. Immediately his soul descended to hell. It was on the fortieth day that the two slaves were thus delivered from him. But when the...

## Orientation regarding Jalal al-Din Rumi.

WHINFIELD, *Masnavi i ma’navi: The Spiritual Couplets of Maulana Jalalu ’d-din Muhammad Rumi*, second edition, London 1898. <note>The Masnavi is one of the most influential works of Persian literature and Sufi mysticism.</note>

NICHOLSON, *Selected Poems from the Divani Shamsi Tabriz*, Cambridge 1898. <note>The Divan-e Shams-e Tabrizi is a collection of lyric poems named in honor of Rumi’s spiritual mentor.</note>

WILSON, *The Masnavi by Jalalu ’d-din Rumi*, London 1910.

Also noteworthy are the essays on Rumi and Oriental mysticism by **E. G. Browne** in *A Literary History of Persia*, by **Horn** in *History of Persian Literature* <note>original: "Geschichte der persischen Literatur"</note>, and in **Ethé’s** "New Persian Literature" in **Geyer** and **Kuhn’s** *Outline of Iranian Philology* <note>original: "Grundriß der iranischen Philologie"</note>.

Regarding Georg Rosen's literary and scholarly life's work, see the appendix to *Tuti Nameh* <gloss>The Book of the Parrot</gloss>. Leipzig (Insel Publishing) 1912. <note>Georg Rosen was a prominent German orientalist and diplomat; the Tuti Nameh is a famous 14th-century series of Persian stories.</note>

The book design was handled by Paul Renner. <note>Paul Renner (1878–1956) was a renowned German typeface designer and author, best known for creating the Futura typeface.</note>
